# Three phosphatase families form a community: The phosphohydrolases that act upon inositol pyrophosphates.
**Authors:** Rolfes RJ
**Journal:** FEBS Lett (2026)
**DOI:** [10.1002/1873-3468.70394](https://doi.org/10.1002/1873-3468.70394)
**PMC:** [PMC13573052](https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/articles/PMC13573052/)

## Abstract

1. FEBS Lett. 2026 Sep;600(17):2579-2614. doi: 10.1002/1873-3468.70394. Epub 2026
 Jun 25.

Three phosphatase families form a community: The phosphohydrolases that act upon
inositol pyrophosphates.

Rolfes RJ(1).

Author information:
(1)Department of Biology, Georgetown University, Washington, DC, USA.

Inositol pyrophosphates (PP-InsP) are high energy signaling molecules that play
important roles in eukaryotes. They are synthesized from inositol polyphosphates
by kinases that add a diphosphate (β phosphate) at one or more positions on the
myo-inositol ring. The focus of this review is on the phosphatases specific for
the β phosphate. These enzymes are members of three different families of
phosphohydrolases: the histidine acid phosphatase family that is specific for β
phosphate on 1C, the cysteine phosphatase family that is specific for the
diphosphate on 5C, and the Nudix family that is promiscuous both in terms of the
position of the diphosphate on PP-InsPs and recognition of a range of molecules
with phosphoanhydride linkages. These three families of phosphatases share
overlapping PP-InsP substrates, and as a community of enzymes they have a
combined influence on PP-InsP pools, impacting cellular processes and
phenotypes. Each phosphatase family is discussed from biochemical perspectives
(enzyme structure, substrate preferences, kinetic properties), distribution
across eukaryotes (evolutionary, phylogenetic), and the similarities and
differences in the affected cellular processes across fungi, animals, and
plants.

Diphosphoinositol polyphosphates (PP‐InsPs), also known as inositol pyrophosphates, are energy‐rich signaling molecules that play diverse biochemical roles to affect a wide range of cellular processes including energy metabolism, phosphate homeostasis, stress responses, vesicle trafficking, transcription, chromatin structure and more. These processes are affected by PP‐InsPs due to multiple modes of action as phosphate donors, allosteric regulators and structural cofactors (reviewed in [1]). Thus, the metabolism of PP‐InsPs and the regulation of their synthesis by kinases and their degradation by phosphatases are of interest to many.PP‐InsPs are the phosphorylated myo‐inositol ring with a diphosphate (pyrophosphate) on one or more of the carbon positions. The fully phosphorylated myo‐inositol is inositol‐1,2,3,4,5,6‐hexkisphosphate, or more simply denoted as InsP6 (also called phytate). As shown in Fig. 1, the most common PP‐InsP isoforms identified in eukaryotes are 1‐diphosphoinositol‐2,3,4,5,6‐pentakisphosphate (1PP‐InsP5), which will be denoted here as 1‐InsP7 to emphasize 7 total phosphates with the β phosphate on the 1C, and 5‐InsP7 (5‐diphosphoinositol‐1,2,3,4,6‐pentakisphosphate, also known as 5PP‐InsP5). Although not shown in Fig. 1, the 4/6‐InsP7 isomer (in which the β phosphate is either on the enantiomeric 4C or 6C) and 2‐InsP7 have been detected. Isoforms have also been isolated with two diphosphate groups present; the most common of these is 1,5‐bisdiphosphoinositol‐2,3,4,6‐tetrakisphosphate (1,5PP‐InsP4) which will be abbreviated here as InsP8 (shortened from 1,5‐InsP8).Fig. 1Structures of the common inositol pyrophosphates (PP‐InsP). InsP3 is phosphorylated by the inositol polyphosphate multikinase (IPMK, Arg82, Ipk2) or the Ins(1,4,5)P3‐3 kinase (IP3K) to form InsP4 (Ins(1,3,4,5)tetrakisphosphate). IPMK phosphorylates the hydroxyl on 6C to form InsP5 (InsP(1,3,4,5,6)pentakisphosphate). A lipid‐independent pathway in plants and slime molds converts inositol phosphate to InsP5 through the kinase activity of the inositol 1,3,4‐trisphosphate 5/6‐kinase (ITPK1) to generate InsP5 [5]. A kinase of a different family, inositol pentakisphosphate 2‐kinase (IPPK, IP5‐2 K, Ipk1), phosphorylates the hydroxyl on the 2C to generate the fully phosphorylated molecule InsP6. InsP6 is the substrate for the inositol hexakisphosphate (IP6) kinases (Kcs1, IP6K) and the unrelated ITPK1 kinases in plants and the product is 5‐InsP7. The reverse reaction is catalyzed by the cysteine phosphatases Siw14 or PFA‐DSP that are specific for the 5β phosphate or the general Nudix family phosphatase DIPP. The kinase domain of the bifunctional kinase‐phosphatase (PPIP5K, Vip1, Asp1, Vih1) phosphorylates 5‐InsP7 to 1,5‐InsP8 (an alternate pathway that is not shown phosphorylates InsP6 to 1‐InsP7); the phosphatase domain of this same enzyme will dephosphorylate InsP8 to 5‐InsP7. Diphosphoinositol polyphosphates (PP‐InsPs), also known as inositol pyrophosphates, are energy‐rich signaling molecules that play diverse biochemical roles to affect a wide range of cellular processes including energy metabolism, phosphate homeostasis, stress responses, vesicle trafficking, transcription, chromatin structure and more. These processes are affected by PP‐InsPs due to multiple modes of action as phosphate donors, allosteric regulators and structural cofactors (reviewed in [1]). Thus, the metabolism of PP‐InsPs and the regulation of their synthesis by kinases and their degradation by phosphatases are of interest to many.

PP‐InsPs are the phosphorylated myo‐inositol ring with a diphosphate (pyrophosphate) on one or more of the carbon positions. The fully phosphorylated myo‐inositol is inositol‐1,2,3,4,5,6‐hexkisphosphate, or more simply denoted as InsP6 (also called phytate). As shown in Fig. 1, the most common PP‐InsP isoforms identified in eukaryotes are 1‐diphosphoinositol‐2,3,4,5,6‐pentakisphosphate (1PP‐InsP5), which will be denoted here as 1‐InsP7 to emphasize 7 total phosphates with the β phosphate on the 1C, and 5‐InsP7 (5‐diphosphoinositol‐1,2,3,4,6‐pentakisphosphate, also known as 5PP‐InsP5). Although not shown in Fig. 1, the 4/6‐InsP7 isomer (in which the β phosphate is either on the enantiomeric 4C or 6C) and 2‐InsP7 have been detected. Isoforms have also been isolated with two diphosphate groups present; the most common of these is 1,5‐bisdiphosphoinositol‐2,3,4,6‐tetrakisphosphate (1,5PP‐InsP4) which will be abbreviated here as InsP8 (shortened from 1,5‐InsP8).

Structures of the common inositol pyrophosphates (PP‐InsP). The structures of the two most common isoforms of InsP7 with one pyrophosphate at either the 1C (left) or 5C (middle) positions, and the most common isoform of InsP8 with two pyrophosphates at the 1C and 5C positions (right).

The synthesis of the PP‐InsPs begins with the generation of inositol polyphosphates, followed by further phosphorylation; the pathways have been reviewed elsewhere in detail [1, 2, 3, 4], but below is a brief overview.

In plants, animals and fungi, synthesis via the lipid‐dependent pathway begins with the cleavage of the lipid phosphatidylinositol (4,5)‐bisphosphate (PIP2) by phospholipase C (PLC) to form soluble InsP3 (inositol‐1,4,5‐trisphosphate, Ins(1,4,5)P3). InsP3 is phosphorylated by the inositol polyphosphate multikinase (IPMK, Arg82, Ipk2) or the Ins(1,4,5)P3‐3 kinase (IP3K) to form InsP4 (Ins(1,3,4,5)tetrakisphosphate). IPMK phosphorylates the hydroxyl on 6C to form InsP5 (InsP(1,3,4,5,6)pentakisphosphate). A lipid‐independent pathway in plants and slime molds converts inositol phosphate to InsP5 through the kinase activity of the inositol 1,3,4‐trisphosphate 5/6‐kinase (ITPK1) to generate InsP5 [5]. A kinase of a different family, inositol pentakisphosphate 2‐kinase (IPPK, IP5‐2 K, Ipk1), phosphorylates the hydroxyl on the 2C to generate the fully phosphorylated molecule InsP6. (inositol hexakisphosphate or phytate). Formation of PP‐InsPs typically begins with the polyphosphate InsP6 or InsP5.

The PP‐InsP pathway shown in Fig. 2 is based on the linear pathway of InsP8 synthesis through 5‐InsP7 and its degradation through 1‐InsP7 that is typical in S. cerevisiae [6]. InsP6 is the substrate for the inositol hexakisphosphate (IP6) kinases (Kcs1, IP6K) and the unrelated ITPK1 kinases in plants and the product is 5‐InsP7. The reverse reaction is catalyzed by the cysteine phosphatases Siw14 or PFA‐DSP that are specific for the 5β phosphate or the general Nudix family phosphatase DIPP. The kinase domain of the bifunctional kinase‐phosphatase (PPIP5K, Vip1, Asp1, Vih1) phosphorylates 5‐InsP7 to 1,5‐InsP8 (an alternate pathway that is not shown phosphorylates InsP6 to 1‐InsP7); the phosphatase domain of this same enzyme will dephosphorylate InsP8 to 5‐InsP7. InsP8 is the substrate for two other phosphatases, the 5β‐specific phosphatases (Siw14, PFA‐DSP) or the broader specific Nudix phosphatases (Ddp1, DIPP, NUDT).

Synthesis and degradation of PP‐InsPs. The phosphorylated myo‐inositol ring of InsP6 is depicted as a hexagon, with the 2C at the top and carbons numbered. The mono‐ and α phosphates are shown in blue and the β phosphate in red. The InsP6 kinases Kcs1 (fungal), IP6K (animal), and ITPK1 (plant) generate 5‐InsP7. The cysteine phosphatase family, Siw14 (fungal) or PFA‐DSP (plant), are specific for the 5β position on 5‐InsP7 or InsP8 to reverse the reaction to InsP6 or 1‐InsP7. The Nudix phosphatase family members, Ddp1/Aps1 (fungal), DIPP (animal), or NUDT (plant) have the ability to hydrolyze the β phosphate from multiple positions. The bifunctional kinase‐phosphatase is specific for the 1C position, both adding and removing the phosphate; this enzyme is named Vip1/Asp1 (fungal), PPIP5K (animal), and VIH (plant).

It is worth noting that several species—Dictyostelium (slime molds), Marchantia polymorpha (liverworts), Arabidopsis thaliana, and Cryptococcus neoformans—generate an additional InsP7 isoform, 4/6‐InsP7 in which the β phosphate is either on the enantiomeric 4C or 6C position [7, 8, 9, 10]. In Arabidopsis, there are two genes encoding the inositol polyphosphate multikinase (IPMK) protein, AtIPK2α and AtIPK2β [11, 12] and one gene, MpIPMK, in liverworts; recent work in vitro and in planta showed that these enzymes may be responsible for the synthesis of 4/6‐InsP7, although the impact of other kinases cannot be excluded [10]. Another isoform, 2‐InsP7, was found in mouse heart and colon, and in human patient PBMC samples; however, the kinase responsible for the β phosphate addition at the 2C has not been described [13]. Arabidopsis generates two isoforms of InsP8: the canonical 1,5‐InsP8 (above) and a second, uncharacterized InsP8 isoform [7]. The authors speculate that the VIH1 and VIH2 proteins are capable of converting 4/6‐InsP7 to 1,4/6‐InsP8, but this remains to be determined [14]. Because the newly described second InsP8 isoform is uncharacterized, InsP8 in this review will refer to the 1,5‐InsP8 isoform.

The steady state levels of the PP‐InsP isoforms are determined by substrate availability and the relative activities of the kinases and phosphatases, all of which could be regulated. Inhibition of the kinase or stimulation of the phosphatase would decrease levels of a PP‐InsP, whereas the increase in kinase activity or inhibition of phosphatase activity would lead to an increase in levels of the PP‐InsP isoform.

Phosphatases are a broad class of enzymes that are engaged in hydrolysis reactions, and they are found in all kingdoms of life. Enzymes from three superfamilies are pyrophosphohydrolases that act on PP‐InsPs. These enzymes belong to the histidine acid phosphatase, cysteine phosphatase, and Nudix superfamilies (see Table 1 for an overview). While the Nudix family members have many substrates in addition to the PP‐InsP molecules, the cysteine and histidine acid phosphatases are specific for the 5β and 1β phosphate, respectively. Each phosphatase will be discussed in sections below, and for the most part, kinases will not be discussed. Insights from the structures of the enzymes and their biochemistry will be considered, along with evolutionary, genetic, and cell biology perspectives.

Phosphatase families that act upon PP‐InsPs.

Oxidation of cysteine (reversible);

Pseudoenzyme(s) are present in some species

The histidine acid phosphatase superfamilyHistidine acid phosphatases are members of a large and diverse superfamily of proteins that includes phosphohydrolases and mutases. The phosphatases that act upon PP‐InsPs are in Branch 2 of the histidine acid phosphatase family (PF00328; EC2.7.4.24) and are most closely related to prokaryotic and eukaryotic phytases, although importantly they hydrolyze phosphoanhydride bonds instead of phosphomonoester bonds. These enzymes have an active site motif of RH[G/A]xRxP; this motif contains the catalytic histidine, which becomes phosphorylated during catalysis, and two arginines that interact with the negatively charged phosphates [15, 16]. A water molecule hydrolyzes the enzyme‐substrate intermediate to release inorganic phosphate. Importantly, the histidine acid phosphatases specific for PP‐InsPs are found exclusively across eukaryotes as bifunctional enzymes with opposing catalytic functions: the N‐terminal domain is a kinase that specifically phosphorylates the 1C of 5‐InsP7 to generate 1,5‐InsP8 and the C‐terminal domain is the histidine acid phosphatase that specifically cleaves the β phosphate at the 1C position to regenerate 5‐InsP7 [6, 17, 18, 19, 20, 21, 22, 23, 24]. This review will focus on the pyrophosphatase domain, phenotypes associated with this domain, and a recent determination of its structure [25]; readers are directed to other articles that focus on the structures of the isolated kinase domain from humans and yeasts [25, 26, 27, 28].The bifunctional kinase‐phosphatase arose early in the evolution of eukaryotes and confers specificity to PP‐InsP signalingThe evolution and biochemistry of the bifunctional kinase‐phosphatase pair has been discussed in an excellent review and perspective [16], and the key points are summarized here. Randall and colleagues performed a phylogenetic analysis and found no genes that encode a similar bifunctional enzyme in prokaryotes, archaea or viruses. Instead, they describe an ancient origin of the bifunctional enzyme in the earliest eukaryotes, prior to the divergence of the protists, plants, animals, and fungi approximately 2.1 billion years ago [16]. There was a phylum‐wide loss of the ancestral gene in Mycetozoa (slime molds) as no related histidine acid phosphatase gene was discovered in their genomes; instead of synthesizing the canonical InsP8, slime molds produce 5,6‐InsP8 and cells accumulate very high levels of PP‐InsPs (60–180 μm), consistent with altered metabolism. Fungi, bryophytes, and predeuterostome species have one gene encoding the bifunctional enzyme (e.g., S. cerevisiae VIP1, Schizosaccharomyces pombe ASP1, and Marchantia polymorpha MpVIP1/MpVIH), whereas Chlamydomonas, Volvox, Arabidopsis and deuterostomes carry two genes from independent duplications [16, 18, 21, 25, 29, 30, 31]. Bryophytes are an early diverging lineage of land plants, and liverworts are considered to be most similar to the first plants capable of colonizing land [32]. In mammals, these genes are named PPIP5K1 and PPIP5K2 (note—the genes are named for the first enzymatic activity, the kinase that converts 5‐InsP7 (PPIP5) to 1,5‐InsP8) and in Arabidopsis they are known as AtVIH1 (At5g10570, alternately AtVIP2) and AtVIH2 (At3g01310, alternately AtVIP1) [20, 23, 24, 33].Bifunctionality has been retained throughout the evolution of eukaryotes, an extremely rare phenomenon [16]. What reasons might account for a bifunctional enzyme that both makes and degrades InsP8, at the expense of an ATP? One explanation is that bifunctionality allows for increased coordinated control of the levels of InsP8 due to lower stochastic variation in the levels of the two enzymes if they were encoded by two genes [16]. Secondly, the product from the first reaction is released near the active site of the second reaction, effectively increasing the local substrate concentration. Thirdly, the PPIP5K kinase domain operates under zero‐order kinetics; it exhibits concentration robustness, meaning that kinase activity is insensitive to changes in substrate levels with little to no impact on InsP8 levels [34]. This has been shown experimentally with a range of substrate concentrations in vitro [34] or when comparing the high intracellular levels of the substrate generated in vivo by mutants relative to the wild‐type [29, 35, 36]. Concentration robustness effectively frees the 5‐InsP7 molecule to perform other cellular functions (reviewed in [37, 38]). As opposed to the kinase domain, the phosphatase domain exhibits first‐order kinetics, and local substrate concentration increases combined with regulation of the phosphatase will determine substrate and product levels.A structure of the phosphatase domain reveals regulation and substrate deliveryA recent publication reported the first structures of the histidine acid phosphatase domain from the S. cerevisiae Vip1 protein in apo‐ (9GRH, 9GRN) and substrate‐bound (9GRO) forms [25]. The core phosphatase domain is structurally related to histidine acid phosphatases from prokaryotes and eukaryotes that recognize phytate and a range of other substrates. Unexpectedly, a Zn2+ ion was found to be coordinated between the core phosphatase and an α‐helical insertion, indicating a role in stabilizing the tertiary structure. An insertion relative to phytases, the GAF domain, is found specific to the PP‐InsP phosphatases. The GAF domain plays a role in channeling the substrate through a lysine‐rich pore from an outer binding site to the active site. Structures of the catalytically active enzyme and the protein with active site residue substitutions allowed detection of both open and closed conformations, suggesting that the GAF domain acts as a hinge during catalysis (Fig. 3).Fig. 3The GAF domain‐induced substrate channel transports 1,5‐InsP8 to the active site. (A) Electrostatic surface charge distribution of the apo‐ (top) and 1,5‐InsP8‐bound (bottom) phosphatase domain structures (in surface representation). (B) Cutaway front view of the apo‐ and 1,5‐InsP8‐bound structures depicting the outer and inner substrate molecules in the open (top) and closed (bottom) structures. The closed GAF domain forms the substrate channel. Adapted from Raia et al., 2025 [25], http://creativecommons.org/licenses/by/4.0/.These structures allowed the researchers to address two questions that had been raised about this enzyme. The first has to do with the catalytic mechanism and the importance of a proposed second conserved motif (M2), composed of H[I/V/A], in which the histidine was proposed to be a proton donor in acid–base chemistry [16, 18, 20, 21]. Conservation of this motif has been noted and mutation of the aliphatic residue in M2 inactivated the phosphatase activity of the enzyme [21]. Analysis of the enzyme structure determined that these residues are in an α helix that is buried in the core domain, suggesting that protein stability might account for the phenotypes [25]. A second question concerned possible allosteric communications between the kinase and phosphatase domains, as suggested based upon the differences in kinase activity of the human protein between the full‐length protein with active vs. catalytically dead phosphatase domain [34]. Addition of InsP8 and ATP substrates to the full‐length ScVip1 protein demonstrated localized protection within each catalytic domain, indicating no long‐range interactions and suggesting independence of the two enzymatic activities in the fungal enzyme [25]. However, interaction between the two domains may occur in the human PPIP5K2 and S. pombe enzymes [22, 34, 39].Biochemical assays demonstrate that the phosphatase domain cleaves the diphosphate on the 1C of 1,5‐InsP8
 to generate 5‐InsP7

Phosphatase activity against the diphosphate at 1C was demonstrated for SpAsp1, ScVip1, and the two human isoforms PPIP5K1 and PPIP5K2 [17, 22, 34, 40, 41]. In vitro assays showed that the ScVip1 phosphatase domain has an 18‐fold higher catalytic efficiency for InsP8 over 1‐InsP7 [25]. Mutation of the active site residues—R547, H548, or R551 to alanine—reduces hydrolysis of InsP8 to less than 3% of the wild‐type activity, confirming their importance [25]. Mutation of four lysine residues in the outer InsP8 binding site (K558A, K605A, K732A, and K817A) as well as mutations in the GAF domain (E576A, K623A, Q625A, K627A) inactivated enzyme activity, indicating a requirement for the substrate binding sites in the channel. Two previously identified loss‐of‐function mutations in the S. pombe ortholog Asp1, H686Y, and C643Y, map to the corresponding residues in the ScVip1 protein that coordinate the Zn2+ ion (H836 and C793, respectively), supporting the important role for zinc in protein stability [25, 42].Mutations in the phosphatase domain increase the intracellular concentration of 1,5‐InsP8

Given that the enzyme is bifunctional and the phosphatase domain depends on the activity of the N‐terminal kinase domain, deletion of the entire gene or mutations in the kinase domain fail to produce InsP8 and 1‐InsP7, whereas mutations in the phosphatase active site or in the InsP8 binding site or within the channel lead to increased intracellular InsP8. Indeed, no InsP8 is detected in S. cerevisiae, S. pombe, or C. neoformans strains with deletion (Scvip1Δ, Spasp1Δ, Cnasp1Δ) or kinase‐dead (Scvip1‐D487A, Spasp1‐D333A) alleles [6, 17, 43, 44] and increases in the accumulation of the 5‐InsP7 substrate for the enzyme were described for the yeast species [6, 35, 36, 43, 44]. Alternatively, expression of a phosphatase‐dead mutant (Spasp1‐H397A) or overexpression of the full‐length Spasp1+ gene led to a ~2‐fold increase in InsP8 [17, 21]. Similar findings were described in the liverwort M. polymorpha and in Arabidopsis: deletion of the MpVIP1/MpVIH gene in liverwort or a double deletion of the AtVIH1 AtVIH2 genes in Arabidopsis led to a significant decrease in InsP8 and an increase in 5‐InsP7 levels [29, 31, 41, 45]. Overexpression of the AtVih1 kinase domain led to a three‐ to sixfold increase in InsP8 production [46]. Using CRISPR/Cas9 to delete both the PPIP5K1 and PPIP5K2 genes in the human colon cancer cell line HCT116 generated the same impacts on InsP8 and 5‐InsP7 levels [40, 47]. Disruption of the phosphate‐encoding region of the mouse PPIP5K2 gene led to increased cellular levels of InsP8 [39]. Thus, these in vivo results agree with the in vitro biochemical assays and underscore the importance of the bifunctional enzyme to generate InsP8 and modulate 5‐InsP7 pools.Cellular inorganic phosphate regulates the activity of phosphatase domainInorganic phosphate (Pi) is an important cellular macronutrient, as it is incorporated into nucleic acids, lipids, and other molecules, and cells require an abundant supply of it. However, Pi is also the product of ATP‐driven energy reactions (ATP ➔ ADP + Pi), and high concentrations would affect the free energy derived from these reactions [48]. Phosphate homeostasis is regulated by PP‐InsPs across eukaryotes, although the regulators, enzymes, and transporters differ (reviewed in [14, 48, 49, 50]). Regulation of these various proteins occurs by PP‐InsPs binding to SPX domains (named after the yeast genes
S

YG1 and
P

HO81 and the human gene
X

PR1; Pfam ID: PF03105), with InsP8 binding with the highest affinity [21, 36, 51, 52, 53, 54, 55].Cellular levels of InsP8 are determined by the relative activities of the kinase and phosphatase domains, and the zero‐order kinetics of the kinase domain indicate that it would be refractory to fluctuations in the 5‐InsP7 substrate. The kinase domains of SpAsp1, ScVip1, and human PPIP5K2 (but not PPIPK1) are stimulated by Pi and by Mg2+‐ATP [34, 41, 56]. Importantly, enzyme activity from the histidine acid phosphatase is inhibited by Pi at physiological concentrations [16, 25, 34, 41, 56]. Inhibition occurs with the isolated phosphatase domain, indicating direct action by Pi [41]. The exact mechanism for Pi inhibition is not fully understood because crystals with Pi or its analogs would not diffract [25]; however, the authors speculated that Pi binding to the outer substrate binding surface may block association of the 5‐InsP7 substrate with the enzyme [25]. Stimulation of kinase activity by Pi and Mg‐ATP while inhibition of phosphatase activity by Pi allows robust regulation of InsP8 levels in response to cellular energy (ATP:ADP) and cytosolic phosphate pools.Phosphate homeostasis and other phenotypes are associated with perturbations in InsP8

InsP8 is implicated in the regulation of proteins involved in phosphate homeostasis across eukaryotes through the binding to SPX domains [57, 58, 59]. Additionally, InsP8 and 1‐InsP7 have been implicated in other cellular processes, beyond phosphate homeostasis, indicating an important role for the bifunctional enzyme within the cell.FungiThe responses to phosphate limitation in S. cerevisiae and S. pombe and the phenotypes associated with mutations in the enzymes that metabolize PP‐InsPs have been the subjects of excellent reviews [48, 49, 60, 61]; here, this review will emphasize recent advances.Briefly, phosphate homeostasis in S. cerevisiae depends upon Pi uptake by low affinity inorganic phosphate importers (Pho87, Pho90); it is synthesized into polyphosphate (polyP) by the VTC complex and stored in the vacuole or it is secreted into the extracellular space, likely through the Syg1 transporter (review by [48]). Phosphate limitation is sensed by Pho81, which inhibits the Pho80/Pho85 kinase, and allows the transcription factors Pho4/Pho2 to upregulate genes encoding high affinity transporters (PHO84, PHO89), secreted phosphatases (PHO5), and other genes [48]. Phosphate homeostasis in Candida albicans and Cryptococcus neoformans replies on similar proteins [54, 62, 63]. In S. pombe, phosphate homeostasis depends upon Pi transporters (Pho84, Pho841), the vacuolar VTC complex, the Xpr1/Spx2 phosphate exporter, a cell surface acid phosphatase encoded by Pho1, and a glycerophosphate transporter Tgp1. The transcription factor Pho7 induces gene expression under phosphate starvation conditions but it is not under regulation of a cyclin‐dependent kinase; instead, long noncoding RNAs repress expression of the target genes [61]. S. cerevisiae has 10 proteins with SPX domains and S. pombe has 6, all of which are involved in phosphate homeostasis [48, 64]. Deletion of the ScVIP1 gene, necessary for synthesis of 1‐InsP7 and InsP8, has little to no effect on the polyP pools [36, 65].A recent study sheds light on the importance of the yeast vacuole as a buffer for Pi storage and utilization [66]. Under Pi replete conditions, high levels of InsP8 bind to the SPX domains on the VTC complex, stimulating it to synthesize and translocate polyP into the vacuole; additionally, InsP8 binds to the SPX domain on Pho81, the vacuolar phosphate transporter, to inhibit its export activity. Intriguingly, polyP is rapidly broken down by the endopolyphosphatases Ppn1 and Ppn2 to Pi within the vacuole, until high levels (maximum accumulation at 30 mm) inhibit enzyme activity. Thus, the vacuole would contain a mixture of polyP and Pi. When the cytosolic Pi levels fall, the balance of ScVip1 enzymatic activities change from kinase to pyrophosphatase leading to InsP8 degradation; low InsP8 relieves Pho81 inhibition, and Pi is released from the vacuole into the cytosol.This bifunctional enzyme (Asp1/Vip1/Vlp1) from several fungal species—S. pombe, S. cerevisiae, Aspergillus nidulans and Ustilago maydis—affects the cortical actin cytoskeleton, microtubule dynamics and organization, and polarized cell growth [19, 22, 67]; in fact, the first gene identified was discovered as a suppressor of a cold‐sensitive arp3 mutant allele in S. pombe (ASP1 named as arp‐sop‐profilin interactor). Loss of the pyrophosphatase activity (Spasp1‐H397A) led to flocculation and an increase in invasive growth in S. pombe [19]. In addition to the actin and microtubule cytoskeleton, the SpAsp1 enzyme affects chromosome segregation. SpAsp1 generation of PP‐InsPs is required for spindle assembly; the loss of pyrophosphatase activity increases microtubule stability and overexpression decreases stability [21, 68]. SpAsp1 is necessary for chromosome biorientation and for correct chromosome segregation [68]. The ScVIP1 gene shows genetic interactions with ScBUB3, which encodes a kinetochore checkpoint protein [69]. Similarly, phenotypes due to temperature‐sensitive alleles of proteins in the fungal constitutive centromere‐associated network (CCAN: Spmal2‐1, Spfta2‐291, Spmis6‐302) were suppressed by mutations in Scasp1

365‐920
 (pyrophosphatase domain) or enhanced by mutations in Scasp

1‐364
 (kinase domain), which indicates a critical role for InsP8 in chromosome segregation [70]. SpAsp1 is also important in generating PP‐InsPs (InsP8 and/or 1‐InsP7) that are necessary for the nuclear production of polyP and the polyphosphorylation of the fungal nucleolin SpGar2 [71]. It remains to be determined how InsP8 affects these processes—by allosteric regulation, protein pyrophosphorylation or protein polyphosphorylation—but points to significant roles for InsP8 outside of phosphate homeostasis.In S. pombe, PP‐InsPs are agonists of RNA polymerase II 3′‐end processing and transcriptional termination [61, 72]. The effects of the phosphatase‐dead allele asp1‐H397A are mild; however, they are synthetically lethal with a null allele in SpAPS1, a Nudix phosphatase (see below) that also dephosphorylates the β phosphate on the 1C of 1‐InsP7 and InsP8 [72]. Suppression of lethality occurs with mutations in subunits of the cleavage and transcription termination complex CPF and substitution of Thr4 to Ala in the C‐terminal domain (CTD) repeats of RNA polymerase II [42, 72, 73, 74]. Thr4 of the CTD is nonessential in S. cerevisiae but is phosphorylated and a mark for termination [75]. Intriguingly, the Thr4 position is also important in S. cerevisiae for responses to phosphate limitation, but the mechanism may be linked to chromatin structure rather than transcriptional termination [76]. Analysis of the S. pombe double mutant (asp1‐H397A aps1Δ) in the presence of suppressors permitted an analysis of PP‐InsP levels; these mutants accumulated an excess 1‐InsP7 that led to overzealous 3′‐end processing and transcriptional termination [77]. The inability to generate a double mutant in M. polymorpha with the genes MpVIP1 and MpDDP1 (the ortholog of SpAPS1) suggests synthetic lethality [29], although the underlying reasons are unknown. The impacts of PP‐InsPs on 3′‐end processing and termination in other species have not been investigated, but these findings indicate a role for InsP8 or 1‐InsP7 in gene expression.MammalsThe human PPIP5K1 gene is located on chromosome 15, and PPIP5K2 is located on chromosome 5; both genes have over 30 exons, show alternative splicing, and are widely expressed in tissues across the body [78]. PPIP5K1 showed highest expression in skeletal muscle, brain, and heart in adult tissues and higher expression in the duodenum and intestine during fetal development [20, 79, 80]. Expression of PPIP5K1 is induced ~4‐fold by osmotic stress in HEK cells in culture [33]. PPIP5K2 showed expression in the cochlear and vestibular sensory hair cells, in supporting cells, and in spiral ganglion neurons as well as in many other tissues [39, 81]. The PPIP5K1 protein has 1406 amino acids and PPIP5K2 with 1243 amino acids; both proteins have an N‐terminal kinase domain, the histidine acid pyrophosphatase domain, and C‐terminal intrinsically disordered regions [33, 82]. Deletion of both the PPIP5K1 and PPIP5K2 genes in the HCT116 colon cancer cell line led to an increase in cellular ATP levels that may be due to enhanced glycolysis and increased oxidative phosphorylation [47].Disease‐associated mutations have been identified in the phosphatase domain of the PPIP5K2 gene. The R837H substitution variant is associated with an inherited form of deafness [39]. There is reduced pyrophosphatase activity and increased kinase activity, leading to an increase in InsP8 [39]. In a mouse model, deletion of the pyrophosphatase domain led to degeneration of cochlear outer hair cells [39]. The PPIP5K2 gene is linked to familial keratoconus, which is degeneration of the cornea [83]; PPIP5K2 showed fivefold higher expression than PPIP5K1 in corneal cells. The missense mutations S419A and N843S both occur in the pyrophosphatase domain [83, 84]. Increased mitochondrial ATP production and decreased glycolysis were found in primary human corneal fibroblast cells in which the expression of PPIP5K2 was knocked down in experiments using shRNAs, indicating altered energy homeostasis might contribute to keratoconus [85].InsP8, generated by PPIP5K, participates in phosphate homeostasis in animals through the transporter XPR1 (xenotropic and polytropic retrovirus receptor 1). In mammals, phosphate uptake, storage, and utilization are regulated at the organismal and cellular levels; ~85% of phosphate is found as apatite (calcium phosphate) crystals associated with collagen in bone, ~14% is in soft tissue, and ~ 1% is circulating in serum [86]. At the organismal level, regulation of serum phosphate is tightly controlled by parathyroid hormone (PTH), fibroblast growth factor 23 (FGF23), and vitamin D (reviewed in [86, 87]). At the cellular level, phosphate homeostasis depends upon transporter activity for phosphate uptake and efflux [88]. Uptake relies on PiT1 and PiT2 (Na+‐Pi cotransporters) and efflux by XPR1 [89]. XPR1 is an essential gene that is expressed in all cells, and gene variants are associated with the hereditary neurodegenerative disorder primary familial brain calcification [89, 90]. InsP8 binds to the SPX domain of XPR1 to enhance Pi efflux [34, 51, 91, 92]. Several recent structural studies have demonstrated mechanisms for the opening of the channel in response to InsP8 association with the SPX domain [55, 93, 94, 95, 96, 97, 98, 99, 100, 101]. Activity by the bifunctional PPIP5K enzymes to generate InsP8 in response to cytosolic Pi levels regulates export by XPR1.In addition to generating InsP8 to regulate XPR1, mammalian PPIP5K proteins have been shown to interact with other cellular components. PPIP5K1 protein has a conserved C‐terminal intrinsically disordered region (IDR) that interacts with exocyst complex 1 and other proteins involved in vesicular transport [82]. The knock‐down of PPIP5K1 decreases cell motility in wound healing assays [82]. Machkalyan and colleagues noted the intriguing functional similarities of the human PPIP5K1 and S. pombe Asp1 in affecting the actin cytoskeleton, although mechanistic details differ greatly. The PPIP5K1 protein also interacts with phosphatidylinositol‐(4,5)‐bisphosphate on the plasma membrane [102]. Increased synthesis of InsP8 occurred in response to PDGF (platelet‐derived growth factor) and insulin treatment of myoblast cells; the increase in InsP8 was due to inhibition of pyrophosphatase activity by the phosphoinositide [102]. These results suggest there are regulatory mechanisms to generate local concentrations of InsP8 or 1‐InsP7 that may be critical for vesicular trafficking and cell motility.Plants and algaeGenes encoding the bifunctional enzyme are found in green algae (C. reinhardtii CrVIP1, CrVIP2), bryophytes (MpVIP1/MpVIH), and land plants (e.g., Arabidopsis AtVIH1, AtVIH2), and the product InsP8 is associated with phosphate homeostasis and other responses. The emphasis here will be on new findings and readers are encouraged to see recent reviews for more information [14, 103, 104].In plants, the essential macronutrient Pi is often limiting in the environment, leading to stunted growth and lower crop yields. Plants and algae have evolved mechanisms to maximize phosphate acquisition and distribution within the plant (reviewed in [14, 103, 104]). In brief, the phosphate starvation response includes the transcription factors AtPHR1 and AtPHL1 (Arabidopsis), MpPHR1 (M. polymorpha), or CrPSR1 (Chlamydomonas reinhardtii) to respond to phosphate limitation and to induce expression of a network of adaptive target genes. Under phosphate‐replete conditions, activation by these transcription factors (PHR1 as exemplar) is inhibited by an SPX repressor protein. PHR1‐SPX interactions are strengthened by InsP8; high cytosolic Pi promotes kinase activity and inhibits phosphatase activity to increase InsP8 levels. When phosphate becomes limiting, InsP8 levels decline, the affinity of the SPX regulator for the PHR1 regulator is weakened, and PHR1 binds DNA and activates transcription of target genes. These SPX domain proteins respond to inorganic phosphate levels through InsP8. The deletion of both the AtVIH1 and AtVIH2 genes shows lethality and hyperaccumulates Pi whereas the Mpvip1
ge and Mpvih
Tak2 mutants are viable and show no changes to Pi levels [29, 31, 41, 59].SPX proteins regulate other responses. Algae and plants have a large number of SPX proteins (5 in Chlamydomonas, 20 in Arabidopsis, 15 in rice); some are solo SPX domain and others have an N‐terminal SPX domains associated with other moieties (SPX‐EXS transporter, SPX‐RING, SPX‐MFS) [51, 58, 59, 105]. In rice and Arabidopsis, SPX domain proteins regulate nitrate signaling in addition to phosphate, to coordinate these two critical metabolites [106, 107, 108]. In Chlamydomonas, CrVip1 is important for carbon metabolism in addition to phosphate [30].Lines of M. polymorpha were generated lacking the phosphatase domain while retaining the kinase domain (Mpvip1Δdp
ge) and others lacking the entire gene (Mpvip1
ge, Mpvih
Tak2) [29, 31]. As noted above, Laurent and colleagues could not recover a double mutant lacking both the phosphatase domain of MpVIP1 combined with a deletion of MpNUDT1, suggesting synthetic lethality, but they were able to obtain the Mpvip1Δdp
ge
Mppfa‐dsp1

ge
 double mutant (see below for discussion on the other two phosphatases). The Mpvip1Δdp
ge mutant had reduced thallus surface area, which was reduced further in the Mpvip1Δdp
ge
Mppfa‐dsp1

ge
 double mutant. The double mutant also had an increase in rhizoid mass, did not develop gemmae cups (structures that carry clones of the parent plant that are dispersed by rain drops), accumulated higher nitrate levels, and accumulated lower Pi than the parental plants [29]. InsP8 produced by MpVIH inhibits DELLA proteins by promoting their degradation through the proteasome to increase growth and thallus development [31]. These findings indicate important roles for the pyrophosphatases in regulating PP‐InsP levels that affect important cellular functions in metabolism, growth, and development.There is a twofold increase in InsP8 levels upon treatment of Arabidopsis seedlings with methyl jasmonate, and this increase was dependent on AtVih2 [24]. The InsP8 molecule is a cofactor in the COI1‐JAZ receptor [24]. This was important for plant defenses as moth and butterfly larvae feeding on plants with mutations in AtVih2 gained significantly more weight and the plants showed increased leaf damage [24, 46], indicating a role in defenses against herbivory. Given the Pi regulation of the AtVih2 protein, there is a link of defense responses to nutrient status. Overexpression of the AtVih2 kinase domain leads to the jasmonate responses; it remains to be determined if the regulation to increase InsP8 occurs by increased kinase domain activity or inhibition of the phosphatase domain.Summary of the histidine acid phosphatase superfamily and unanswered questionsThe histidine acid phosphatase superfamily is present in essentially all eukaryotes and is exclusively found as a bifunctional enzyme with an N‐terminal kinase domain and a C‐terminal phosphatase domain that act upon the same phosphate: The diphosphate at the 1C position of 1,5‐InsP8 and 1‐InsP7. Catalytic activity of the phosphatase domain is regulated by inorganic phosphate, likely by blocking access of the substrate to the active site. The conserved mechanism across eukaryotes is that InsP8 regulates phosphate homeostasis through allostery, by binding to SPX domains, although the particular proteins are species specific. Importantly, InsP8 and 1‐InsP7 are implicated in other cellular processes including polarized cell growth, vesicular trafficking, the actin and microtubule cytoskeleton, chromosome segregation, 3′‐end processing and transcription termination, and as a cofactor in receptors. Open questions that need addressing concern a deeper understanding of the molecular mechanisms for these processes, if there is regulated production or degradation of InsP8 or 1‐InsP7 to affect these processes, and which of these affected processes are generally affected across eukaryotes and which ones are clade or species specific.

The histidine acid phosphatase superfamily

Histidine acid phosphatases are members of a large and diverse superfamily of proteins that includes phosphohydrolases and mutases. The phosphatases that act upon PP‐InsPs are in Branch 2 of the histidine acid phosphatase family (PF00328; EC2.7.4.24) and are most closely related to prokaryotic and eukaryotic phytases, although importantly they hydrolyze phosphoanhydride bonds instead of phosphomonoester bonds. These enzymes have an active site motif of RH[G/A]xRxP; this motif contains the catalytic histidine, which becomes phosphorylated during catalysis, and two arginines that interact with the negatively charged phosphates [15, 16]. A water molecule hydrolyzes the enzyme‐substrate intermediate to release inorganic phosphate. Importantly, the histidine acid phosphatases specific for PP‐InsPs are found exclusively across eukaryotes as bifunctional enzymes with opposing catalytic functions: the N‐terminal domain is a kinase that specifically phosphorylates the 1C of 5‐InsP7 to generate 1,5‐InsP8 and the C‐terminal domain is the histidine acid phosphatase that specifically cleaves the β phosphate at the 1C position to regenerate 5‐InsP7 [6, 17, 18, 19, 20, 21, 22, 23, 24]. This review will focus on the pyrophosphatase domain, phenotypes associated with this domain, and a recent determination of its structure [25]; readers are directed to other articles that focus on the structures of the isolated kinase domain from humans and yeasts [25, 26, 27, 28].

The bifunctional kinase‐phosphatase arose early in the evolution of eukaryotes and confers specificity to PP‐InsP signalingThe evolution and biochemistry of the bifunctional kinase‐phosphatase pair has been discussed in an excellent review and perspective [16], and the key points are summarized here. Randall and colleagues performed a phylogenetic analysis and found no genes that encode a similar bifunctional enzyme in prokaryotes, archaea or viruses. Instead, they describe an ancient origin of the bifunctional enzyme in the earliest eukaryotes, prior to the divergence of the protists, plants, animals, and fungi approximately 2.1 billion years ago [16]. There was a phylum‐wide loss of the ancestral gene in Mycetozoa (slime molds) as no related histidine acid phosphatase gene was discovered in their genomes; instead of synthesizing the canonical InsP8, slime molds produce 5,6‐InsP8 and cells accumulate very high levels of PP‐InsPs (60–180 μm), consistent with altered metabolism. Fungi, bryophytes, and predeuterostome species have one gene encoding the bifunctional enzyme (e.g., S. cerevisiae VIP1, Schizosaccharomyces pombe ASP1, and Marchantia polymorpha MpVIP1/MpVIH), whereas Chlamydomonas, Volvox, Arabidopsis and deuterostomes carry two genes from independent duplications [16, 18, 21, 25, 29, 30, 31]. Bryophytes are an early diverging lineage of land plants, and liverworts are considered to be most similar to the first plants capable of colonizing land [32]. In mammals, these genes are named PPIP5K1 and PPIP5K2 (note—the genes are named for the first enzymatic activity, the kinase that converts 5‐InsP7 (PPIP5) to 1,5‐InsP8) and in Arabidopsis they are known as AtVIH1 (At5g10570, alternately AtVIP2) and AtVIH2 (At3g01310, alternately AtVIP1) [20, 23, 24, 33].Bifunctionality has been retained throughout the evolution of eukaryotes, an extremely rare phenomenon [16]. What reasons might account for a bifunctional enzyme that both makes and degrades InsP8, at the expense of an ATP? One explanation is that bifunctionality allows for increased coordinated control of the levels of InsP8 due to lower stochastic variation in the levels of the two enzymes if they were encoded by two genes [16]. Secondly, the product from the first reaction is released near the active site of the second reaction, effectively increasing the local substrate concentration. Thirdly, the PPIP5K kinase domain operates under zero‐order kinetics; it exhibits concentration robustness, meaning that kinase activity is insensitive to changes in substrate levels with little to no impact on InsP8 levels [34]. This has been shown experimentally with a range of substrate concentrations in vitro [34] or when comparing the high intracellular levels of the substrate generated in vivo by mutants relative to the wild‐type [29, 35, 36]. Concentration robustness effectively frees the 5‐InsP7 molecule to perform other cellular functions (reviewed in [37, 38]). As opposed to the kinase domain, the phosphatase domain exhibits first‐order kinetics, and local substrate concentration increases combined with regulation of the phosphatase will determine substrate and product levels.

The bifunctional kinase‐phosphatase arose early in the evolution of eukaryotes and confers specificity to PP‐InsP signaling

The evolution and biochemistry of the bifunctional kinase‐phosphatase pair has been discussed in an excellent review and perspective [16], and the key points are summarized here. Randall and colleagues performed a phylogenetic analysis and found no genes that encode a similar bifunctional enzyme in prokaryotes, archaea or viruses. Instead, they describe an ancient origin of the bifunctional enzyme in the earliest eukaryotes, prior to the divergence of the protists, plants, animals, and fungi approximately 2.1 billion years ago [16]. There was a phylum‐wide loss of the ancestral gene in Mycetozoa (slime molds) as no related histidine acid phosphatase gene was discovered in their genomes; instead of synthesizing the canonical InsP8, slime molds produce 5,6‐InsP8 and cells accumulate very high levels of PP‐InsPs (60–180 μm), consistent with altered metabolism. Fungi, bryophytes, and predeuterostome species have one gene encoding the bifunctional enzyme (e.g., S. cerevisiae VIP1, Schizosaccharomyces pombe ASP1, and Marchantia polymorpha MpVIP1/MpVIH), whereas Chlamydomonas, Volvox, Arabidopsis and deuterostomes carry two genes from independent duplications [16, 18, 21, 25, 29, 30, 31]. Bryophytes are an early diverging lineage of land plants, and liverworts are considered to be most similar to the first plants capable of colonizing land [32]. In mammals, these genes are named PPIP5K1 and PPIP5K2 (note—the genes are named for the first enzymatic activity, the kinase that converts 5‐InsP7 (PPIP5) to 1,5‐InsP8) and in Arabidopsis they are known as AtVIH1 (At5g10570, alternately AtVIP2) and AtVIH2 (At3g01310, alternately AtVIP1) [20, 23, 24, 33].

Bifunctionality has been retained throughout the evolution of eukaryotes, an extremely rare phenomenon [16]. What reasons might account for a bifunctional enzyme that both makes and degrades InsP8, at the expense of an ATP? One explanation is that bifunctionality allows for increased coordinated control of the levels of InsP8 due to lower stochastic variation in the levels of the two enzymes if they were encoded by two genes [16]. Secondly, the product from the first reaction is released near the active site of the second reaction, effectively increasing the local substrate concentration. Thirdly, the PPIP5K kinase domain operates under zero‐order kinetics; it exhibits concentration robustness, meaning that kinase activity is insensitive to changes in substrate levels with little to no impact on InsP8 levels [34]. This has been shown experimentally with a range of substrate concentrations in vitro [34] or when comparing the high intracellular levels of the substrate generated in vivo by mutants relative to the wild‐type [29, 35, 36]. Concentration robustness effectively frees the 5‐InsP7 molecule to perform other cellular functions (reviewed in [37, 38]). As opposed to the kinase domain, the phosphatase domain exhibits first‐order kinetics, and local substrate concentration increases combined with regulation of the phosphatase will determine substrate and product levels.

A structure of the phosphatase domain reveals regulation and substrate deliveryA recent publication reported the first structures of the histidine acid phosphatase domain from the S. cerevisiae Vip1 protein in apo‐ (9GRH, 9GRN) and substrate‐bound (9GRO) forms [25]. The core phosphatase domain is structurally related to histidine acid phosphatases from prokaryotes and eukaryotes that recognize phytate and a range of other substrates. Unexpectedly, a Zn2+ ion was found to be coordinated between the core phosphatase and an α‐helical insertion, indicating a role in stabilizing the tertiary structure. An insertion relative to phytases, the GAF domain, is found specific to the PP‐InsP phosphatases. The GAF domain plays a role in channeling the substrate through a lysine‐rich pore from an outer binding site to the active site. Structures of the catalytically active enzyme and the protein with active site residue substitutions allowed detection of both open and closed conformations, suggesting that the GAF domain acts as a hinge during catalysis (Fig. 3).Fig. 3The GAF domain‐induced substrate channel transports 1,5‐InsP8 to the active site. (A) Electrostatic surface charge distribution of the apo‐ (top) and 1,5‐InsP8‐bound (bottom) phosphatase domain structures (in surface representation). (B) Cutaway front view of the apo‐ and 1,5‐InsP8‐bound structures depicting the outer and inner substrate molecules in the open (top) and closed (bottom) structures. The closed GAF domain forms the substrate channel. Adapted from Raia et al., 2025 [25], http://creativecommons.org/licenses/by/4.0/.These structures allowed the researchers to address two questions that had been raised about this enzyme. The first has to do with the catalytic mechanism and the importance of a proposed second conserved motif (M2), composed of H[I/V/A], in which the histidine was proposed to be a proton donor in acid–base chemistry [16, 18, 20, 21]. Conservation of this motif has been noted and mutation of the aliphatic residue in M2 inactivated the phosphatase activity of the enzyme [21]. Analysis of the enzyme structure determined that these residues are in an α helix that is buried in the core domain, suggesting that protein stability might account for the phenotypes [25]. A second question concerned possible allosteric communications between the kinase and phosphatase domains, as suggested based upon the differences in kinase activity of the human protein between the full‐length protein with active vs. catalytically dead phosphatase domain [34]. Addition of InsP8 and ATP substrates to the full‐length ScVip1 protein demonstrated localized protection within each catalytic domain, indicating no long‐range interactions and suggesting independence of the two enzymatic activities in the fungal enzyme [25]. However, interaction between the two domains may occur in the human PPIP5K2 and S. pombe enzymes [22, 34, 39].

A structure of the phosphatase domain reveals regulation and substrate delivery

A recent publication reported the first structures of the histidine acid phosphatase domain from the S. cerevisiae Vip1 protein in apo‐ (9GRH, 9GRN) and substrate‐bound (9GRO) forms [25]. The core phosphatase domain is structurally related to histidine acid phosphatases from prokaryotes and eukaryotes that recognize phytate and a range of other substrates. Unexpectedly, a Zn2+ ion was found to be coordinated between the core phosphatase and an α‐helical insertion, indicating a role in stabilizing the tertiary structure. An insertion relative to phytases, the GAF domain, is found specific to the PP‐InsP phosphatases. The GAF domain plays a role in channeling the substrate through a lysine‐rich pore from an outer binding site to the active site. Structures of the catalytically active enzyme and the protein with active site residue substitutions allowed detection of both open and closed conformations, suggesting that the GAF domain acts as a hinge during catalysis (Fig. 3).

The GAF domain‐induced substrate channel transports 1,5‐InsP8 to the active site. (A) Electrostatic surface charge distribution of the apo‐ (top) and 1,5‐InsP8‐bound (bottom) phosphatase domain structures (in surface representation). (B) Cutaway front view of the apo‐ and 1,5‐InsP8‐bound structures depicting the outer and inner substrate molecules in the open (top) and closed (bottom) structures. The closed GAF domain forms the substrate channel. Adapted from Raia et al., 2025 [25], http://creativecommons.org/licenses/by/4.0/.

These structures allowed the researchers to address two questions that had been raised about this enzyme. The first has to do with the catalytic mechanism and the importance of a proposed second conserved motif (M2), composed of H[I/V/A], in which the histidine was proposed to be a proton donor in acid–base chemistry [16, 18, 20, 21]. Conservation of this motif has been noted and mutation of the aliphatic residue in M2 inactivated the phosphatase activity of the enzyme [21]. Analysis of the enzyme structure determined that these residues are in an α helix that is buried in the core domain, suggesting that protein stability might account for the phenotypes [25]. A second question concerned possible allosteric communications between the kinase and phosphatase domains, as suggested based upon the differences in kinase activity of the human protein between the full‐length protein with active vs. catalytically dead phosphatase domain [34]. Addition of InsP8 and ATP substrates to the full‐length ScVip1 protein demonstrated localized protection within each catalytic domain, indicating no long‐range interactions and suggesting independence of the two enzymatic activities in the fungal enzyme [25]. However, interaction between the two domains may occur in the human PPIP5K2 and S. pombe enzymes [22, 34, 39].

Biochemical assays demonstrate that the phosphatase domain cleaves the diphosphate on the 1C of 1,5‐InsP8
 to generate 5‐InsP7

Phosphatase activity against the diphosphate at 1C was demonstrated for SpAsp1, ScVip1, and the two human isoforms PPIP5K1 and PPIP5K2 [17, 22, 34, 40, 41]. In vitro assays showed that the ScVip1 phosphatase domain has an 18‐fold higher catalytic efficiency for InsP8 over 1‐InsP7 [25]. Mutation of the active site residues—R547, H548, or R551 to alanine—reduces hydrolysis of InsP8 to less than 3% of the wild‐type activity, confirming their importance [25]. Mutation of four lysine residues in the outer InsP8 binding site (K558A, K605A, K732A, and K817A) as well as mutations in the GAF domain (E576A, K623A, Q625A, K627A) inactivated enzyme activity, indicating a requirement for the substrate binding sites in the channel. Two previously identified loss‐of‐function mutations in the S. pombe ortholog Asp1, H686Y, and C643Y, map to the corresponding residues in the ScVip1 protein that coordinate the Zn2+ ion (H836 and C793, respectively), supporting the important role for zinc in protein stability [25, 42].

Biochemical assays demonstrate that the phosphatase domain cleaves the diphosphate on the 1C of 1,5‐InsP8
 to generate 5‐InsP7

Phosphatase activity against the diphosphate at 1C was demonstrated for SpAsp1, ScVip1, and the two human isoforms PPIP5K1 and PPIP5K2 [17, 22, 34, 40, 41]. In vitro assays showed that the ScVip1 phosphatase domain has an 18‐fold higher catalytic efficiency for InsP8 over 1‐InsP7 [25]. Mutation of the active site residues—R547, H548, or R551 to alanine—reduces hydrolysis of InsP8 to less than 3% of the wild‐type activity, confirming their importance [25]. Mutation of four lysine residues in the outer InsP8 binding site (K558A, K605A, K732A, and K817A) as well as mutations in the GAF domain (E576A, K623A, Q625A, K627A) inactivated enzyme activity, indicating a requirement for the substrate binding sites in the channel. Two previously identified loss‐of‐function mutations in the S. pombe ortholog Asp1, H686Y, and C643Y, map to the corresponding residues in the ScVip1 protein that coordinate the Zn2+ ion (H836 and C793, respectively), supporting the important role for zinc in protein stability [25, 42].

Mutations in the phosphatase domain increase the intracellular concentration of 1,5‐InsP8

Given that the enzyme is bifunctional and the phosphatase domain depends on the activity of the N‐terminal kinase domain, deletion of the entire gene or mutations in the kinase domain fail to produce InsP8 and 1‐InsP7, whereas mutations in the phosphatase active site or in the InsP8 binding site or within the channel lead to increased intracellular InsP8. Indeed, no InsP8 is detected in S. cerevisiae, S. pombe, or C. neoformans strains with deletion (Scvip1Δ, Spasp1Δ, Cnasp1Δ) or kinase‐dead (Scvip1‐D487A, Spasp1‐D333A) alleles [6, 17, 43, 44] and increases in the accumulation of the 5‐InsP7 substrate for the enzyme were described for the yeast species [6, 35, 36, 43, 44]. Alternatively, expression of a phosphatase‐dead mutant (Spasp1‐H397A) or overexpression of the full‐length Spasp1+ gene led to a ~2‐fold increase in InsP8 [17, 21]. Similar findings were described in the liverwort M. polymorpha and in Arabidopsis: deletion of the MpVIP1/MpVIH gene in liverwort or a double deletion of the AtVIH1 AtVIH2 genes in Arabidopsis led to a significant decrease in InsP8 and an increase in 5‐InsP7 levels [29, 31, 41, 45]. Overexpression of the AtVih1 kinase domain led to a three‐ to sixfold increase in InsP8 production [46]. Using CRISPR/Cas9 to delete both the PPIP5K1 and PPIP5K2 genes in the human colon cancer cell line HCT116 generated the same impacts on InsP8 and 5‐InsP7 levels [40, 47]. Disruption of the phosphate‐encoding region of the mouse PPIP5K2 gene led to increased cellular levels of InsP8 [39]. Thus, these in vivo results agree with the in vitro biochemical assays and underscore the importance of the bifunctional enzyme to generate InsP8 and modulate 5‐InsP7 pools.

Mutations in the phosphatase domain increase the intracellular concentration of 1,5‐InsP8

Given that the enzyme is bifunctional and the phosphatase domain depends on the activity of the N‐terminal kinase domain, deletion of the entire gene or mutations in the kinase domain fail to produce InsP8 and 1‐InsP7, whereas mutations in the phosphatase active site or in the InsP8 binding site or within the channel lead to increased intracellular InsP8. Indeed, no InsP8 is detected in S. cerevisiae, S. pombe, or C. neoformans strains with deletion (Scvip1Δ, Spasp1Δ, Cnasp1Δ) or kinase‐dead (Scvip1‐D487A, Spasp1‐D333A) alleles [6, 17, 43, 44] and increases in the accumulation of the 5‐InsP7 substrate for the enzyme were described for the yeast species [6, 35, 36, 43, 44]. Alternatively, expression of a phosphatase‐dead mutant (Spasp1‐H397A) or overexpression of the full‐length Spasp1+ gene led to a ~2‐fold increase in InsP8 [17, 21]. Similar findings were described in the liverwort M. polymorpha and in Arabidopsis: deletion of the MpVIP1/MpVIH gene in liverwort or a double deletion of the AtVIH1 AtVIH2 genes in Arabidopsis led to a significant decrease in InsP8 and an increase in 5‐InsP7 levels [29, 31, 41, 45]. Overexpression of the AtVih1 kinase domain led to a three‐ to sixfold increase in InsP8 production [46]. Using CRISPR/Cas9 to delete both the PPIP5K1 and PPIP5K2 genes in the human colon cancer cell line HCT116 generated the same impacts on InsP8 and 5‐InsP7 levels [40, 47]. Disruption of the phosphate‐encoding region of the mouse PPIP5K2 gene led to increased cellular levels of InsP8 [39]. Thus, these in vivo results agree with the in vitro biochemical assays and underscore the importance of the bifunctional enzyme to generate InsP8 and modulate 5‐InsP7 pools.

Cellular inorganic phosphate regulates the activity of phosphatase domainInorganic phosphate (Pi) is an important cellular macronutrient, as it is incorporated into nucleic acids, lipids, and other molecules, and cells require an abundant supply of it. However, Pi is also the product of ATP‐driven energy reactions (ATP ➔ ADP + Pi), and high concentrations would affect the free energy derived from these reactions [48]. Phosphate homeostasis is regulated by PP‐InsPs across eukaryotes, although the regulators, enzymes, and transporters differ (reviewed in [14, 48, 49, 50]). Regulation of these various proteins occurs by PP‐InsPs binding to SPX domains (named after the yeast genes
S

YG1 and
P

HO81 and the human gene
X

PR1; Pfam ID: PF03105), with InsP8 binding with the highest affinity [21, 36, 51, 52, 53, 54, 55].Cellular levels of InsP8 are determined by the relative activities of the kinase and phosphatase domains, and the zero‐order kinetics of the kinase domain indicate that it would be refractory to fluctuations in the 5‐InsP7 substrate. The kinase domains of SpAsp1, ScVip1, and human PPIP5K2 (but not PPIPK1) are stimulated by Pi and by Mg2+‐ATP [34, 41, 56]. Importantly, enzyme activity from the histidine acid phosphatase is inhibited by Pi at physiological concentrations [16, 25, 34, 41, 56]. Inhibition occurs with the isolated phosphatase domain, indicating direct action by Pi [41]. The exact mechanism for Pi inhibition is not fully understood because crystals with Pi or its analogs would not diffract [25]; however, the authors speculated that Pi binding to the outer substrate binding surface may block association of the 5‐InsP7 substrate with the enzyme [25]. Stimulation of kinase activity by Pi and Mg‐ATP while inhibition of phosphatase activity by Pi allows robust regulation of InsP8 levels in response to cellular energy (ATP:ADP) and cytosolic phosphate pools.

Cellular inorganic phosphate regulates the activity of phosphatase domain

Inorganic phosphate (Pi) is an important cellular macronutrient, as it is incorporated into nucleic acids, lipids, and other molecules, and cells require an abundant supply of it. However, Pi is also the product of ATP‐driven energy reactions (ATP ➔ ADP + Pi), and high concentrations would affect the free energy derived from these reactions [48]. Phosphate homeostasis is regulated by PP‐InsPs across eukaryotes, although the regulators, enzymes, and transporters differ (reviewed in [14, 48, 49, 50]). Regulation of these various proteins occurs by PP‐InsPs binding to SPX domains (named after the yeast genes
S

YG1 and
P

HO81 and the human gene
X

PR1; Pfam ID: PF03105), with InsP8 binding with the highest affinity [21, 36, 51, 52, 53, 54, 55].

Cellular levels of InsP8 are determined by the relative activities of the kinase and phosphatase domains, and the zero‐order kinetics of the kinase domain indicate that it would be refractory to fluctuations in the 5‐InsP7 substrate. The kinase domains of SpAsp1, ScVip1, and human PPIP5K2 (but not PPIPK1) are stimulated by Pi and by Mg2+‐ATP [34, 41, 56]. Importantly, enzyme activity from the histidine acid phosphatase is inhibited by Pi at physiological concentrations [16, 25, 34, 41, 56]. Inhibition occurs with the isolated phosphatase domain, indicating direct action by Pi [41]. The exact mechanism for Pi inhibition is not fully understood because crystals with Pi or its analogs would not diffract [25]; however, the authors speculated that Pi binding to the outer substrate binding surface may block association of the 5‐InsP7 substrate with the enzyme [25]. Stimulation of kinase activity by Pi and Mg‐ATP while inhibition of phosphatase activity by Pi allows robust regulation of InsP8 levels in response to cellular energy (ATP:ADP) and cytosolic phosphate pools.

Phosphate homeostasis and other phenotypes are associated with perturbations in InsP8

InsP8 is implicated in the regulation of proteins involved in phosphate homeostasis across eukaryotes through the binding to SPX domains [57, 58, 59]. Additionally, InsP8 and 1‐InsP7 have been implicated in other cellular processes, beyond phosphate homeostasis, indicating an important role for the bifunctional enzyme within the cell.FungiThe responses to phosphate limitation in S. cerevisiae and S. pombe and the phenotypes associated with mutations in the enzymes that metabolize PP‐InsPs have been the subjects of excellent reviews [48, 49, 60, 61]; here, this review will emphasize recent advances.Briefly, phosphate homeostasis in S. cerevisiae depends upon Pi uptake by low affinity inorganic phosphate importers (Pho87, Pho90); it is synthesized into polyphosphate (polyP) by the VTC complex and stored in the vacuole or it is secreted into the extracellular space, likely through the Syg1 transporter (review by [48]). Phosphate limitation is sensed by Pho81, which inhibits the Pho80/Pho85 kinase, and allows the transcription factors Pho4/Pho2 to upregulate genes encoding high affinity transporters (PHO84, PHO89), secreted phosphatases (PHO5), and other genes [48]. Phosphate homeostasis in Candida albicans and Cryptococcus neoformans replies on similar proteins [54, 62, 63]. In S. pombe, phosphate homeostasis depends upon Pi transporters (Pho84, Pho841), the vacuolar VTC complex, the Xpr1/Spx2 phosphate exporter, a cell surface acid phosphatase encoded by Pho1, and a glycerophosphate transporter Tgp1. The transcription factor Pho7 induces gene expression under phosphate starvation conditions but it is not under regulation of a cyclin‐dependent kinase; instead, long noncoding RNAs repress expression of the target genes [61]. S. cerevisiae has 10 proteins with SPX domains and S. pombe has 6, all of which are involved in phosphate homeostasis [48, 64]. Deletion of the ScVIP1 gene, necessary for synthesis of 1‐InsP7 and InsP8, has little to no effect on the polyP pools [36, 65].A recent study sheds light on the importance of the yeast vacuole as a buffer for Pi storage and utilization [66]. Under Pi replete conditions, high levels of InsP8 bind to the SPX domains on the VTC complex, stimulating it to synthesize and translocate polyP into the vacuole; additionally, InsP8 binds to the SPX domain on Pho81, the vacuolar phosphate transporter, to inhibit its export activity. Intriguingly, polyP is rapidly broken down by the endopolyphosphatases Ppn1 and Ppn2 to Pi within the vacuole, until high levels (maximum accumulation at 30 mm) inhibit enzyme activity. Thus, the vacuole would contain a mixture of polyP and Pi. When the cytosolic Pi levels fall, the balance of ScVip1 enzymatic activities change from kinase to pyrophosphatase leading to InsP8 degradation; low InsP8 relieves Pho81 inhibition, and Pi is released from the vacuole into the cytosol.This bifunctional enzyme (Asp1/Vip1/Vlp1) from several fungal species—S. pombe, S. cerevisiae, Aspergillus nidulans and Ustilago maydis—affects the cortical actin cytoskeleton, microtubule dynamics and organization, and polarized cell growth [19, 22, 67]; in fact, the first gene identified was discovered as a suppressor of a cold‐sensitive arp3 mutant allele in S. pombe (ASP1 named as arp‐sop‐profilin interactor). Loss of the pyrophosphatase activity (Spasp1‐H397A) led to flocculation and an increase in invasive growth in S. pombe [19]. In addition to the actin and microtubule cytoskeleton, the SpAsp1 enzyme affects chromosome segregation. SpAsp1 generation of PP‐InsPs is required for spindle assembly; the loss of pyrophosphatase activity increases microtubule stability and overexpression decreases stability [21, 68]. SpAsp1 is necessary for chromosome biorientation and for correct chromosome segregation [68]. The ScVIP1 gene shows genetic interactions with ScBUB3, which encodes a kinetochore checkpoint protein [69]. Similarly, phenotypes due to temperature‐sensitive alleles of proteins in the fungal constitutive centromere‐associated network (CCAN: Spmal2‐1, Spfta2‐291, Spmis6‐302) were suppressed by mutations in Scasp1

365‐920
 (pyrophosphatase domain) or enhanced by mutations in Scasp

1‐364
 (kinase domain), which indicates a critical role for InsP8 in chromosome segregation [70]. SpAsp1 is also important in generating PP‐InsPs (InsP8 and/or 1‐InsP7) that are necessary for the nuclear production of polyP and the polyphosphorylation of the fungal nucleolin SpGar2 [71]. It remains to be determined how InsP8 affects these processes—by allosteric regulation, protein pyrophosphorylation or protein polyphosphorylation—but points to significant roles for InsP8 outside of phosphate homeostasis.In S. pombe, PP‐InsPs are agonists of RNA polymerase II 3′‐end processing and transcriptional termination [61, 72]. The effects of the phosphatase‐dead allele asp1‐H397A are mild; however, they are synthetically lethal with a null allele in SpAPS1, a Nudix phosphatase (see below) that also dephosphorylates the β phosphate on the 1C of 1‐InsP7 and InsP8 [72]. Suppression of lethality occurs with mutations in subunits of the cleavage and transcription termination complex CPF and substitution of Thr4 to Ala in the C‐terminal domain (CTD) repeats of RNA polymerase II [42, 72, 73, 74]. Thr4 of the CTD is nonessential in S. cerevisiae but is phosphorylated and a mark for termination [75]. Intriguingly, the Thr4 position is also important in S. cerevisiae for responses to phosphate limitation, but the mechanism may be linked to chromatin structure rather than transcriptional termination [76]. Analysis of the S. pombe double mutant (asp1‐H397A aps1Δ) in the presence of suppressors permitted an analysis of PP‐InsP levels; these mutants accumulated an excess 1‐InsP7 that led to overzealous 3′‐end processing and transcriptional termination [77]. The inability to generate a double mutant in M. polymorpha with the genes MpVIP1 and MpDDP1 (the ortholog of SpAPS1) suggests synthetic lethality [29], although the underlying reasons are unknown. The impacts of PP‐InsPs on 3′‐end processing and termination in other species have not been investigated, but these findings indicate a role for InsP8 or 1‐InsP7 in gene expression.MammalsThe human PPIP5K1 gene is located on chromosome 15, and PPIP5K2 is located on chromosome 5; both genes have over 30 exons, show alternative splicing, and are widely expressed in tissues across the body [78]. PPIP5K1 showed highest expression in skeletal muscle, brain, and heart in adult tissues and higher expression in the duodenum and intestine during fetal development [20, 79, 80]. Expression of PPIP5K1 is induced ~4‐fold by osmotic stress in HEK cells in culture [33]. PPIP5K2 showed expression in the cochlear and vestibular sensory hair cells, in supporting cells, and in spiral ganglion neurons as well as in many other tissues [39, 81]. The PPIP5K1 protein has 1406 amino acids and PPIP5K2 with 1243 amino acids; both proteins have an N‐terminal kinase domain, the histidine acid pyrophosphatase domain, and C‐terminal intrinsically disordered regions [33, 82]. Deletion of both the PPIP5K1 and PPIP5K2 genes in the HCT116 colon cancer cell line led to an increase in cellular ATP levels that may be due to enhanced glycolysis and increased oxidative phosphorylation [47].Disease‐associated mutations have been identified in the phosphatase domain of the PPIP5K2 gene. The R837H substitution variant is associated with an inherited form of deafness [39]. There is reduced pyrophosphatase activity and increased kinase activity, leading to an increase in InsP8 [39]. In a mouse model, deletion of the pyrophosphatase domain led to degeneration of cochlear outer hair cells [39]. The PPIP5K2 gene is linked to familial keratoconus, which is degeneration of the cornea [83]; PPIP5K2 showed fivefold higher expression than PPIP5K1 in corneal cells. The missense mutations S419A and N843S both occur in the pyrophosphatase domain [83, 84]. Increased mitochondrial ATP production and decreased glycolysis were found in primary human corneal fibroblast cells in which the expression of PPIP5K2 was knocked down in experiments using shRNAs, indicating altered energy homeostasis might contribute to keratoconus [85].InsP8, generated by PPIP5K, participates in phosphate homeostasis in animals through the transporter XPR1 (xenotropic and polytropic retrovirus receptor 1). In mammals, phosphate uptake, storage, and utilization are regulated at the organismal and cellular levels; ~85% of phosphate is found as apatite (calcium phosphate) crystals associated with collagen in bone, ~14% is in soft tissue, and ~ 1% is circulating in serum [86]. At the organismal level, regulation of serum phosphate is tightly controlled by parathyroid hormone (PTH), fibroblast growth factor 23 (FGF23), and vitamin D (reviewed in [86, 87]). At the cellular level, phosphate homeostasis depends upon transporter activity for phosphate uptake and efflux [88]. Uptake relies on PiT1 and PiT2 (Na+‐Pi cotransporters) and efflux by XPR1 [89]. XPR1 is an essential gene that is expressed in all cells, and gene variants are associated with the hereditary neurodegenerative disorder primary familial brain calcification [89, 90]. InsP8 binds to the SPX domain of XPR1 to enhance Pi efflux [34, 51, 91, 92]. Several recent structural studies have demonstrated mechanisms for the opening of the channel in response to InsP8 association with the SPX domain [55, 93, 94, 95, 96, 97, 98, 99, 100, 101]. Activity by the bifunctional PPIP5K enzymes to generate InsP8 in response to cytosolic Pi levels regulates export by XPR1.In addition to generating InsP8 to regulate XPR1, mammalian PPIP5K proteins have been shown to interact with other cellular components. PPIP5K1 protein has a conserved C‐terminal intrinsically disordered region (IDR) that interacts with exocyst complex 1 and other proteins involved in vesicular transport [82]. The knock‐down of PPIP5K1 decreases cell motility in wound healing assays [82]. Machkalyan and colleagues noted the intriguing functional similarities of the human PPIP5K1 and S. pombe Asp1 in affecting the actin cytoskeleton, although mechanistic details differ greatly. The PPIP5K1 protein also interacts with phosphatidylinositol‐(4,5)‐bisphosphate on the plasma membrane [102]. Increased synthesis of InsP8 occurred in response to PDGF (platelet‐derived growth factor) and insulin treatment of myoblast cells; the increase in InsP8 was due to inhibition of pyrophosphatase activity by the phosphoinositide [102]. These results suggest there are regulatory mechanisms to generate local concentrations of InsP8 or 1‐InsP7 that may be critical for vesicular trafficking and cell motility.Plants and algaeGenes encoding the bifunctional enzyme are found in green algae (C. reinhardtii CrVIP1, CrVIP2), bryophytes (MpVIP1/MpVIH), and land plants (e.g., Arabidopsis AtVIH1, AtVIH2), and the product InsP8 is associated with phosphate homeostasis and other responses. The emphasis here will be on new findings and readers are encouraged to see recent reviews for more information [14, 103, 104].In plants, the essential macronutrient Pi is often limiting in the environment, leading to stunted growth and lower crop yields. Plants and algae have evolved mechanisms to maximize phosphate acquisition and distribution within the plant (reviewed in [14, 103, 104]). In brief, the phosphate starvation response includes the transcription factors AtPHR1 and AtPHL1 (Arabidopsis), MpPHR1 (M. polymorpha), or CrPSR1 (Chlamydomonas reinhardtii) to respond to phosphate limitation and to induce expression of a network of adaptive target genes. Under phosphate‐replete conditions, activation by these transcription factors (PHR1 as exemplar) is inhibited by an SPX repressor protein. PHR1‐SPX interactions are strengthened by InsP8; high cytosolic Pi promotes kinase activity and inhibits phosphatase activity to increase InsP8 levels. When phosphate becomes limiting, InsP8 levels decline, the affinity of the SPX regulator for the PHR1 regulator is weakened, and PHR1 binds DNA and activates transcription of target genes. These SPX domain proteins respond to inorganic phosphate levels through InsP8. The deletion of both the AtVIH1 and AtVIH2 genes shows lethality and hyperaccumulates Pi whereas the Mpvip1
ge and Mpvih
Tak2 mutants are viable and show no changes to Pi levels [29, 31, 41, 59].SPX proteins regulate other responses. Algae and plants have a large number of SPX proteins (5 in Chlamydomonas, 20 in Arabidopsis, 15 in rice); some are solo SPX domain and others have an N‐terminal SPX domains associated with other moieties (SPX‐EXS transporter, SPX‐RING, SPX‐MFS) [51, 58, 59, 105]. In rice and Arabidopsis, SPX domain proteins regulate nitrate signaling in addition to phosphate, to coordinate these two critical metabolites [106, 107, 108]. In Chlamydomonas, CrVip1 is important for carbon metabolism in addition to phosphate [30].Lines of M. polymorpha were generated lacking the phosphatase domain while retaining the kinase domain (Mpvip1Δdp
ge) and others lacking the entire gene (Mpvip1
ge, Mpvih
Tak2) [29, 31]. As noted above, Laurent and colleagues could not recover a double mutant lacking both the phosphatase domain of MpVIP1 combined with a deletion of MpNUDT1, suggesting synthetic lethality, but they were able to obtain the Mpvip1Δdp
ge
Mppfa‐dsp1

ge
 double mutant (see below for discussion on the other two phosphatases). The Mpvip1Δdp
ge mutant had reduced thallus surface area, which was reduced further in the Mpvip1Δdp
ge
Mppfa‐dsp1

ge
 double mutant. The double mutant also had an increase in rhizoid mass, did not develop gemmae cups (structures that carry clones of the parent plant that are dispersed by rain drops), accumulated higher nitrate levels, and accumulated lower Pi than the parental plants [29]. InsP8 produced by MpVIH inhibits DELLA proteins by promoting their degradation through the proteasome to increase growth and thallus development [31]. These findings indicate important roles for the pyrophosphatases in regulating PP‐InsP levels that affect important cellular functions in metabolism, growth, and development.There is a twofold increase in InsP8 levels upon treatment of Arabidopsis seedlings with methyl jasmonate, and this increase was dependent on AtVih2 [24]. The InsP8 molecule is a cofactor in the COI1‐JAZ receptor [24]. This was important for plant defenses as moth and butterfly larvae feeding on plants with mutations in AtVih2 gained significantly more weight and the plants showed increased leaf damage [24, 46], indicating a role in defenses against herbivory. Given the Pi regulation of the AtVih2 protein, there is a link of defense responses to nutrient status. Overexpression of the AtVih2 kinase domain leads to the jasmonate responses; it remains to be determined if the regulation to increase InsP8 occurs by increased kinase domain activity or inhibition of the phosphatase domain.
