Suppression of lethality occurs with mutations in subunits of the cleavage and transcription termination complex CPF and substitution of Thr4 to Ala in the C‐terminal domain (CTD) repeats of RNA polymerase II [42, 72, 73, 74]. Thr4 of the CTD is nonessential in S. cerevisiae but is phosphorylated and a mark for termination [75]. Intriguingly, the Thr4 position is also important in S. cerevisiae for responses to phosphate limitation, but the mechanism may be linked to chromatin structure rather than transcriptional termination [76]. Analysis of the S. pombe double mutant (asp1‐H397A aps1Δ) in the presence of suppressors permitted an analysis of PP‐InsP levels; these mutants accumulated an excess 1‐InsP7 that led to overzealous 3′‐end processing and transcriptional termination [77]. The inability to generate a double mutant in M. polymorpha with the genes MpVIP1 and MpDDP1 (the ortholog of SpAPS1) suggests synthetic lethality [29], although the underlying reasons are unknown. The impacts of PP‐InsPs on 3′‐end processing and termination in other species have not been investigated, but these findings indicate a role for InsP8 or 1‐InsP7 in gene expression.

The responses to phosphate limitation in S. cerevisiae and S. pombe and the phenotypes associated with mutations in the enzymes that metabolize PP‐InsPs have been the subjects of excellent reviews [48, 49, 60, 61]; here, this review will emphasize recent advances.

Briefly, phosphate homeostasis in S. cerevisiae depends upon Pi uptake by low affinity inorganic phosphate importers (Pho87, Pho90); it is synthesized into polyphosphate (polyP) by the VTC complex and stored in the vacuole or it is secreted into the extracellular space, likely through the Syg1 transporter (review by [48]). Phosphate limitation is sensed by Pho81, which inhibits the Pho80/Pho85 kinase, and allows the transcription factors Pho4/Pho2 to upregulate genes encoding high affinity transporters (PHO84, PHO89), secreted phosphatases (PHO5), and other genes [48]. Phosphate homeostasis in Candida albicans and Cryptococcus neoformans replies on similar proteins [54, 62, 63]. In S. pombe, phosphate homeostasis depends upon Pi transporters (Pho84, Pho841), the vacuolar VTC complex, the Xpr1/Spx2 phosphate exporter, a cell surface acid phosphatase encoded by Pho1, and a glycerophosphate transporter Tgp1. The transcription factor Pho7 induces gene expression under phosphate starvation conditions but it is not under regulation of a cyclin‐dependent kinase; instead, long noncoding RNAs repress expression of the target genes [61]. S. cerevisiae has 10 proteins with SPX domains and S. pombe has 6, all of which are involved in phosphate homeostasis [48, 64]. Deletion of the ScVIP1 gene, necessary for synthesis of 1‐InsP7 and InsP8, has little to no effect on the polyP pools [36, 65].

A recent study sheds light on the importance of the yeast vacuole as a buffer for Pi storage and utilization [66]. Under Pi replete conditions, high levels of InsP8 bind to the SPX domains on the VTC complex, stimulating it to synthesize and translocate polyP into the vacuole; additionally, InsP8 binds to the SPX domain on Pho81, the vacuolar phosphate transporter, to inhibit its export activity. Intriguingly, polyP is rapidly broken down by the endopolyphosphatases Ppn1 and Ppn2 to Pi within the vacuole, until high levels (maximum accumulation at 30 mm) inhibit enzyme activity. Thus, the vacuole would contain a mixture of polyP and Pi. When the cytosolic Pi levels fall, the balance of ScVip1 enzymatic activities change from kinase to pyrophosphatase leading to InsP8 degradation; low InsP8 relieves Pho81 inhibition, and Pi is released from the vacuole into the cytosol.

This bifunctional enzyme (Asp1/Vip1/Vlp1) from several fungal species—S. pombe, S. cerevisiae, Aspergillus nidulans and Ustilago maydis—affects the cortical actin cytoskeleton, microtubule dynamics and organization, and polarized cell growth [19, 22, 67]; in fact, the first gene identified was discovered as a suppressor of a cold‐sensitive arp3 mutant allele in S. pombe (ASP1 named as arp‐sop‐profilin interactor). Loss of the pyrophosphatase activity (Spasp1‐H397A) led to flocculation and an increase in invasive growth in S. pombe [19]. In addition to the actin and microtubule cytoskeleton, the SpAsp1 enzyme affects chromosome segregation. SpAsp1 generation of PP‐InsPs is required for spindle assembly; the loss of pyrophosphatase activity increases microtubule stability and overexpression decreases stability [21, 68]. SpAsp1 is necessary for chromosome biorientation and for correct chromosome segregation [68]. The ScVIP1 gene shows genetic interactions with ScBUB3, which encodes a kinetochore checkpoint protein [69]. Similarly, phenotypes due to temperature‐sensitive alleles of proteins in the fungal constitutive centromere‐associated network (CCAN: Spmal2‐1, Spfta2‐291, Spmis6‐302) were suppressed by mutations in Scasp1

365‐920
 (pyrophosphatase domain) or enhanced by mutations in Scasp

1‐364
 (kinase domain), which indicates a critical role for InsP8 in chromosome segregation [70]. SpAsp1 is also important in generating PP‐InsPs (InsP8 and/or 1‐InsP7) that are necessary for the nuclear production of polyP and the polyphosphorylation of the fungal nucleolin SpGar2 [71]. It remains to be determined how InsP8 affects these processes—by allosteric regulation, protein pyrophosphorylation or protein polyphosphorylation—but points to significant roles for InsP8 outside of phosphate homeostasis.

In S. pombe, PP‐InsPs are agonists of RNA polymerase II 3′‐end processing and transcriptional termination [61, 72]. The effects of the phosphatase‐dead allele asp1‐H397A are mild; however, they are synthetically lethal with a null allele in SpAPS1, a Nudix phosphatase (see below) that also dephosphorylates the β phosphate on the 1C of 1‐InsP7 and InsP8 [72]. Suppression of lethality occurs with mutations in subunits of the cleavage and transcription termination complex CPF and substitution of Thr4 to Ala in the C‐terminal domain (CTD) repeats of RNA polymerase II [42, 72, 73, 74]. Thr4 of the CTD is nonessential in S. cerevisiae but is phosphorylated and a mark for termination [75]. Intriguingly, the Thr4 position is also important in S. cerevisiae for responses to phosphate limitation, but the mechanism may be linked to chromatin structure rather than transcriptional termination [76]. Analysis of the S. pombe double mutant (asp1‐H397A aps1Δ) in the presence of suppressors permitted an analysis of PP‐InsP levels; these mutants accumulated an excess 1‐InsP7 that led to overzealous 3′‐end processing and transcriptional termination [77]. The inability to generate a double mutant in M. polymorpha with the genes MpVIP1 and MpDDP1 (the ortholog of SpAPS1) suggests synthetic lethality [29], although the underlying reasons are unknown. The impacts of PP‐InsPs on 3′‐end processing and termination in other species have not been investigated, but these findings indicate a role for InsP8 or 1‐InsP7 in gene expression.

MammalsThe human PPIP5K1 gene is located on chromosome 15, and PPIP5K2 is located on chromosome 5; both genes have over 30 exons, show alternative splicing, and are widely expressed in tissues across the body [78]. PPIP5K1 showed highest expression in skeletal muscle, brain, and heart in adult tissues and higher expression in the duodenum and intestine during fetal development [20, 79, 80]. Expression of PPIP5K1 is induced ~4‐fold by osmotic stress in HEK cells in culture [33]. PPIP5K2 showed expression in the cochlear and vestibular sensory hair cells, in supporting cells, and in spiral ganglion neurons as well as in many other tissues [39, 81]. The PPIP5K1 protein has 1406 amino acids and PPIP5K2 with 1243 amino acids; both proteins have an N‐terminal kinase domain, the histidine acid pyrophosphatase domain, and C‐terminal intrinsically disordered regions [33, 82]. Deletion of both the PPIP5K1 and PPIP5K2 genes in the HCT116 colon cancer cell line led to an increase in cellular ATP levels that may be due to enhanced glycolysis and increased oxidative phosphorylation [47].Disease‐associated mutations have been identified in the phosphatase domain of the PPIP5K2 gene. The R837H substitution variant is associated with an inherited form of deafness [39]. There is reduced pyrophosphatase activity and increased kinase activity, leading to an increase in InsP8 [39]. In a mouse model, deletion of the pyrophosphatase domain led to degeneration of cochlear outer hair cells [39]. The PPIP5K2 gene is linked to familial keratoconus, which is degeneration of the cornea [83]; PPIP5K2 showed fivefold higher expression than PPIP5K1 in corneal cells. The missense mutations S419A and N843S both occur in the pyrophosphatase domain [83, 84]. Increased mitochondrial ATP production and decreased glycolysis were found in primary human corneal fibroblast cells in which the expression of PPIP5K2 was knocked down in experiments using shRNAs, indicating altered energy homeostasis might contribute to keratoconus [85].InsP8, generated by PPIP5K, participates in phosphate homeostasis in animals through the transporter XPR1 (xenotropic and polytropic retrovirus receptor 1). In mammals, phosphate uptake, storage, and utilization are regulated at the organismal and cellular levels; ~85% of phosphate is found as apatite (calcium phosphate) crystals associated with collagen in bone, ~14% is in soft tissue, and ~ 1% is circulating in serum [86]. At the organismal level, regulation of serum phosphate is tightly controlled by parathyroid hormone (PTH), fibroblast growth factor 23 (FGF23), and vitamin D (reviewed in [86, 87]). At the cellular level, phosphate homeostasis depends upon transporter activity for phosphate uptake and efflux [88]. Uptake relies on PiT1 and PiT2 (Na+‐Pi cotransporters) and efflux by XPR1 [89]. XPR1 is an essential gene that is expressed in all cells, and gene variants are associated with the hereditary neurodegenerative disorder primary familial brain calcification [89, 90]. InsP8 binds to the SPX domain of XPR1 to enhance Pi efflux [34, 51, 91, 92]. Several recent structural studies have demonstrated mechanisms for the opening of the channel in response to InsP8 association with the SPX domain [55, 93, 94, 95, 96, 97, 98, 99, 100, 101]. Activity by the bifunctional PPIP5K enzymes to generate InsP8 in response to cytosolic Pi levels regulates export by XPR1.In addition to generating InsP8 to regulate XPR1, mammalian PPIP5K proteins have been shown to interact with other cellular components. PPIP5K1 protein has a conserved C‐terminal intrinsically disordered region (IDR) that interacts with exocyst complex 1 and other proteins involved in vesicular transport [82]. The knock‐down of PPIP5K1 decreases cell motility in wound healing assays [82]. Machkalyan and colleagues noted the intriguing functional similarities of the human PPIP5K1 and S. pombe Asp1 in affecting the actin cytoskeleton, although mechanistic details differ greatly. The PPIP5K1 protein also interacts with phosphatidylinositol‐(4,5)‐bisphosphate on the plasma membrane [102]. Increased synthesis of InsP8 occurred in response to PDGF (platelet‐derived growth factor) and insulin treatment of myoblast cells; the increase in InsP8 was due to inhibition of pyrophosphatase activity by the phosphoinositide [102]. These results suggest there are regulatory mechanisms to generate local concentrations of InsP8 or 1‐InsP7 that may be critical for vesicular trafficking and cell motility.

The human PPIP5K1 gene is located on chromosome 15, and PPIP5K2 is located on chromosome 5; both genes have over 30 exons, show alternative splicing, and are widely expressed in tissues across the body [78]. PPIP5K1 showed highest expression in skeletal muscle, brain, and heart in adult tissues and higher expression in the duodenum and intestine during fetal development [20, 79, 80]. Expression of PPIP5K1 is induced ~4‐fold by osmotic stress in HEK cells in culture [33]. PPIP5K2 showed expression in the cochlear and vestibular sensory hair cells, in supporting cells, and in spiral ganglion neurons as well as in many other tissues [39, 81]. The PPIP5K1 protein has 1406 amino acids and PPIP5K2 with 1243 amino acids; both proteins have an N‐terminal kinase domain, the histidine acid pyrophosphatase domain, and C‐terminal intrinsically disordered regions [33, 82]. Deletion of both the PPIP5K1 and PPIP5K2 genes in the HCT116 colon cancer cell line led to an increase in cellular ATP levels that may be due to enhanced glycolysis and increased oxidative phosphorylation [47].

Disease‐associated mutations have been identified in the phosphatase domain of the PPIP5K2 gene. The R837H substitution variant is associated with an inherited form of deafness [39]. There is reduced pyrophosphatase activity and increased kinase activity, leading to an increase in InsP8 [39]. In a mouse model, deletion of the pyrophosphatase domain led to degeneration of cochlear outer hair cells [39]. The PPIP5K2 gene is linked to familial keratoconus, which is degeneration of the cornea [83]; PPIP5K2 showed fivefold higher expression than PPIP5K1 in corneal cells. The missense mutations S419A and N843S both occur in the pyrophosphatase domain [83, 84]. Increased mitochondrial ATP production and decreased glycolysis were found in primary human corneal fibroblast cells in which the expression of PPIP5K2 was knocked down in experiments using shRNAs, indicating altered energy homeostasis might contribute to keratoconus [85].

InsP8, generated by PPIP5K, participates in phosphate homeostasis in animals through the transporter XPR1 (xenotropic and polytropic retrovirus receptor 1). In mammals, phosphate uptake, storage, and utilization are regulated at the organismal and cellular levels; ~85% of phosphate is found as apatite (calcium phosphate) crystals associated with collagen in bone, ~14% is in soft tissue, and ~ 1% is circulating in serum [86]. At the organismal level, regulation of serum phosphate is tightly controlled by parathyroid hormone (PTH), fibroblast growth factor 23 (FGF23), and vitamin D (reviewed in [86, 87]). At the cellular level, phosphate homeostasis depends upon transporter activity for phosphate uptake and efflux [88]. Uptake relies on PiT1 and PiT2 (Na+‐Pi cotransporters) and efflux by XPR1 [89]. XPR1 is an essential gene that is expressed in all cells, and gene variants are associated with the hereditary neurodegenerative disorder primary familial brain calcification [89, 90]. InsP8 binds to the SPX domain of XPR1 to enhance Pi efflux [34, 51, 91, 92]. Several recent structural studies have demonstrated mechanisms for the opening of the channel in response to InsP8 association with the SPX domain [55, 93, 94, 95, 96, 97, 98, 99, 100, 101]. Activity by the bifunctional PPIP5K enzymes to generate InsP8 in response to cytosolic Pi levels regulates export by XPR1.

In addition to generating InsP8 to regulate XPR1, mammalian PPIP5K proteins have been shown to interact with other cellular components. PPIP5K1 protein has a conserved C‐terminal intrinsically disordered region (IDR) that interacts with exocyst complex 1 and other proteins involved in vesicular transport [82]. The knock‐down of PPIP5K1 decreases cell motility in wound healing assays [82]. Machkalyan and colleagues noted the intriguing functional similarities of the human PPIP5K1 and S. pombe Asp1 in affecting the actin cytoskeleton, although mechanistic details differ greatly. The PPIP5K1 protein also interacts with phosphatidylinositol‐(4,5)‐bisphosphate on the plasma membrane [102]. Increased synthesis of InsP8 occurred in response to PDGF (platelet‐derived growth factor) and insulin treatment of myoblast cells; the increase in InsP8 was due to inhibition of pyrophosphatase activity by the phosphoinositide [102]. These results suggest there are regulatory mechanisms to generate local concentrations of InsP8 or 1‐InsP7 that may be critical for vesicular trafficking and cell motility.

Plants and algaeGenes encoding the bifunctional enzyme are found in green algae (C. reinhardtii CrVIP1, CrVIP2), bryophytes (MpVIP1/MpVIH), and land plants (e.g., Arabidopsis AtVIH1, AtVIH2), and the product InsP8 is associated with phosphate homeostasis and other responses. The emphasis here will be on new findings and readers are encouraged to see recent reviews for more information [14, 103, 104].In plants, the essential macronutrient Pi is often limiting in the environment, leading to stunted growth and lower crop yields. Plants and algae have evolved mechanisms to maximize phosphate acquisition and distribution within the plant (reviewed in [14, 103, 104]). In brief, the phosphate starvation response includes the transcription factors AtPHR1 and AtPHL1 (Arabidopsis), MpPHR1 (M. polymorpha), or CrPSR1 (Chlamydomonas reinhardtii) to respond to phosphate limitation and to induce expression of a network of adaptive target genes. Under phosphate‐replete conditions, activation by these transcription factors (PHR1 as exemplar) is inhibited by an SPX repressor protein. PHR1‐SPX interactions are strengthened by InsP8; high cytosolic Pi promotes kinase activity and inhibits phosphatase activity to increase InsP8 levels. When phosphate becomes limiting, InsP8 levels decline, the affinity of the SPX regulator for the PHR1 regulator is weakened, and PHR1 binds DNA and activates transcription of target genes. These SPX domain proteins respond to inorganic phosphate levels through InsP8. The deletion of both the AtVIH1 and AtVIH2 genes shows lethality and hyperaccumulates Pi whereas the Mpvip1
ge and Mpvih
Tak2 mutants are viable and show no changes to Pi levels [29, 31, 41, 59].SPX proteins regulate other responses. Algae and plants have a large number of SPX proteins (5 in Chlamydomonas, 20 in Arabidopsis, 15 in rice); some are solo SPX domain and others have an N‐terminal SPX domains associated with other moieties (SPX‐EXS transporter, SPX‐RING, SPX‐MFS) [51, 58, 59, 105]. In rice and Arabidopsis, SPX domain proteins regulate nitrate signaling in addition to phosphate, to coordinate these two critical metabolites [106, 107, 108]. In Chlamydomonas, CrVip1 is important for carbon metabolism in addition to phosphate [30].Lines of M. polymorpha were generated lacking the phosphatase domain while retaining the kinase domain (Mpvip1Δdp

ge

The double mutant also had an increase in rhizoid mass, did not develop gemmae cups (structures that carry clones of the parent plant that are dispersed by rain drops), accumulated higher nitrate levels, and accumulated lower Pi than the parental plants [29]. InsP8 produced by MpVIH inhibits DELLA proteins by promoting their degradation through the proteasome to increase growth and thallus development [31]. These findings indicate important roles for the pyrophosphatases in regulating PP‐InsP levels that affect important cellular functions in metabolism, growth, and development.There is a twofold increase in InsP8 levels upon treatment of Arabidopsis seedlings with methyl jasmonate, and this increase was dependent on AtVih2 [24]. The InsP8 molecule is a cofactor in the COI1‐JAZ receptor [24]. This was important for plant defenses as moth and butterfly larvae feeding on plants with mutations in AtVih2 gained significantly more weight and the plants showed increased leaf damage [24, 46], indicating a role in defenses against herbivory. Given the Pi regulation of the AtVih2 protein, there is a link of defense responses to nutrient status. Overexpression of the AtVih2 kinase domain leads to the jasmonate responses; it remains to be determined if the regulation to increase InsP8 occurs by increased kinase domain activity or inhibition of the phosphatase domain.

Plants and algae

Genes encoding the bifunctional enzyme are found in green algae (C. reinhardtii CrVIP1, CrVIP2), bryophytes (MpVIP1/MpVIH), and land plants (e.g., Arabidopsis AtVIH1, AtVIH2), and the product InsP8 is associated with phosphate homeostasis and other responses. The emphasis here will be on new findings and readers are encouraged to see recent reviews for more information [14, 103, 104].

In plants, the essential macronutrient Pi is often limiting in the environment, leading to stunted growth and lower crop yields. Plants and algae have evolved mechanisms to maximize phosphate acquisition and distribution within the plant (reviewed in [14, 103, 104]). In brief, the phosphate starvation response includes the transcription factors AtPHR1 and AtPHL1 (Arabidopsis), MpPHR1 (M. polymorpha), or CrPSR1 (Chlamydomonas reinhardtii) to respond to phosphate limitation and to induce expression of a network of adaptive target genes. Under phosphate‐replete conditions, activation by these transcription factors (PHR1 as exemplar) is inhibited by an SPX repressor protein. PHR1‐SPX interactions are strengthened by InsP8; high cytosolic Pi promotes kinase activity and inhibits phosphatase activity to increase InsP8 levels. When phosphate becomes limiting, InsP8 levels decline, the affinity of the SPX regulator for the PHR1 regulator is weakened, and PHR1 binds DNA and activates transcription of target genes. These SPX domain proteins respond to inorganic phosphate levels through InsP8. The deletion of both the AtVIH1 and AtVIH2 genes shows lethality and hyperaccumulates Pi whereas the Mpvip1
ge and Mpvih
Tak2 mutants are viable and show no changes to Pi levels [29, 31, 41, 59].

SPX proteins regulate other responses. Algae and plants have a large number of SPX proteins (5 in Chlamydomonas, 20 in Arabidopsis, 15 in rice); some are solo SPX domain and others have an N‐terminal SPX domains associated with other moieties (SPX‐EXS transporter, SPX‐RING, SPX‐MFS) [51, 58, 59, 105]. In rice and Arabidopsis, SPX domain proteins regulate nitrate signaling in addition to phosphate, to coordinate these two critical metabolites [106, 107, 108]. In Chlamydomonas, CrVip1 is important for carbon metabolism in addition to phosphate [30].

Lines of M. polymorpha were generated lacking the phosphatase domain while retaining the kinase domain (Mpvip1Δdp
ge) and others lacking the entire gene (Mpvip1
ge, Mpvih
Tak2) [29, 31]. As noted above, Laurent and colleagues could not recover a double mutant lacking both the phosphatase domain of MpVIP1 combined with a deletion of MpNUDT1, suggesting synthetic lethality, but they were able to obtain the Mpvip1Δdp
ge
Mppfa‐dsp1

ge
 double mutant (see below for discussion on the other two phosphatases). The Mpvip1Δdp
ge mutant had reduced thallus surface area, which was reduced further in the Mpvip1Δdp
ge
Mppfa‐dsp1

ge
 double mutant. The double mutant also had an increase in rhizoid mass, did not develop gemmae cups (structures that carry clones of the parent plant that are dispersed by rain drops), accumulated higher nitrate levels, and accumulated lower Pi than the parental plants [29]. InsP8 produced by MpVIH inhibits DELLA proteins by promoting their degradation through the proteasome to increase growth and thallus development [31]. These findings indicate important roles for the pyrophosphatases in regulating PP‐InsP levels that affect important cellular functions in metabolism, growth, and development.

There is a twofold increase in InsP8 levels upon treatment of Arabidopsis seedlings with methyl jasmonate, and this increase was dependent on AtVih2 [24]. The InsP8 molecule is a cofactor in the COI1‐JAZ receptor [24]. This was important for plant defenses as moth and butterfly larvae feeding on plants with mutations in AtVih2 gained significantly more weight and the plants showed increased leaf damage [24, 46], indicating a role in defenses against herbivory. Given the Pi regulation of the AtVih2 protein, there is a link of defense responses to nutrient status. Overexpression of the AtVih2 kinase domain leads to the jasmonate responses; it remains to be determined if the regulation to increase InsP8 occurs by increased kinase domain activity or inhibition of the phosphatase domain.

Summary of the histidine acid phosphatase superfamily and unanswered questionsThe histidine acid phosphatase superfamily is present in essentially all eukaryotes and is exclusively found as a bifunctional enzyme with an N‐terminal kinase domain and a C‐terminal phosphatase domain that act upon the same phosphate: The diphosphate at the 1C position of 1,5‐InsP8 and 1‐InsP7. Catalytic activity of the phosphatase domain is regulated by inorganic phosphate, likely by blocking access of the substrate to the active site. The conserved mechanism across eukaryotes is that InsP8 regulates phosphate homeostasis through allostery, by binding to SPX domains, although the particular proteins are species specific. Importantly, InsP8 and 1‐InsP7 are implicated in other cellular processes including polarized cell growth, vesicular trafficking, the actin and microtubule cytoskeleton, chromosome segregation, 3′‐end processing and transcription termination, and as a cofactor in receptors. Open questions that need addressing concern a deeper understanding of the molecular mechanisms for these processes, if there is regulated production or degradation of InsP8 or 1‐InsP7 to affect these processes, and which of these affected processes are generally affected across eukaryotes and which ones are clade or species specific.

Summary of the histidine acid phosphatase superfamily and unanswered questions

The histidine acid phosphatase superfamily is present in essentially all eukaryotes and is exclusively found as a bifunctional enzyme with an N‐terminal kinase domain and a C‐terminal phosphatase domain that act upon the same phosphate: The diphosphate at the 1C position of 1,5‐InsP8 and 1‐InsP7. Catalytic activity of the phosphatase domain is regulated by inorganic phosphate, likely by blocking access of the substrate to the active site. The conserved mechanism across eukaryotes is that InsP8 regulates phosphate homeostasis through allostery, by binding to SPX domains, although the particular proteins are species specific. Importantly, InsP8 and 1‐InsP7 are implicated in other cellular processes including polarized cell growth, vesicular trafficking, the actin and microtubule cytoskeleton, chromosome segregation, 3′‐end processing and transcription termination, and as a cofactor in receptors. Open questions that need addressing concern a deeper understanding of the molecular mechanisms for these processes, if there is regulated production or degradation of InsP8 or 1‐InsP7 to affect these processes, and which of these affected processes are generally affected across eukaryotes and which ones are clade or species specific.

The cysteine phosphatase superfamilyCysteine phosphatases are part of a large superfamily of hydrolases with an active site cysteine that acts as a nucleophile (reviewed in [109, 110, 111]). The active site residues, HCxxGxxR (x stands for any amino acid), are found in proteins with three different folds indicating convergent evolution [112]. The Cys‐class 1 (CC1) fold includes the protein tyrosine phosphatase (PTP) superfamily that hydrolyzes the phosphate ester from phosphotyrosine (P‐Tyr) in proteins and peptides, the dual‐specificity protein tyrosine phosphatase (DSP or DUSP) subfamily that also hydrolyzes phosphoserine (P‐Ser) and phosphothreonine (P‐Thr) in addition to P‐Tyr, and a third subfamily, the atypical DSPs, that recognize a broader range of substrates including dephosphorylated nucleotide triphosphates, phosphorylated carbohydrates, and phosphatidylinositides [109, 112]. The focus of this review is on a subgroup within this latter family, the plant–fungal atypical dual‐specificity phosphatases (PFA‐DSP) subfamily. Enzymes in this PFA‐DSP family are capable of specifically cleaving the β phosphate in PP‐InsPs.Enzymes of the PFA‐DSP family are found in plants, fungi, kinetoplastids, slime molds, choanoflagellates, and sponges, but they are not present in eumetazoan clades suggesting the genes encoding them were lost during evolution [29, 112, 113, 114, 115]. The PFA‐DSP family has been expanded in plants; for example, A. thaliana carries five homologs [114, 115].Structures of the PFA‐DSP family members provided insights into critical differences from the PTP family and substrate specificityA pair of structural papers established the novel PFA‐DSP class of proteins (see Table 2). The first report was a low‐resolution structure of the Arabidopsis AtPFA‐DSP1 as an unusual member of the DSP1 family and a metal‐independent phosphatase [118], and the second examined conserved protein motifs and phylogeny [113]. Both noted the sequence conservation with the ScSiw14. Two structures of ScSiw14 were determined (Fig. 4); a 2.35 Å structure of the catalytic domain lacking the N terminus [116] and a pseudoatomic model of the full‐length ScSiw14 protein based on crystallography, secondary structure assays using circular dichroism, and modeling studies [117]. The full‐length structural model demonstrated that ScSiw14 is a zwitterionic protein with an N‐terminal domain of 108 amino acids that is composed of α‐helical bundles and is acidic in character (isoelectric point of 4.4), and a C‐terminal DSP domain of 165 amino acids that is basic in character (isoelectric point of 9.0) [117]. Each of these reports described the highly positively charged active site cleft and the lack of conservation of the Asp residue in the flexible WPD loop of canonical PTPs that performs acid–base catalysis.Table 2Structures of the S. cerevisiae Siw14 and A. thaliana PFA‐DSP1 proteins.ProteinOrganismPDB codeDescriptionReferences

Each of the inositol polyphosphates was present at a concentration of 10 μm and incubated with purified Siw14 for 30 min. 5PP‐InsP4 is shorthand for 5‐diphosphoinositol‐1,3,4,6‐tetrakisphosphate; InsP6 lacks diphosphates at any position; 1‐ to 6‐InsP7 have the pyrophosphate on the indicated position; and InsP8 carries β phosphates at both the 1C and 5C positions. From Wang et al., 2018 [116], http://creativecommons.org/licenses/by/4.0/.The active site residues in ScSiw14 are 213HCNRGKHR220. The first histidine is invariant in the PTP family, and ionic interactions between His213 and the adjacent Cys214 are critical to stabilize the thiolate anion [120]. Cys214 acts as the nucleophile to form thiophosphate, the enzyme‐substrate intermediate. Arg220 interacts with the PP‐InsP substrate and stabilizes the cysteine‐phosphate intermediate during catalysis [116, 117]. The requirement for this active site was confirmed by showing the loss of activity when the critical amino acids Cys214, Gly217, and Arg220 were mutated [35, 116, 121]. The catalytic cleft is highly basic, and substitutions of the Arg216 and Lys218 residues reduce enzyme efficiency [116]. An unexpected finding from the structural analysis of ScSiw14 is the absence of the canonical general acidic residue, usually an aspartate, that is found in the mobile WPD loop of PTPs; this residue protonates the leaving group and then activates the water to hydrolyze the cysteinyl‐phosphate intermediate. Glu185 lies within the flexible loop, and its substitution with alanine reduced enzymatic activity by 67%; this reduction was substantial but not as severe as the near‐complete loss of function observed in catalytic residue mutants such as Cys214 or Arg220 [116]. The issue was unresolved as one report postulated the lack of the requirement for this general acid, possibly due to hydrolysis of a phosphoanhydride bond as opposed to a phosphoester linkage [116], while a second report postulated that two glutamate residues, Glu120 and Glu185, may both be involved in enzymatic activity, possibly acting as an acidic dyad [117]. However, a recent report of a high‐resolution structure for the Arabidopsis AtPFA‐DSP1 enzyme with substrate has uncovered an important mechanistic role for a histidine residue equivalent to position His219 [119], a novel role that may hold true for the yeast enzyme (see below).The AtPFA‐DSP1 protein has the active site consensus sequence 149HCKRGKHR156; importantly, this amino acid sequence is identical in the five Arabidopsis orthologs (except for substitution of R152S in AtPFA‐DSP4, using the AtPFA‐DSP1 numbering), the four rice orthologs (OsPFA‐DSP1 to OsPFA‐DSP4), other plant orthologs, and, with the exception of the K151N substitution, it is identical in Saccharomyces and other ascomycetes [113, 119, 122]. Substitution of serine for cysteine (C150S) destroyed catalytic activity of AtPFA‐DSP1 [115, 119]. All five of the Arabidopsis AtPFA‐DSP enzymes exhibited robust in vitro activity with 5‐InsP7 and minor activity with 4‐InsP7 and 6‐InsP7 [29, 115, 119]. Two isoforms of InsP8 were examined, the canonical 1,5‐InsP8 as well as 3,5‐InsP8. ScSiw14 and the AtPFA‐DSP enzymes (except AtPFA‐DSP5) cleaved these substrates to either 1‐InsP7 or 3‐InsP7, demonstrating specificity for the 5β phosphate [115].The structure of AtPFA‐DSP1 identified several key enzyme features that may be important in this broader family of PFA‐DSP enzymes [119]. First, both 5‐InsP7 and 6‐InsP7 bound in virtually identical positions and made the same protein contacts [119]; this is important because plants generate 4/6‐InsP7 and AtPFA‐DSP1 could be involved in the hydrolysis of the β phosphate if it is 6‐InsP7. A second finding is that His155 acts as a ‘Janus’ residue: the imidazole ring flips 180° during its interactions with the substrate 5‐InsP7 relative to the product Pi after hydrolysis. A third observation is that a polarized water molecule interacts with His155 which activates it as the general base, an activity normally performed by the WPD loop, and suggesting that this histidine would be invariant [114, 119]. The rotation by His155 and its role as a general base underscores its importance as a conserved amino acid. Finally, while InsP6 is released upon hydrolysis, the second product, Pi, remains bound by the enzyme until it is displaced by a new PP‐InsP substrate in a ‘prisoner exchange’ mechanism [119]. Given the extensive sequence and structural conservation between AtPFA‐DSP1 and ScSiw14, it is possible that these enzymatic features are also found in the yeast protein.Enzymes in the PFA‐DSP family from S. pombe and M. polymorpha hydrolyze the 5β phosphate in vitro
Studies have also been performed on orthologs from S. pombe and an early diverging plant, the bryophyte M. polymorpha (liverwort). Bryophytes carry one gene, MpPFA‐DSP1 (Mp3g10950), that is homologous to ScSIW14 and the five Arabidopsis PFA‐DSP genes [29]. MpPFA‐DSP1 has the conserved active site residues 104HCNKGKHR111, and it has the same substrate preference for the 5β phosphate as the yeast and plant enzymes, with highest activity with 5‐InsP7 and secondly for InsP8 [29]. Mutation of the catalytic cysteine (C105) to alanine eliminated enzyme activity in vitro [29].The S. pombe genome has one member of the PFA‐DSP family, SpSiw14, but it is more diverged from the ScSiw14 protein than is the Arabidopsis AtPFA‐DSP1 protein: a BLAST alignment between ScSiw14 (116‐270) and AtPFA‐DSP1 (53‐206) found 59% identities and 72% similarities whereas the BLAST alignment between ScSiw14 (98‐265) and SpSiw14 found 37% identities and 58% similarities. The critical active site residues are conserved: 188HCSRGKHR195, and mutation of C189S abrogates activity. Interestingly, the SpSiw14 enzyme recognizes and hydrolyzes a broader range of substrates in vitro. In addition to the β phosphate on the 5C of 5‐InsP7 and InsP8, it hydrolyzes the β phosphate on 1‐InsP7 as well as short‐chain polyP molecules [123].
PP‐InsP metabolism in vivo is affected in PFA‐DSP mutants of yeasts and plantsIn S. cerevisiae, siw14Δ deletion strains have elevated levels of 5‐InsP7 and InsP8, and a decrease in InsP6 [35, 36] and the heterologous expression of ScSIW14 in a mammalian cell line depleted the endogenous InsP7 pools [35]. In S. pombe, deletion of the SpSIW14 did not lead to alterations in endogenous PP‐InsP levels until the mutation was combined with a mutation in a gene encoding a second PP‐InsP phosphatase, the deletion of the DIPP family phosphatase aps1
+. The double mutant had a ninefold increase in 5‐InsP7 levels and no change in the 1‐InsP7 or InsP8 levels [77].
M. polymorpha produces the molecules 1‐InsP7, 4/6‐InsP7, 5‐InsP7 and InsP8 at levels comparable to Arabidopsis [7, 10, 29]. The MpPFA‐DSP1 gene was disrupted using CRISPR/Cas9 in plants [29]. In the Mppfa‐dsp1

ge
 line, the levels of 5‐InsP7 were significantly increased, InsP6 was significantly decreased, and there were no significant changes in the levels of 1‐InsP7, 4/6‐InsP7 or InsP8. Together with the in vitro biochemical data discussed above, these results support the role for MpPFA‐DSP1 as a bona fide 5β pyrophosphatase.Expression of the five Arabidopsis PFA‐DSP genes was examined in several adult plant tissues: root, stem, leaf, flower, and silique [114]. AtPFA‐DSP2 (At2g32960) was expressed at a constant level in all of the tissues examined; however, the other genes were expressed throughout the plant but showed variation and tissue‐specific expression patterns. Expression of each of the AtPFA‐DSP genes complemented the Scsiw14Δ mutant phenotype of elevated InsP7 levels and wortmannin sensitivity [115]. A T‐DNA insertion led to a 6‐fold overexpression of the AtPFA‐DSP1 gene compared with the wild‐type control and, concomitantly, a 36% reduction in InsP7 in 20‐day‐old seedlings [115]. High‐level expression of AtPFA‐DSP2 from the ubiquitin promoter led to depletion of 5‐InsP7 and InsP8 in seedlings [29]. Heterologous high‐level expression of AtPFA‐DSP1 but not the catalytically dead AtPFA‐DSP1C150S in tobacco leaves decreased the endogenous levels of 5‐InsP7 and InsP8 [115]. Loss‐of‐function mutant analysis has not been reported in Arabidopsis; although the multiple genes and overlapping expression patterns might make this analysis difficult. The in vivo results from fungi and plants confirm the in vitro activities in biochemical assays.Oxidation of the active site cysteine inhibits enzyme activityAn important feature of the PTP superfamily is the reversible oxidation of the catalytic cysteine. The pK
a of a noncatalytic cysteine is ~8.4; however, in the PTP superfamily, the pK
a of the catalytic cysteine is ~3 logs lower, in the range of 4–6 [120]. The low pK
a of the thiolate anion makes it more susceptible to oxidation and the sulfenic acid is incapable of acting as the nucleophile, rendering the oxidized enzyme inactive [124, 125]. This feature is also described in the PFA‐DSP family of proteins.The ScSiw14, AtPFA‐DSP1, and OsPFA‐DSP1 proteins lose catalytic activity when exposed to H2O2, and this is reversible upon treatment with the reducing agent dithiothreitol [113, 122, 126]. Whether or not this is an important in vivo mechanism for regulating enzyme activity needs additional work. Saccharomyces strains with mutations in the SIW14 and the OCA genes are necessary for the oxidative stress response (see more below) [126, 127, 128]. The ScOCA1 gene was discovered as being required for cell cycle arrest in response to linoleic acid hydroperoxide, a product of lipid oxidation [129], and the S. cerevisiae mutants siw14Δ, oca1Δ, and oca2Δ are all sensitive to growth on this compound [130].Pseudophosphatases are found in clades related to the PFA‐DSP cladeThe presence of protein orthologs that lack critical amino acids necessary for catalysis is also commonly found in the PTP superfamily [109, 112, 131, 132, 133]. In animals, pseudophosphatases play important cellular roles using several different regulatory mechanisms including competition for substrate binding, allosteric regulation of an active phosphatase, spatial anchoring of a heterologous binding partner, and signal integration through juxtaposition of protein motifs [109, 112, 131, 132, 133]. Many pseudophosphatases form heterodimers with the active phosphatase to modulate the activity [131, 133, 134].The PFA‐DSP family has a sister clade, the OCA clade, that shares overall sequence conservation and the CC1 structural fold; however, these proteins have substitutions or deletions in the critical active site residues [112, 114]. S. cerevisiae has SIW14 and four genes from the OCA clade. These proteins are predicted to be pseudophosphatases because they carry substitutions in the active site (active site residues are underlined, HCxxGxHR, from the discussion above, and substitutions of key sites are in bold): ScOca1,
CCGMGRHR; ScOca2, HS
NKGKHR; ScOca4, deletion of all of the active site residues, and ScOca6, HCTNGE
LI
. Recombinant ScOca1 lacks catalytic activity towards p‐nitrophenylphosphate (pNPP), 3‐O‐methyl fluorescein phosphate (OMFP) and PI(3,4,5,)P2 [114]. Using protein interaction assays (immunoprecipitation‐mass spectrometry and two‐hybrid), these proteins interact with each other, although the stoichiometry and membership of the complex(es) is uncertain [135, 136, 137, 138, 139, 140, 141, 142, 143].There is interesting and not fully explained gain and loss of the PFA‐DSP and OCA clades during evolution. Animals lack both the PFA‐DSP and OCA clades [114]. Both clades have fungal and protist members; however, they are not found in all fungi: S. pombe lacks genes from the OCA clade but Saccharomyces and closely related ascomycetes (e.g., Candida albicans) have genes from both clades [114]. Land plants and green algae lack orthologs in the OCA clade, suggesting gene loss; however, the early diverging bryophytes have three genes in the OCA clade, these are denoted as MpPFA‐DSP2 to MpPFA‐DSP4 [29] (note, the M. polymorpha genome was published in 2017 and so was not included in the earlier phylogenetic analysis [114]). Land plants have expanded the number of genes encoding catalytically active PFA‐DSP enzymes to 4 or 5 genes in Arabidopsis and rice [114, 122]. The roles for these OCA proteins have not been defined, but they may play a similar role as the animal pseudophosphatases to modulate activity from the catalytically active PFA‐DSP enzyme.Mutations in the genes encoding the PFA‐DSP and OCA enzymes lead to phenotypesIn the organisms in which it has been examined, there are phenotypes associated with mutations in the PFA‐DSP and OCA genes.FungiIn S. cerevisiae, mutations in SIW14 and the OCA genes (OCA1/2/4/6) lead to caffeine and rapamycin sensitivity [35, 114, 121, 127, 130, 144, 145, 146, 147]. Overexpression of SIW14 in strains carrying mutations in the oca1Δ, oca2Δ, oca4Δ, and oca6Δ genes suppressed the caffeine sensitivity [114]. Rapamycin is a strong inhibitor of the Tor1/2 kinases (TOR is target of rapamycin), and caffeine is a weak inhibitor of TOR, mimicking nutrient depletion [148, 149, 150]. Transcriptome analysis of the siw14Δ mutant found activation of transcription by factors in the TOR pathway, suggesting that PP‐InsPs molecules inhibit the TOR pathway [126, 151, 152]. The TOR pathway also regulates the pseudohyphal growth pathway [153], and strains with the siw14Δ deletion have an exaggerated pseudohyphal growth phenotype [154, 155].Caffeine sensitivity impacts other pathways in addition to the TOR pathway. Mutants of the cell wall integrity pathway (CWI) are sensitive to caffeine, and the ScSIW14 gene shows genetic interactions with the ScSLT2 gene, which encodes a MAP kinase in the CWI pathway [121]. Increased caffeine sensitivity is also associated with vesicle trafficking defects, and the siw14Δ mutant is defective in fluid‐phase exocytosis and shows genetic interactions with the Ark1 and Prk1 kinases associated with endocytosis [121]. Mutations in the yeast SIW14 and all four of the OCA genes suppress the cdc13‐1 temperature‐sensitive allele, implicating an involvement in telomere maintenance or DNA damage responses [156].In S. pombe, there is no effect on growth in either the Spsiw14Δ deletion or Spsiw14‐C189S point mutants, indicating that neither the protein itself nor its enzymatic activity is essential [123]. However, the Spsiw14Δ shows synthetic lethality when combined with a mutation in the Spaps1
+ gene; both gene products degrade InsP8 and the accumulation of this molecule is toxic [123]. Suppression of lethality occurs with mutations in the genes encoding the kinases SpKcs1 and SpAsp1 necessary to generation InsP8 or in the fission yeast 3′‐processing/termination machinery SpSwd22 and SpCft1, the nuclear poly(A)‐binding protein SpNab2, or the PP‐InsP sensor SpSpx1 [73, 77].
C. albicans, a human commensal and opportunistic pathogen, carries orthologs to the ScSIW14 and ScOCA genes, and some phenotypes associated with these have been examined. One study made mutations in 21 genes of C. albicans that encode putative phosphatases, and then screened these mutants for virulence in a silkworm model [157]; they found reduced virulence in the Casiw14Δ, Caoca1Δ, and Caoca6Δ mutants. Increased resistance to caspofungin (an antifungal echinocandin drug) was reported for the insertion of the transposon PB into the CaSIW14 gene in a haploid Candida strain [158]. A third study examined the formation of filamentous hyphae during polymicrobial infections with bacteria (Escherichia coli, Pseudomonas aeruginosa, and Staphylococcus aureus); bacteria inhibit the formation of hyphae but transposon insertions into the CaOCA1 gene allowed filamentation in the presence of bacteria [159].
Cryptococcus neoformans, a basidiomycetous pathogenic yeast, carries three genes in the PFA‐DSP and OCA families, CnSIW14, CnOCA1, and CnOCA101 (a paralog of Oca1) [160]. The C. neoformans mutants lacking CnSiw14, CnOca1, and CnOca101 were defective in virulence assays (a Galleria insect model and a murine inhalation model). The Cnsiw14Δ had increased melanin production and increased thermotolerance, two critical virulence traits [160]. The CnSiw14 protein is important for blood–brain barrier adhesion and crossing [160]. These data indicate the importance of PP‐InsPs in virulence in both Cryptococcus and C. albicans.Bryophytes and land plantsSome of the first experiments done on the PFA‐DSP enzymes in plants were performed in rice, and these were reported before the recognition of inositol pyrophosphates as substrates [122, 161]. The OsPFA‐DSP1 gene was expressed in seedlings, and the booting and dough stages of rice, and OsPFA‐DSP2 gene was expressed in calli, seedlings, roots, and panicles [122, 161]. OsPFA‐DSP1 expression was induced under drought and salt stress conditions and was downregulated during heat stress [122]. Overexpression of OsPFA‐DSP1 in rice and ectopic overexpression of it in tobacco increased drought sensitivity, possibly through enhanced water loss [122]. Overexpression of OsPFA‐DSP2 increased sensitivity to the fungal pathogen Magnaporthe grisea, consistent with decreased expression of pathogenesis‐related genes and lower H2O2 accumulation [161].In Arabidopsis, overexpression of AtPFA‐DSP1, AtPFA‐DSP2, or AtPFA‐DSP4 led to stunted growth, and the rosette surface area was decreased in two of three plant lines overexpressing AtPFA‐DSP2 [29]. Phosphate starvation conditions led to increased expression of the AtPFA‐DSP1, AtPFA‐DSP2, and AtPFA‐DSP4 genes [29]. Overexpression of AtPFA‐DSP4 (the ortholog of OsPFA‐DSP2) led to increased sensitivity to the pathogenic bacteria Pseudomonas syringae pv. tomato; in this case, there was reduced photosynthesis and lower accumulation of H2O2 [161].Liverwort plants with the mutation in the gene encoding PFA‐DSP1, line Mppfa‐dsp1

ge
, displayed decreased thallus surface area, increased mass of the rhizoid, and reduced numbers of gemmae cups [29]. The Mppfa‐dsp1

ge
 mutant accumulates less phosphate and shows increased expression of the Pi transporter under phosphate replete conditions, indicating that homeostasis was altered [29]. Expression of other genes was also affected, many of which are involved in the cell wall, and the composition of the cell wall was altered in the Mppfa‐dsp1

ge
 mutant [29]. Liverwort plants were generated with mutations in genes encoding MpPFA‐DSP1 and the phosphatase domain of MpVip1 (retaining the PPIP5K kinase domain); note that double mutants of either of these genes with Mpnudt1

ge
 were not recovered. This double mutant was discussed earlier in the section on MpVip1. It is not known if these phenotypes are due to the increase in 5‐InsP7 levels, the decrease in InsP6, or both.Summary of the cysteine phosphatase family and unanswered questionsThe cysteine phosphatase subfamily PFA‐DSP is present in some eukaryotes (plants, fungi, protists, and other basal species) but was lost in the eumetazoan clades, and this family was amplified in the genomes of land plants where they show tissue‐specific gene expression. These enzymes are specific for the β phosphate on 5C, and the enzymes have diverged greatly from the PTP superfamily in their catalytic mechanism. The active site cysteine displays reversible oxidation that inhibits its activity; a possible regulatory mechanism that needs further exploration. Pseudoenzymes related to the PFA‐DSP enzymes are found in many but not all basal eukaryotes—fungal, protist, and bryophytes—and were lost in plants as well as animals; a deeper examination of the phylogeny of gene loss and retention is warranted as well as more investigations into the physiological role(s) for the pseudoenzymes in the species that have them. Genetic loss of these enzymes leads to increased 5‐InsP7 levels, and phenotypes such as increased stress resistance and TOR inhibition, changes to the actin cytoskeleton, endocytosis defects, caspofungin resistance and reduced virulence, and stunted growth. Further work is needed to understand the molecular mechanisms associated with these traits.

The cysteine phosphatase superfamily

Cysteine phosphatases are part of a large superfamily of hydrolases with an active site cysteine that acts as a nucleophile (reviewed in [109, 110, 111]). The active site residues, HCxxGxxR (x stands for any amino acid), are found in proteins with three different folds indicating convergent evolution [112]. The Cys‐class 1 (CC1) fold includes the protein tyrosine phosphatase (PTP) superfamily that hydrolyzes the phosphate ester from phosphotyrosine (P‐Tyr) in proteins and peptides, the dual‐specificity protein tyrosine phosphatase (DSP or DUSP) subfamily that also hydrolyzes phosphoserine (P‐Ser) and phosphothreonine (P‐Thr) in addition to P‐Tyr, and a third subfamily, the atypical DSPs, that recognize a broader range of substrates including dephosphorylated nucleotide triphosphates, phosphorylated carbohydrates, and phosphatidylinositides [109, 112]. The focus of this review is on a subgroup within this latter family, the plant–fungal atypical dual‐specificity phosphatases (PFA‐DSP) subfamily. Enzymes in this PFA‐DSP family are capable of specifically cleaving the β phosphate in PP‐InsPs.

Enzymes of the PFA‐DSP family are found in plants, fungi, kinetoplastids, slime molds, choanoflagellates, and sponges, but they are not present in eumetazoan clades suggesting the genes encoding them were lost during evolution [29, 112, 113, 114, 115]. The PFA‐DSP family has been expanded in plants; for example, A. thaliana carries five homologs [114, 115].

Structures of the PFA‐DSP family members provided insights into critical differences from the PTP family and substrate specificityA pair of structural papers established the novel PFA‐DSP class of proteins (see Table 2). The first report was a low‐resolution structure of the Arabidopsis AtPFA‐DSP1 as an unusual member of the DSP1 family and a metal‐independent phosphatase [118], and the second examined conserved protein motifs and phylogeny [113]. Both noted the sequence conservation with the ScSiw14. Two structures of ScSiw14 were determined (Fig. 4); a 2.35 Å structure of the catalytic domain lacking the N terminus [116] and a pseudoatomic model of the full‐length ScSiw14 protein based on crystallography, secondary structure assays using circular dichroism, and modeling studies [117]. The full‐length structural model demonstrated that ScSiw14 is a zwitterionic protein with an N‐terminal domain of 108 amino acids that is composed of α‐helical bundles and is acidic in character (isoelectric point of 4.4), and a C‐terminal DSP domain of 165 amino acids that is basic in character (isoelectric point of 9.0) [117]. Each of these reports described the highly positively charged active site cleft and the lack of conservation of the Asp residue in the flexible WPD loop of canonical PTPs that performs acid–base catalysis.Table 2Structures of the S. cerevisiae Siw14 and A. thaliana PFA‐DSP1 proteins.ProteinOrganismPDB codeDescriptionReferences

From Wang et al., 2018 [116], http://creativecommons.org/licenses/by/4.0/.The active site residues in ScSiw14 are 213HCNRGKHR220. The first histidine is invariant in the PTP family, and ionic interactions between His213 and the adjacent Cys214 are critical to stabilize the thiolate anion [120]. Cys214 acts as the nucleophile to form thiophosphate, the enzyme‐substrate intermediate. Arg220 interacts with the PP‐InsP substrate and stabilizes the cysteine‐phosphate intermediate during catalysis [116, 117]. The requirement for this active site was confirmed by showing the loss of activity when the critical amino acids Cys214, Gly217, and Arg220 were mutated [35, 116, 121]. The catalytic cleft is highly basic, and substitutions of the Arg216 and Lys218 residues reduce enzyme efficiency [116]. An unexpected finding from the structural analysis of ScSiw14 is the absence of the canonical general acidic residue, usually an aspartate, that is found in the mobile WPD loop of PTPs; this residue protonates the leaving group and then activates the water to hydrolyze the cysteinyl‐phosphate intermediate. Glu185 lies within the flexible loop, and its substitution with alanine reduced enzymatic activity by 67%; this reduction was substantial but not as severe as the near‐complete loss of function observed in catalytic residue mutants such as Cys214 or Arg220 [116]. The issue was unresolved as one report postulated the lack of the requirement for this general acid, possibly due to hydrolysis of a phosphoanhydride bond as opposed to a phosphoester linkage [116], while a second report postulated that two glutamate residues, Glu120 and Glu185, may both be involved in enzymatic activity, possibly acting as an acidic dyad [117]. However, a recent report of a high‐resolution structure for the Arabidopsis AtPFA‐DSP1 enzyme with substrate has uncovered an important mechanistic role for a histidine residue equivalent to position His219 [119], a novel role that may hold true for the yeast enzyme (see below).The AtPFA‐DSP1 protein has the active site consensus sequence 149HCKRGKHR156; importantly, this amino acid sequence is identical in the five Arabidopsis orthologs (except for substitution of R152S in AtPFA‐DSP4, using the AtPFA‐DSP1 numbering), the four rice orthologs (OsPFA‐DSP1 to OsPFA‐DSP4), other plant orthologs, and, with the exception of the K151N substitution, it is identical in Saccharomyces and other ascomycetes [113, 119, 122]. Substitution of serine for cysteine (C150S) destroyed catalytic activity of AtPFA‐DSP1 [115, 119]. All five of the Arabidopsis AtPFA‐DSP enzymes exhibited robust in vitro activity with 5‐InsP7 and minor activity with 4‐InsP7 and 6‐InsP7 [29, 115, 119]. Two isoforms of InsP8 were examined, the canonical 1,5‐InsP8 as well as 3,5‐InsP8. ScSiw14 and the AtPFA‐DSP enzymes (except AtPFA‐DSP5) cleaved these substrates to either 1‐InsP7 or 3‐InsP7, demonstrating specificity for the 5β phosphate [115].The structure of AtPFA‐DSP1 identified several key enzyme features that may be important in this broader family of PFA‐DSP enzymes [119]. First, both 5‐InsP7 and 6‐InsP7 bound in virtually identical positions and made the same protein contacts [119]; this is important because plants generate 4/6‐InsP7 and AtPFA‐DSP1 could be involved in the hydrolysis of the β phosphate if it is 6‐InsP7. A second finding is that His155 acts as a ‘Janus’ residue: the imidazole ring flips 180° during its interactions with the substrate 5‐InsP7 relative to the product Pi after hydrolysis. A third observation is that a polarized water molecule interacts with His155 which activates it as the general base, an activity normally performed by the WPD loop, and suggesting that this histidine would be invariant [114, 119]. The rotation by His155 and its role as a general base underscores its importance as a conserved amino acid. Finally, while InsP6 is released upon hydrolysis, the second product, Pi, remains bound by the enzyme until it is displaced by a new PP‐InsP substrate in a ‘prisoner exchange’ mechanism [119]. Given the extensive sequence and structural conservation between AtPFA‐DSP1 and ScSiw14, it is possible that these enzymatic features are also found in the yeast protein.

Structures of the PFA‐DSP family members provided insights into critical differences from the PTP family and substrate specificity

A pair of structural papers established the novel PFA‐DSP class of proteins (see Table 2). The first report was a low‐resolution structure of the Arabidopsis AtPFA‐DSP1 as an unusual member of the DSP1 family and a metal‐independent phosphatase [118], and the second examined conserved protein motifs and phylogeny [113]. Both noted the sequence conservation with the ScSiw14. Two structures of ScSiw14 were determined (Fig. 4); a 2.35 Å structure of the catalytic domain lacking the N terminus [116] and a pseudoatomic model of the full‐length ScSiw14 protein based on crystallography, secondary structure assays using circular dichroism, and modeling studies [117]. The full‐length structural model demonstrated that ScSiw14 is a zwitterionic protein with an N‐terminal domain of 108 amino acids that is composed of α‐helical bundles and is acidic in character (isoelectric point of 4.4), and a C‐terminal DSP domain of 165 amino acids that is basic in character (isoelectric point of 9.0) [117]. Each of these reports described the highly positively charged active site cleft and the lack of conservation of the Asp residue in the flexible WPD loop of canonical PTPs that performs acid–base catalysis.
