Structures 7MOD to 7MOJ have a mutation of cysteine by serine (C150S) in complexes with inorganic phosphate, 5‐InsP7, 6‐InsP7, nonhydrolysable 5‐InsP7 analog, 5PP‐InsP4 and Pi, phenyl phosphate, and metaphosphate, respectively.

Structures 7MOK‐7MOM are the WT C150 with Pi in two conformations (A and B) and with metaphosphate, respectively.

Structures of ScSiw14 and AtPFA‐DSP1. (A) Structure of ScSiw14(116‐281) showing a citrate molecule in the active site and the PTP (or P‐) loop with the catalytic residues. (B) Superimposition of ScSiw14 (in color) over the AtPFA‐DSP1 protein (in gray), showing structural conservation. (C) Sequence alignment of the residues from ScSiw14 and AtPFA‐DSP1 (also known as At1g). (D) The electrostatic surface potential of ScSiw14(1‐281) on a pseudoatomic model; the knob at the left side with the basic region (in blue) is the catalytic core, residues 116‐281, and the N‐terminal extension to the right is acidic (in red). The charge scale is between −5 kT/e to +5 kT/e, from red to blue. (A‐C) from Wang et al. 2018 [116], http://creativecommons.org/licenses/by/4.0/; (D) from Florio et al., 2019 [117], used with permission.

A major advance came with the discovery that ScSiw14 is specific for the β phosphate on 5‐InsP7; it does not cleave the β phosphate on 1‐InsP7 nor any of the α phosphates [35]. A second study expanded the possible substrates by exploring the full range of PP‐InsPs (Fig. 5), confirming that the enzyme is specific for the pyrophosphate on 5C in three isoforms, 5‐InsP7, InsP8, and 5PP‐InsP4 [116].

Substrate specificity of Siw14. Each of the inositol polyphosphates was present at a concentration of 10 μm and incubated with purified Siw14 for 30 min. 5PP‐InsP4 is shorthand for 5‐diphosphoinositol‐1,3,4,6‐tetrakisphosphate; InsP6 lacks diphosphates at any position; 1‐ to 6‐InsP7 have the pyrophosphate on the indicated position; and InsP8 carries β phosphates at both the 1C and 5C positions. From Wang et al., 2018 [116], http://creativecommons.org/licenses/by/4.0/.

The active site residues in ScSiw14 are 213HCNRGKHR220. The first histidine is invariant in the PTP family, and ionic interactions between His213 and the adjacent Cys214 are critical to stabilize the thiolate anion [120]. Cys214 acts as the nucleophile to form thiophosphate, the enzyme‐substrate intermediate. Arg220 interacts with the PP‐InsP substrate and stabilizes the cysteine‐phosphate intermediate during catalysis [116, 117]. The requirement for this active site was confirmed by showing the loss of activity when the critical amino acids Cys214, Gly217, and Arg220 were mutated [35, 116, 121]. The catalytic cleft is highly basic, and substitutions of the Arg216 and Lys218 residues reduce enzyme efficiency [116]. An unexpected finding from the structural analysis of ScSiw14 is the absence of the canonical general acidic residue, usually an aspartate, that is found in the mobile WPD loop of PTPs; this residue protonates the leaving group and then activates the water to hydrolyze the cysteinyl‐phosphate intermediate. Glu185 lies within the flexible loop, and its substitution with alanine reduced enzymatic activity by 67%; this reduction was substantial but not as severe as the near‐complete loss of function observed in catalytic residue mutants such as Cys214 or Arg220 [116]. The issue was unresolved as one report postulated the lack of the requirement for this general acid, possibly due to hydrolysis of a phosphoanhydride bond as opposed to a phosphoester linkage [116], while a second report postulated that two glutamate residues, Glu120 and Glu185, may both be involved in enzymatic activity, possibly acting as an acidic dyad [117]. However, a recent report of a high‐resolution structure for the Arabidopsis AtPFA‐DSP1 enzyme with substrate has uncovered an important mechanistic role for a histidine residue equivalent to position His219 [119], a novel role that may hold true for the yeast enzyme (see below).

The AtPFA‐DSP1 protein has the active site consensus sequence 149HCKRGKHR156; importantly, this amino acid sequence is identical in the five Arabidopsis orthologs (except for substitution of R152S in AtPFA‐DSP4, using the AtPFA‐DSP1 numbering), the four rice orthologs (OsPFA‐DSP1 to OsPFA‐DSP4), other plant orthologs, and, with the exception of the K151N substitution, it is identical in Saccharomyces and other ascomycetes [113, 119, 122]. Substitution of serine for cysteine (C150S) destroyed catalytic activity of AtPFA‐DSP1 [115, 119]. All five of the Arabidopsis AtPFA‐DSP enzymes exhibited robust in vitro activity with 5‐InsP7 and minor activity with 4‐InsP7 and 6‐InsP7 [29, 115, 119]. Two isoforms of InsP8 were examined, the canonical 1,5‐InsP8 as well as 3,5‐InsP8. ScSiw14 and the AtPFA‐DSP enzymes (except AtPFA‐DSP5) cleaved these substrates to either 1‐InsP7 or 3‐InsP7, demonstrating specificity for the 5β phosphate [115].

The structure of AtPFA‐DSP1 identified several key enzyme features that may be important in this broader family of PFA‐DSP enzymes [119]. First, both 5‐InsP7 and 6‐InsP7 bound in virtually identical positions and made the same protein contacts [119]; this is important because plants generate 4/6‐InsP7 and AtPFA‐DSP1 could be involved in the hydrolysis of the β phosphate if it is 6‐InsP7. A second finding is that His155 acts as a ‘Janus’ residue: the imidazole ring flips 180° during its interactions with the substrate 5‐InsP7 relative to the product Pi after hydrolysis. A third observation is that a polarized water molecule interacts with His155 which activates it as the general base, an activity normally performed by the WPD loop, and suggesting that this histidine would be invariant [114, 119]. The rotation by His155 and its role as a general base underscores its importance as a conserved amino acid. Finally, while InsP6 is released upon hydrolysis, the second product, Pi, remains bound by the enzyme until it is displaced by a new PP‐InsP substrate in a ‘prisoner exchange’ mechanism [119]. Given the extensive sequence and structural conservation between AtPFA‐DSP1 and ScSiw14, it is possible that these enzymatic features are also found in the yeast protein.

Enzymes in the PFA‐DSP family from S. pombe and M. polymorpha hydrolyze the 5β phosphate in vitro
Studies have also been performed on orthologs from S. pombe and an early diverging plant, the bryophyte M. polymorpha (liverwort). Bryophytes carry one gene, MpPFA‐DSP1 (Mp3g10950), that is homologous to ScSIW14 and the five Arabidopsis PFA‐DSP genes [29]. MpPFA‐DSP1 has the conserved active site residues 104HCNKGKHR111, and it has the same substrate preference for the 5β phosphate as the yeast and plant enzymes, with highest activity with 5‐InsP7 and secondly for InsP8 [29]. Mutation of the catalytic cysteine (C105) to alanine eliminated enzyme activity in vitro [29].The S. pombe genome has one member of the PFA‐DSP family, SpSiw14, but it is more diverged from the ScSiw14 protein than is the Arabidopsis AtPFA‐DSP1 protein: a BLAST alignment between ScSiw14 (116‐270) and AtPFA‐DSP1 (53‐206) found 59% identities and 72% similarities whereas the BLAST alignment between ScSiw14 (98‐265) and SpSiw14 found 37% identities and 58% similarities. The critical active site residues are conserved: 188HCSRGKHR195, and mutation of C189S abrogates activity. Interestingly, the SpSiw14 enzyme recognizes and hydrolyzes a broader range of substrates in vitro. In addition to the β phosphate on the 5C of 5‐InsP7 and InsP8, it hydrolyzes the β phosphate on 1‐InsP7 as well as short‐chain polyP molecules [123].

Enzymes in the PFA‐DSP family from S. pombe and M. polymorpha hydrolyze the 5β phosphate in vitro

Studies have also been performed on orthologs from S. pombe and an early diverging plant, the bryophyte M. polymorpha (liverwort). Bryophytes carry one gene, MpPFA‐DSP1 (Mp3g10950), that is homologous to ScSIW14 and the five Arabidopsis PFA‐DSP genes [29]. MpPFA‐DSP1 has the conserved active site residues 104HCNKGKHR111, and it has the same substrate preference for the 5β phosphate as the yeast and plant enzymes, with highest activity with 5‐InsP7 and secondly for InsP8 [29]. Mutation of the catalytic cysteine (C105) to alanine eliminated enzyme activity in vitro [29].

The S. pombe genome has one member of the PFA‐DSP family, SpSiw14, but it is more diverged from the ScSiw14 protein than is the Arabidopsis AtPFA‐DSP1 protein: a BLAST alignment between ScSiw14 (116‐270) and AtPFA‐DSP1 (53‐206) found 59% identities and 72% similarities whereas the BLAST alignment between ScSiw14 (98‐265) and SpSiw14 found 37% identities and 58% similarities. The critical active site residues are conserved: 188HCSRGKHR195, and mutation of C189S abrogates activity. Interestingly, the SpSiw14 enzyme recognizes and hydrolyzes a broader range of substrates in vitro. In addition to the β phosphate on the 5C of 5‐InsP7 and InsP8, it hydrolyzes the β phosphate on 1‐InsP7 as well as short‐chain polyP molecules [123].

PP‐InsP metabolism in vivo is affected in PFA‐DSP mutants of yeasts and plantsIn S. cerevisiae, siw14Δ deletion strains have elevated levels of 5‐InsP7 and InsP8, and a decrease in InsP6 [35, 36] and the heterologous expression of ScSIW14 in a mammalian cell line depleted the endogenous InsP7 pools [35]. In S. pombe, deletion of the SpSIW14 did not lead to alterations in endogenous PP‐InsP levels until the mutation was combined with a mutation in a gene encoding a second PP‐InsP phosphatase, the deletion of the DIPP family phosphatase aps1
+. The double mutant had a ninefold increase in 5‐InsP7 levels and no change in the 1‐InsP7 or InsP8 levels [77].
M. polymorpha produces the molecules 1‐InsP7, 4/6‐InsP7, 5‐InsP7 and InsP8 at levels comparable to Arabidopsis [7, 10, 29]. The MpPFA‐DSP1 gene was disrupted using CRISPR/Cas9 in plants [29]. In the Mppfa‐dsp1

ge
 line, the levels of 5‐InsP7 were significantly increased, InsP6 was significantly decreased, and there were no significant changes in the levels of 1‐InsP7, 4/6‐InsP7 or InsP8. Together with the in vitro biochemical data discussed above, these results support the role for MpPFA‐DSP1 as a bona fide 5β pyrophosphatase.Expression of the five Arabidopsis PFA‐DSP genes was examined in several adult plant tissues: root, stem, leaf, flower, and silique [114]. AtPFA‐DSP2 (At2g32960) was expressed at a constant level in all of the tissues examined; however, the other genes were expressed throughout the plant but showed variation and tissue‐specific expression patterns. Expression of each of the AtPFA‐DSP genes complemented the Scsiw14Δ mutant phenotype of elevated InsP7 levels and wortmannin sensitivity [115]. A T‐DNA insertion led to a 6‐fold overexpression of the AtPFA‐DSP1 gene compared with the wild‐type control and, concomitantly, a 36% reduction in InsP7 in 20‐day‐old seedlings [115]. High‐level expression of AtPFA‐DSP2 from the ubiquitin promoter led to depletion of 5‐InsP7 and InsP8 in seedlings [29]. Heterologous high‐level expression of AtPFA‐DSP1 but not the catalytically dead AtPFA‐DSP1C150S in tobacco leaves decreased the endogenous levels of 5‐InsP7 and InsP8 [115]. Loss‐of‐function mutant analysis has not been reported in Arabidopsis; although the multiple genes and overlapping expression patterns might make this analysis difficult. The in vivo results from fungi and plants confirm the in vitro activities in biochemical assays.

PP‐InsP metabolism in vivo is affected in PFA‐DSP mutants of yeasts and plants

In S. cerevisiae, siw14Δ deletion strains have elevated levels of 5‐InsP7 and InsP8, and a decrease in InsP6 [35, 36] and the heterologous expression of ScSIW14 in a mammalian cell line depleted the endogenous InsP7 pools [35]. In S. pombe, deletion of the SpSIW14 did not lead to alterations in endogenous PP‐InsP levels until the mutation was combined with a mutation in a gene encoding a second PP‐InsP phosphatase, the deletion of the DIPP family phosphatase aps1
+. The double mutant had a ninefold increase in 5‐InsP7 levels and no change in the 1‐InsP7 or InsP8 levels [77].

M. polymorpha produces the molecules 1‐InsP7, 4/6‐InsP7, 5‐InsP7 and InsP8 at levels comparable to Arabidopsis [7, 10, 29]. The MpPFA‐DSP1 gene was disrupted using CRISPR/Cas9 in plants [29]. In the Mppfa‐dsp1

ge
 line, the levels of 5‐InsP7 were significantly increased, InsP6 was significantly decreased, and there were no significant changes in the levels of 1‐InsP7, 4/6‐InsP7 or InsP8. Together with the in vitro biochemical data discussed above, these results support the role for MpPFA‐DSP1 as a bona fide 5β pyrophosphatase.

Expression of the five Arabidopsis PFA‐DSP genes was examined in several adult plant tissues: root, stem, leaf, flower, and silique [114]. AtPFA‐DSP2 (At2g32960) was expressed at a constant level in all of the tissues examined; however, the other genes were expressed throughout the plant but showed variation and tissue‐specific expression patterns. Expression of each of the AtPFA‐DSP genes complemented the Scsiw14Δ mutant phenotype of elevated InsP7 levels and wortmannin sensitivity [115]. A T‐DNA insertion led to a 6‐fold overexpression of the AtPFA‐DSP1 gene compared with the wild‐type control and, concomitantly, a 36% reduction in InsP7 in 20‐day‐old seedlings [115]. High‐level expression of AtPFA‐DSP2 from the ubiquitin promoter led to depletion of 5‐InsP7 and InsP8 in seedlings [29]. Heterologous high‐level expression of AtPFA‐DSP1 but not the catalytically dead AtPFA‐DSP1C150S in tobacco leaves decreased the endogenous levels of 5‐InsP7 and InsP8 [115]. Loss‐of‐function mutant analysis has not been reported in Arabidopsis; although the multiple genes and overlapping expression patterns might make this analysis difficult. The in vivo results from fungi and plants confirm the in vitro activities in biochemical assays.

Oxidation of the active site cysteine inhibits enzyme activityAn important feature of the PTP superfamily is the reversible oxidation of the catalytic cysteine. The pK
a of a noncatalytic cysteine is ~8.4; however, in the PTP superfamily, the pK
a of the catalytic cysteine is ~3 logs lower, in the range of 4–6 [120]. The low pK
a of the thiolate anion makes it more susceptible to oxidation and the sulfenic acid is incapable of acting as the nucleophile, rendering the oxidized enzyme inactive [124, 125]. This feature is also described in the PFA‐DSP family of proteins.The ScSiw14, AtPFA‐DSP1, and OsPFA‐DSP1 proteins lose catalytic activity when exposed to H2O2, and this is reversible upon treatment with the reducing agent dithiothreitol [113, 122, 126]. Whether or not this is an important in vivo mechanism for regulating enzyme activity needs additional work. Saccharomyces strains with mutations in the SIW14 and the OCA genes are necessary for the oxidative stress response (see more below) [126, 127, 128]. The ScOCA1 gene was discovered as being required for cell cycle arrest in response to linoleic acid hydroperoxide, a product of lipid oxidation [129], and the S. cerevisiae mutants siw14Δ, oca1Δ, and oca2Δ are all sensitive to growth on this compound [130].

Oxidation of the active site cysteine inhibits enzyme activity

An important feature of the PTP superfamily is the reversible oxidation of the catalytic cysteine. The pK
a of a noncatalytic cysteine is ~8.4; however, in the PTP superfamily, the pK
a of the catalytic cysteine is ~3 logs lower, in the range of 4–6 [120]. The low pK
a of the thiolate anion makes it more susceptible to oxidation and the sulfenic acid is incapable of acting as the nucleophile, rendering the oxidized enzyme inactive [124, 125]. This feature is also described in the PFA‐DSP family of proteins.

The ScSiw14, AtPFA‐DSP1, and OsPFA‐DSP1 proteins lose catalytic activity when exposed to H2O2, and this is reversible upon treatment with the reducing agent dithiothreitol [113, 122, 126]. Whether or not this is an important in vivo mechanism for regulating enzyme activity needs additional work. Saccharomyces strains with mutations in the SIW14 and the OCA genes are necessary for the oxidative stress response (see more below) [126, 127, 128]. The ScOCA1 gene was discovered as being required for cell cycle arrest in response to linoleic acid hydroperoxide, a product of lipid oxidation [129], and the S. cerevisiae mutants siw14Δ, oca1Δ, and oca2Δ are all sensitive to growth on this compound [130].

Pseudophosphatases are found in clades related to the PFA‐DSP cladeThe presence of protein orthologs that lack critical amino acids necessary for catalysis is also commonly found in the PTP superfamily [109, 112, 131, 132, 133]. In animals, pseudophosphatases play important cellular roles using several different regulatory mechanisms including competition for substrate binding, allosteric regulation of an active phosphatase, spatial anchoring of a heterologous binding partner, and signal integration through juxtaposition of protein motifs [109, 112, 131, 132, 133]. Many pseudophosphatases form heterodimers with the active phosphatase to modulate the activity [131, 133, 134].The PFA‐DSP family has a sister clade, the OCA clade, that shares overall sequence conservation and the CC1 structural fold; however, these proteins have substitutions or deletions in the critical active site residues [112, 114]. S. cerevisiae has SIW14 and four genes from the OCA clade. These proteins are predicted to be pseudophosphatases because they carry substitutions in the active site (active site residues are underlined, HCxxGxHR, from the discussion above, and substitutions of key sites are in bold): ScOca1,
CCGMGRHR; ScOca2, HS
NKGKHR; ScOca4, deletion of all of the active site residues, and ScOca6, HCTNGE
LI
. Recombinant ScOca1 lacks catalytic activity towards p‐nitrophenylphosphate (pNPP), 3‐O‐methyl fluorescein phosphate (OMFP) and PI(3,4,5,)P2 [114]. Using protein interaction assays (immunoprecipitation‐mass spectrometry and two‐hybrid), these proteins interact with each other, although the stoichiometry and membership of the complex(es) is uncertain [135, 136, 137, 138, 139, 140, 141, 142, 143].There is interesting and not fully explained gain and loss of the PFA‐DSP and OCA clades during evolution. Animals lack both the PFA‐DSP and OCA clades [114]. Both clades have fungal and protist members; however, they are not found in all fungi: S. pombe lacks genes from the OCA clade but Saccharomyces and closely related ascomycetes (e.g., Candida albicans) have genes from both clades [114]. Land plants and green algae lack orthologs in the OCA clade, suggesting gene loss; however, the early diverging bryophytes have three genes in the OCA clade, these are denoted as MpPFA‐DSP2 to MpPFA‐DSP4 [29] (note, the M. polymorpha genome was published in 2017 and so was not included in the earlier phylogenetic analysis [114]). Land plants have expanded the number of genes encoding catalytically active PFA‐DSP enzymes to 4 or 5 genes in Arabidopsis and rice [114, 122]. The roles for these OCA proteins have not been defined, but they may play a similar role as the animal pseudophosphatases to modulate activity from the catalytically active PFA‐DSP enzyme.

Pseudophosphatases are found in clades related to the PFA‐DSP clade

The presence of protein orthologs that lack critical amino acids necessary for catalysis is also commonly found in the PTP superfamily [109, 112, 131, 132, 133]. In animals, pseudophosphatases play important cellular roles using several different regulatory mechanisms including competition for substrate binding, allosteric regulation of an active phosphatase, spatial anchoring of a heterologous binding partner, and signal integration through juxtaposition of protein motifs [109, 112, 131, 132, 133]. Many pseudophosphatases form heterodimers with the active phosphatase to modulate the activity [131, 133, 134].

The PFA‐DSP family has a sister clade, the OCA clade, that shares overall sequence conservation and the CC1 structural fold; however, these proteins have substitutions or deletions in the critical active site residues [112, 114]. S. cerevisiae has SIW14 and four genes from the OCA clade. These proteins are predicted to be pseudophosphatases because they carry substitutions in the active site (active site residues are underlined, HCxxGxHR, from the discussion above, and substitutions of key sites are in bold): ScOca1,
CCGMGRHR; ScOca2, HS
NKGKHR; ScOca4, deletion of all of the active site residues, and ScOca6, HCTNGE
LI
. Recombinant ScOca1 lacks catalytic activity towards p‐nitrophenylphosphate (pNPP), 3‐O‐methyl fluorescein phosphate (OMFP) and PI(3,4,5,)P2 [114]. Using protein interaction assays (immunoprecipitation‐mass spectrometry and two‐hybrid), these proteins interact with each other, although the stoichiometry and membership of the complex(es) is uncertain [135, 136, 137, 138, 139, 140, 141, 142, 143].

There is interesting and not fully explained gain and loss of the PFA‐DSP and OCA clades during evolution. Animals lack both the PFA‐DSP and OCA clades [114]. Both clades have fungal and protist members; however, they are not found in all fungi: S. pombe lacks genes from the OCA clade but Saccharomyces and closely related ascomycetes (e.g., Candida albicans) have genes from both clades [114]. Land plants and green algae lack orthologs in the OCA clade, suggesting gene loss; however, the early diverging bryophytes have three genes in the OCA clade, these are denoted as MpPFA‐DSP2 to MpPFA‐DSP4 [29] (note, the M. polymorpha genome was published in 2017 and so was not included in the earlier phylogenetic analysis [114]). Land plants have expanded the number of genes encoding catalytically active PFA‐DSP enzymes to 4 or 5 genes in Arabidopsis and rice [114, 122]. The roles for these OCA proteins have not been defined, but they may play a similar role as the animal pseudophosphatases to modulate activity from the catalytically active PFA‐DSP enzyme.

Mutations in the genes encoding the PFA‐DSP and OCA enzymes lead to phenotypesIn the organisms in which it has been examined, there are phenotypes associated with mutations in the PFA‐DSP and OCA genes.FungiIn S. cerevisiae, mutations in SIW14 and the OCA genes (OCA1/2/4/6) lead to caffeine and rapamycin sensitivity [35, 114, 121, 127, 130, 144, 145, 146, 147]. Overexpression of SIW14 in strains carrying mutations in the oca1Δ, oca2Δ, oca4Δ, and oca6Δ genes suppressed the caffeine sensitivity [114]. Rapamycin is a strong inhibitor of the Tor1/2 kinases (TOR is target of rapamycin), and caffeine is a weak inhibitor of TOR, mimicking nutrient depletion [148, 149, 150]. Transcriptome analysis of the siw14Δ mutant found activation of transcription by factors in the TOR pathway, suggesting that PP‐InsPs molecules inhibit the TOR pathway [126, 151, 152]. The TOR pathway also regulates the pseudohyphal growth pathway [153], and strains with the siw14Δ deletion have an exaggerated pseudohyphal growth phenotype [154, 155].Caffeine sensitivity impacts other pathways in addition to the TOR pathway. Mutants of the cell wall integrity pathway (CWI) are sensitive to caffeine, and the ScSIW14 gene shows genetic interactions with the ScSLT2 gene, which encodes a MAP kinase in the CWI pathway [121]. Increased caffeine sensitivity is also associated with vesicle trafficking defects, and the siw14Δ mutant is defective in fluid‐phase exocytosis and shows genetic interactions with the Ark1 and Prk1 kinases associated with endocytosis [121]. Mutations in the yeast SIW14 and all four of the OCA genes suppress the cdc13‐1 temperature‐sensitive allele, implicating an involvement in telomere maintenance or DNA damage responses [156].In S. pombe, there is no effect on growth in either the Spsiw14Δ deletion or Spsiw14‐C189S point mutants, indicating that neither the protein itself nor its enzymatic activity is essential [123]. However, the Spsiw14Δ shows synthetic lethality when combined with a mutation in the Spaps1
+ gene; both gene products degrade InsP8 and the accumulation of this molecule is toxic [123]. Suppression of lethality occurs with mutations in the genes encoding the kinases SpKcs1 and SpAsp1 necessary to generation InsP8 or in the fission yeast 3′‐processing/termination machinery SpSwd22 and SpCft1, the nuclear poly(A)‐binding protein SpNab2, or the PP‐InsP sensor SpSpx1 [73, 77].
C. albicans, a human commensal and opportunistic pathogen, carries orthologs to the ScSIW14 and ScOCA genes, and some phenotypes associated with these have been examined. One study made mutations in 21 genes of C. albicans that encode putative phosphatases, and then screened these mutants for virulence in a silkworm model [157]; they found reduced virulence in the Casiw14Δ, Caoca1Δ, and Caoca6Δ mutants. Increased resistance to caspofungin (an antifungal echinocandin drug) was reported for the insertion of the transposon PB into the CaSIW14 gene in a haploid Candida strain [158]. A third study examined the formation of filamentous hyphae during polymicrobial infections with bacteria (Escherichia coli, Pseudomonas aeruginosa, and Staphylococcus aureus); bacteria inhibit the formation of hyphae but transposon insertions into the CaOCA1 gene allowed filamentation in the presence of bacteria [159].
Cryptococcus neoformans, a basidiomycetous pathogenic yeast, carries three genes in the PFA‐DSP and OCA families, CnSIW14, CnOCA1, and CnOCA101 (a paralog of Oca1) [160]. The C. neoformans mutants lacking CnSiw14, CnOca1, and CnOca101 were defective in virulence assays (a Galleria insect model and a murine inhalation model). The Cnsiw14Δ had increased melanin production and increased thermotolerance, two critical virulence traits [160]. The CnSiw14 protein is important for blood–brain barrier adhesion and crossing [160]. These data indicate the importance of PP‐InsPs in virulence in both Cryptococcus and C. albicans.Bryophytes and land plantsSome of the first experiments done on the PFA‐DSP enzymes in plants were performed in rice, and these were reported before the recognition of inositol pyrophosphates as substrates [122, 161]. The OsPFA‐DSP1 gene was expressed in seedlings, and the booting and dough stages of rice, and OsPFA‐DSP2 gene was expressed in calli, seedlings, roots, and panicles [122, 161]. OsPFA‐DSP1 expression was induced under drought and salt stress conditions and was downregulated during heat stress [122]. Overexpression of OsPFA‐DSP1 in rice and ectopic overexpression of it in tobacco increased drought sensitivity, possibly through enhanced water loss [122]. Overexpression of OsPFA‐DSP2 increased sensitivity to the fungal pathogen Magnaporthe grisea, consistent with decreased expression of pathogenesis‐related genes and lower H2O2 accumulation [161].In Arabidopsis, overexpression of AtPFA‐DSP1, AtPFA‐DSP2, or AtPFA‐DSP4 led to stunted growth, and the rosette surface area was decreased in two of three plant lines overexpressing AtPFA‐DSP2 [29]. Phosphate starvation conditions led to increased expression of the AtPFA‐DSP1, AtPFA‐DSP2, and AtPFA‐DSP4 genes [29]. Overexpression of AtPFA‐DSP4 (the ortholog of OsPFA‐DSP2) led to increased sensitivity to the pathogenic bacteria Pseudomonas syringae pv. tomato; in this case, there was reduced photosynthesis and lower accumulation of H2O2 [161].Liverwort plants with the mutation in the gene encoding PFA‐DSP1, line Mppfa‐dsp1

ge
, displayed decreased thallus surface area, increased mass of the rhizoid, and reduced numbers of gemmae cups [29]. The Mppfa‐dsp1

ge
 mutant accumulates less phosphate and shows increased expression of the Pi transporter under phosphate replete conditions, indicating that homeostasis was altered [29]. Expression of other genes was also affected, many of which are involved in the cell wall, and the composition of the cell wall was altered in the Mppfa‐dsp1

ge
 mutant [29]. Liverwort plants were generated with mutations in genes encoding MpPFA‐DSP1 and the phosphatase domain of MpVip1 (retaining the PPIP5K kinase domain); note that double mutants of either of these genes with Mpnudt1

ge
 were not recovered. This double mutant was discussed earlier in the section on MpVip1. It is not known if these phenotypes are due to the increase in 5‐InsP7 levels, the decrease in InsP6, or both.

Mutations in the genes encoding the PFA‐DSP and OCA enzymes lead to phenotypes

In the organisms in which it has been examined, there are phenotypes associated with mutations in the PFA‐DSP and OCA genes.

FungiIn S. cerevisiae, mutations in SIW14 and the OCA genes (OCA1/2/4/6) lead to caffeine and rapamycin sensitivity [35, 114, 121, 127, 130, 144, 145, 146, 147]. Overexpression of SIW14 in strains carrying mutations in the oca1Δ, oca2Δ, oca4Δ, and oca6Δ genes suppressed the caffeine sensitivity [114]. Rapamycin is a strong inhibitor of the Tor1/2 kinases (TOR is target of rapamycin), and caffeine is a weak inhibitor of TOR, mimicking nutrient depletion [148, 149, 150]. Transcriptome analysis of the siw14Δ mutant found activation of transcription by factors in the TOR pathway, suggesting that PP‐InsPs molecules inhibit the TOR pathway [126, 151, 152]. The TOR pathway also regulates the pseudohyphal growth pathway [153], and strains with the siw14Δ deletion have an exaggerated pseudohyphal growth phenotype [154, 155].Caffeine sensitivity impacts other pathways in addition to the TOR pathway. Mutants of the cell wall integrity pathway (CWI) are sensitive to caffeine, and the ScSIW14 gene shows genetic interactions with the ScSLT2 gene, which encodes a MAP kinase in the CWI pathway [121]. Increased caffeine sensitivity is also associated with vesicle trafficking defects, and the siw14Δ mutant is defective in fluid‐phase exocytosis and shows genetic interactions with the Ark1 and Prk1 kinases associated with endocytosis [121]. Mutations in the yeast SIW14 and all four of the OCA genes suppress the cdc13‐1 temperature‐sensitive allele, implicating an involvement in telomere maintenance or DNA damage responses [156].In S. pombe, there is no effect on growth in either the Spsiw14Δ deletion or Spsiw14‐C189S point mutants, indicating that neither the protein itself nor its enzymatic activity is essential [123]. However, the Spsiw14Δ shows synthetic lethality when combined with a mutation in the Spaps1
+ gene; both gene products degrade InsP8 and the accumulation of this molecule is toxic [123]. Suppression of lethality occurs with mutations in the genes encoding the kinases SpKcs1 and SpAsp1 necessary to generation InsP8 or in the fission yeast 3′‐processing/termination machinery SpSwd22 and SpCft1, the nuclear poly(A)‐binding protein SpNab2, or the PP‐InsP sensor SpSpx1 [73, 77].
C. albicans, a human commensal and opportunistic pathogen, carries orthologs to the ScSIW14 and ScOCA genes, and some phenotypes associated with these have been examined. One study made mutations in 21 genes of C. albicans that encode putative phosphatases, and then screened these mutants for virulence in a silkworm model [157]; they found reduced virulence in the Casiw14Δ, Caoca1Δ, and Caoca6Δ mutants. Increased resistance to caspofungin (an antifungal echinocandin drug) was reported for the insertion of the transposon PB into the CaSIW14 gene in a haploid Candida strain [158]. A third study examined the formation of filamentous hyphae during polymicrobial infections with bacteria (Escherichia coli, Pseudomonas aeruginosa, and Staphylococcus aureus); bacteria inhibit the formation of hyphae but transposon insertions into the CaOCA1 gene allowed filamentation in the presence of bacteria [159].
Cryptococcus neoformans, a basidiomycetous pathogenic yeast, carries three genes in the PFA‐DSP and OCA families, CnSIW14, CnOCA1, and CnOCA101 (a paralog of Oca1) [160]. The C. neoformans mutants lacking CnSiw14, CnOca1, and CnOca101 were defective in virulence assays (a Galleria insect model and a murine inhalation model). The Cnsiw14Δ had increased melanin production and increased thermotolerance, two critical virulence traits [160]. The CnSiw14 protein is important for blood–brain barrier adhesion and crossing [160]. These data indicate the importance of PP‐InsPs in virulence in both Cryptococcus and C. albicans.

In S. cerevisiae, mutations in SIW14 and the OCA genes (OCA1/2/4/6) lead to caffeine and rapamycin sensitivity [35, 114, 121, 127, 130, 144, 145, 146, 147]. Overexpression of SIW14 in strains carrying mutations in the oca1Δ, oca2Δ, oca4Δ, and oca6Δ genes suppressed the caffeine sensitivity [114]. Rapamycin is a strong inhibitor of the Tor1/2 kinases (TOR is target of rapamycin), and caffeine is a weak inhibitor of TOR, mimicking nutrient depletion [148, 149, 150]. Transcriptome analysis of the siw14Δ mutant found activation of transcription by factors in the TOR pathway, suggesting that PP‐InsPs molecules inhibit the TOR pathway [126, 151, 152]. The TOR pathway also regulates the pseudohyphal growth pathway [153], and strains with the siw14Δ deletion have an exaggerated pseudohyphal growth phenotype [154, 155].

Caffeine sensitivity impacts other pathways in addition to the TOR pathway. Mutants of the cell wall integrity pathway (CWI) are sensitive to caffeine, and the ScSIW14 gene shows genetic interactions with the ScSLT2 gene, which encodes a MAP kinase in the CWI pathway [121]. Increased caffeine sensitivity is also associated with vesicle trafficking defects, and the siw14Δ mutant is defective in fluid‐phase exocytosis and shows genetic interactions with the Ark1 and Prk1 kinases associated with endocytosis [121]. Mutations in the yeast SIW14 and all four of the OCA genes suppress the cdc13‐1 temperature‐sensitive allele, implicating an involvement in telomere maintenance or DNA damage responses [156].

In S. pombe, there is no effect on growth in either the Spsiw14Δ deletion or Spsiw14‐C189S point mutants, indicating that neither the protein itself nor its enzymatic activity is essential [123]. However, the Spsiw14Δ shows synthetic lethality when combined with a mutation in the Spaps1
+ gene; both gene products degrade InsP8 and the accumulation of this molecule is toxic [123]. Suppression of lethality occurs with mutations in the genes encoding the kinases SpKcs1 and SpAsp1 necessary to generation InsP8 or in the fission yeast 3′‐processing/termination machinery SpSwd22 and SpCft1, the nuclear poly(A)‐binding protein SpNab2, or the PP‐InsP sensor SpSpx1 [73, 77].

C. albicans, a human commensal and opportunistic pathogen, carries orthologs to the ScSIW14 and ScOCA genes, and some phenotypes associated with these have been examined. One study made mutations in 21 genes of C. albicans that encode putative phosphatases, and then screened these mutants for virulence in a silkworm model [157]; they found reduced virulence in the Casiw14Δ, Caoca1Δ, and Caoca6Δ mutants. Increased resistance to caspofungin (an antifungal echinocandin drug) was reported for the insertion of the transposon PB into the CaSIW14 gene in a haploid Candida strain [158]. A third study examined the formation of filamentous hyphae during polymicrobial infections with bacteria (Escherichia coli, Pseudomonas aeruginosa, and Staphylococcus aureus); bacteria inhibit the formation of hyphae but transposon insertions into the CaOCA1 gene allowed filamentation in the presence of bacteria [159].

Cryptococcus neoformans, a basidiomycetous pathogenic yeast, carries three genes in the PFA‐DSP and OCA families, CnSIW14, CnOCA1, and CnOCA101 (a paralog of Oca1) [160]. The C. neoformans mutants lacking CnSiw14, CnOca1, and CnOca101 were defective in virulence assays (a Galleria insect model and a murine inhalation model). The Cnsiw14Δ had increased melanin production and increased thermotolerance, two critical virulence traits [160]. The CnSiw14 protein is important for blood–brain barrier adhesion and crossing [160]. These data indicate the importance of PP‐InsPs in virulence in both Cryptococcus and C. albicans.

Bryophytes and land plantsSome of the first experiments done on the PFA‐DSP enzymes in plants were performed in rice, and these were reported before the recognition of inositol pyrophosphates as substrates [122, 161]. The OsPFA‐DSP1 gene was expressed in seedlings, and the booting and dough stages of rice, and OsPFA‐DSP2 gene was expressed in calli, seedlings, roots, and panicles [122, 161]. OsPFA‐DSP1 expression was induced under drought and salt stress conditions and was downregulated during heat stress [122]. Overexpression of OsPFA‐DSP1 in rice and ectopic overexpression of it in tobacco increased drought sensitivity, possibly through enhanced water loss [122]. Overexpression of OsPFA‐DSP2 increased sensitivity to the fungal pathogen Magnaporthe grisea, consistent with decreased expression of pathogenesis‐related genes and lower H2O2 accumulation [161].In Arabidopsis, overexpression of AtPFA‐DSP1, AtPFA‐DSP2, or AtPFA‐DSP4 led to stunted growth, and the rosette surface area was decreased in two of three plant lines overexpressing AtPFA‐DSP2 [29]. Phosphate starvation conditions led to increased expression of the AtPFA‐DSP1, AtPFA‐DSP2, and AtPFA‐DSP4 genes [29]. Overexpression of AtPFA‐DSP4 (the ortholog of OsPFA‐DSP2) led to increased sensitivity to the pathogenic bacteria Pseudomonas syringae pv. tomato; in this case, there was reduced photosynthesis and lower accumulation of H2O2 [161].Liverwort plants with the mutation in the gene encoding PFA‐DSP1, line Mppfa‐dsp1

ge
, displayed decreased thallus surface area, increased mass of the rhizoid, and reduced numbers of gemmae cups [29]. The Mppfa‐dsp1

ge
 mutant accumulates less phosphate and shows increased expression of the Pi transporter under phosphate replete conditions, indicating that homeostasis was altered [29]. Expression of other genes was also affected, many of which are involved in the cell wall, and the composition of the cell wall was altered in the Mppfa‐dsp1

ge
 mutant [29]. Liverwort plants were generated with mutations in genes encoding MpPFA‐DSP1 and the phosphatase domain of MpVip1 (retaining the PPIP5K kinase domain); note that double mutants of either of these genes with Mpnudt1

ge
 were not recovered. This double mutant was discussed earlier in the section on MpVip1. It is not known if these phenotypes are due to the increase in 5‐InsP7 levels, the decrease in InsP6, or both.

Bryophytes and land plants

Some of the first experiments done on the PFA‐DSP enzymes in plants were performed in rice, and these were reported before the recognition of inositol pyrophosphates as substrates [122, 161]. The OsPFA‐DSP1 gene was expressed in seedlings, and the booting and dough stages of rice, and OsPFA‐DSP2 gene was expressed in calli, seedlings, roots, and panicles [122, 161]. OsPFA‐DSP1 expression was induced under drought and salt stress conditions and was downregulated during heat stress [122]. Overexpression of OsPFA‐DSP1 in rice and ectopic overexpression of it in tobacco increased drought sensitivity, possibly through enhanced water loss [122]. Overexpression of OsPFA‐DSP2 increased sensitivity to the fungal pathogen Magnaporthe grisea, consistent with decreased expression of pathogenesis‐related genes and lower H2O2 accumulation [161].

In Arabidopsis, overexpression of AtPFA‐DSP1, AtPFA‐DSP2, or AtPFA‐DSP4 led to stunted growth, and the rosette surface area was decreased in two of three plant lines overexpressing AtPFA‐DSP2 [29]. Phosphate starvation conditions led to increased expression of the AtPFA‐DSP1, AtPFA‐DSP2, and AtPFA‐DSP4 genes [29]. Overexpression of AtPFA‐DSP4 (the ortholog of OsPFA‐DSP2) led to increased sensitivity to the pathogenic bacteria Pseudomonas syringae pv. tomato; in this case, there was reduced photosynthesis and lower accumulation of H2O2 [161].

Liverwort plants with the mutation in the gene encoding PFA‐DSP1, line Mppfa‐dsp1

ge
, displayed decreased thallus surface area, increased mass of the rhizoid, and reduced numbers of gemmae cups [29]. The Mppfa‐dsp1

ge
 mutant accumulates less phosphate and shows increased expression of the Pi transporter under phosphate replete conditions, indicating that homeostasis was altered [29]. Expression of other genes was also affected, many of which are involved in the cell wall, and the composition of the cell wall was altered in the Mppfa‐dsp1

ge
 mutant [29]. Liverwort plants were generated with mutations in genes encoding MpPFA‐DSP1 and the phosphatase domain of MpVip1 (retaining the PPIP5K kinase domain); note that double mutants of either of these genes with Mpnudt1

ge
 were not recovered. This double mutant was discussed earlier in the section on MpVip1. It is not known if these phenotypes are due to the increase in 5‐InsP7 levels, the decrease in InsP6, or both.

Summary of the cysteine phosphatase family and unanswered questionsThe cysteine phosphatase subfamily PFA‐DSP is present in some eukaryotes (plants, fungi, protists, and other basal species) but was lost in the eumetazoan clades, and this family was amplified in the genomes of land plants where they show tissue‐specific gene expression. These enzymes are specific for the β phosphate on 5C, and the enzymes have diverged greatly from the PTP superfamily in their catalytic mechanism. The active site cysteine displays reversible oxidation that inhibits its activity; a possible regulatory mechanism that needs further exploration. Pseudoenzymes related to the PFA‐DSP enzymes are found in many but not all basal eukaryotes—fungal, protist, and bryophytes—and were lost in plants as well as animals; a deeper examination of the phylogeny of gene loss and retention is warranted as well as more investigations into the physiological role(s) for the pseudoenzymes in the species that have them. Genetic loss of these enzymes leads to increased 5‐InsP7 levels, and phenotypes such as increased stress resistance and TOR inhibition, changes to the actin cytoskeleton, endocytosis defects, caspofungin resistance and reduced virulence, and stunted growth. Further work is needed to understand the molecular mechanisms associated with these traits.

Summary of the cysteine phosphatase family and unanswered questions

The cysteine phosphatase subfamily PFA‐DSP is present in some eukaryotes (plants, fungi, protists, and other basal species) but was lost in the eumetazoan clades, and this family was amplified in the genomes of land plants where they show tissue‐specific gene expression. These enzymes are specific for the β phosphate on 5C, and the enzymes have diverged greatly from the PTP superfamily in their catalytic mechanism. The active site cysteine displays reversible oxidation that inhibits its activity; a possible regulatory mechanism that needs further exploration. Pseudoenzymes related to the PFA‐DSP enzymes are found in many but not all basal eukaryotes—fungal, protist, and bryophytes—and were lost in plants as well as animals; a deeper examination of the phylogeny of gene loss and retention is warranted as well as more investigations into the physiological role(s) for the pseudoenzymes in the species that have them. Genetic loss of these enzymes leads to increased 5‐InsP7 levels, and phenotypes such as increased stress resistance and TOR inhibition, changes to the actin cytoskeleton, endocytosis defects, caspofungin resistance and reduced virulence, and stunted growth. Further work is needed to understand the molecular mechanisms associated with these traits.

The Nudix superfamilyThe structure and biochemical characterization of the Nudix suprafamily of enzymes have been reviewed in detail in several excellent articles [162, 163, 164, 165]; here I will summarize key points. The Nudix superfamily (PF00293) belongs to the protein clan CL0261 that includes 4 additional superfamilies of enzymes: Nudix‐like (PF09296), Nudix‐2 (PF14815), Nudix‐4 (PF13869), and DBC1 (PF14443) [165]. A recent paper focused on fungi extends this clan to include additional families (PF03559, PF11788, PF15916, PF16262, PF16705, and PF18290) [166]. The Nudix superfamily was named based on the substrate structure described as nucleoside diphosphate linked to another moiety X (NDP‐X), with the Escherichia coli MutT as the founding member. These enzymes are metal‐requiring hydrolases that act upon a wide range of substrates including nucleotide substrates (such as oxidized nucleotides, nucleotide sugars, diadenosine polyphosphates, dinucleotide coenzymes, and capped RNAs) and non‐nucleotide substrates (such as inositol pyrophosphates, 5‐phosphoribosyl 1‐pyrophosphate, and thiamine pyrophosphate). Members of this superfamily have a ~130 amino acid β‐grasp domain [167] and catalysis depends upon the ~23‐amino acid Nudix box with the general sequence Gx5Ex7REUxEExGU, in which U stands for an aliphatic amino acid (Ile, Leu, or Val), X for any amino acid and the key glutamic acid residues in bold [162, 163]. The Nudix motif forms a loop–helix–loop structure that interacts with substrates and with Mg2+, Mn2+ or other divalent cations to activate water to break the covalent bond. As discussed below, enzyme interaction with different cations affects substrate preference.The number of Nudix genes per genome varies across eukaryotes but increases with genome size [163]. S. cerevisiae has 6 Nudix genes (Npy1, Pcd1, Dcp2, Ysa1, YJR142w, and Ddp1), humans have 24, and the plant Arabidopsis thaliana have 28 [168, 169]. A comprehensive analysis of all of the human Nudix proteins with respect to structure, substrate preference and effects on cell cycle was reported by Carreras‐Puigvert and colleagues [170].The DIPP subfamily of Nudix enzymes cleaves multiple substratesThe Nudix superfamily includes the diphosphoinositol polyphosphate phosphohydrolase (DIPP) subfamily. The DIPP subfamily is found across eukaryotes including vertebrates, invertebrates, fungi, and plants but is not found in protists, indicating a later evolution after the divergence of the major domains of life [165, 166]. The DIPP enzymes exhibit substrate ambiguity, meaning they have activity toward substrates that are related to each other but catalysis occurs with differences in efficiency [164]. They have been characterized to hydrolyze several substrates in vitro and some of these have been verified by effects in vivo. Based on kinetic measurements in vitro (Table 3), cleavage of the β phosphate from PP‐InsPs (Fig. 1) is the most physiologically compelling of the investigated substrates. However, in vitro and in vivo data demonstrate strong cases for their activity as endopolyphosphatases on inorganic polyP, which is cleaved more rapidly than 1‐InsP7 [175], and on diadenosine polyphosphates (e.g., Ap6P, Ap5A). Weaker cases are currently made for the hydrolysis of 5‐phosphoribosyl‐1‐pyrophosphate (PRPP) and the 7‐methyguanosine (m7G) cap of mRNAs (Fig. 6; reviewed in [164, 176]). What links each of these substrates is that they have phosphoanhydride bonds, either as the β phosphate in a pyrophosphate group or part of a longer chain of phosphates. Inositol pyrophosphates have been discussed above, and these additional substrates will be briefly described before focusing on recent reports of enzyme structures and substrate recognition.Table 3Kinetic data for ScDdp1 and HsDIPP1.Substrate

These molecules are alternative substrates for the DIPP family of enzymes.PolyP is a linear molecule with three to hundreds of inorganic phosphates connected by energy‐rich phosphoanhydride linkages that is involved in many important cellular roles (reviewed in [177, 178, 179, 180, 181]). PolyP is found in many subcellular compartments including nuclei, plasma membrane, mitochondria, and the cytosol [182]. It is found in very high levels in the acidocalcisomes of protists and mammalian mast cells, the dense granules of platelets, and the vacuoles of fungi serving as a phosphate reservoir (reviewed in [49, 66, 183, 184, 185]). Mammalian cells have substantially lower levels of polyP (25–120 μm) than are found in yeast (estimates from whole cells are ~230 mm and a recent determination found the vacuolar concentration to be ~30 mm) [66, 182, 186, 187]. Plant cells do not synthesize polyP, but instead store inorganic phosphate in vacuoles [185, 188, 189]. PolyP interacts with proteins as a scaffold and chaperone, promotes condensate formation, and can modify proteins post‐translationally (reviewed in [177, 180]). In fungal cells, polyP plays roles in cell cycle progression, DNA repair and genome stability, energy homoeostasis, oxidative stress responses, and virulence [62, 190, 191, 192]. In mammalian cells, induction of polyP synthesis led to its accumulation in the nucleolus, increased liquid–liquid phase separation and altered rRNA processing [193, 194]. PolyP plays important roles in blood coagulation, the inflammatory response, bone formation, neurotransmission and regulation of ion channels [177, 180, 181].Diadenosine polyphosphates (Ap
n
A) are generated in response to environmental and genotoxic stresses (reviewed in [195, 196]). These molecules have the 5'C of adenosine connected by three to six phosphates to the 5'C of a second adenosine. Originally thought to be damaged molecules, these molecules have been proposed to act as intracellular alarmones [195]. Degradation is necessary to regulate the abundance of these signaling molecules, and hydrolysis depends on phosphatases of either the DIPP subfamily or the histidine‐triad (HIT) family of diadenosine polyphosphate phosphatases. The DIPP enzymes interact with Ap6A or Ap5A to generate p4A and ADP or p4A and AMP, respectively, as the major products of hydrolysis [170, 172, 173, 197]. Ap6A and Ap5A are allosteric activators of eukaryotic IMP dehydrogenases, which regulate guanine nucleotide levels [198, 199], and they are potent inhibitors of nucleotide and protein kinases as well as other enzymes [172, 195], affecting metabolic and signaling pathways. In mammalian systems, diadenosine polyphosphates are also found in secretory vesicles of neurons and secretory cells, activating P2Y purinergic receptors upon their release, and they play major roles in the vascular system, affecting platelet cells and endothelial cells and acting as vasoconstrictors [200, 201, 202].The phosphorylated sugar 5‐phosphoribosyl‐1‐pyrophosphate (PRPP) is a substrate for the synthesis of purine, pyrimidine and pyridine nucleotides, and in the biosynthesis of histidine and tryptophan in fungi and plants. In vitro, the DIPP enzymes cleave the β phosphate from PRPP to generate the product ribose‐1,5‐bisphosphate (Rib‐1,5‐P2; [174]). In animals, Rib‐1,5‐P2 is an activator of glycolysis by stimulating the enzymatic activity of phosphofructokinase in brain, muscle, liver, and immune cells [203, 204, 205, 206]. The levels of both PRPP and Rib‐1,5‐P2 rapidly and transiently increase ~7‐fold during hypoxia; activity from a PRPP pyrophosphatase was detected but the identity of the protein was not determined [203]. Whether or not PRPP is a bone fide substrate for the DIPP enzymes has been a point of discussion since the initial observation [164, 176, 207, 208]. Enzyme kinetics argue against it: including an alkaline pH optimum, a high K
m for the PRPP substrate, and PRPP as a poor competitor relative to 5‐InsP7 [172, 203, 209]; however, no other enzyme with pyrophosphatase activities outside of the DIPP enzymes have been described to act upon these molecules.Eukaryotic mRNAs are modified at their 5′‐ends by the 7‐methylguanosine (m7G) cap that is important for translation initiation and mRNA stability, and at their 3′‐ends by the poly‐A tail that is critical for stability, nuclear export and translation. Degradation of mRNAs involves either progressive truncation of the poly‐A tail by exonucleases or cleavage of the cap (decapping) at the 5′ end. Decapping by Dcp2, a protein conserved across eukaryotes, is a regulated process linking the 5′‐3′ degradation to multiple decay pathways (reviewed in [210, 211, 212]). The yeast Ddp1 and mammalian DIPP1 proteins were shown to have robust activity in vitro with capped RNAs [213], suggesting a possible in vivo role which has not been deeply investigated.The structural studies provide insights into substrate recognition and catalysisThe structures of several of the DIPP enzymes have been determined by crystallization and X‐ray diffraction: human DIPP1 [214], entries in the Protein Database for human DIPP2 and DIPP3α and mouse DIPP2α, and the human DIPP1 in complex with 1‐ and 5‐InsP7 [17]. In 2021, two reports expanded our structural understanding of these enzymes. One is of the human DIPP1 enzyme in complex with natural PP‐InsPs, alternative PP‐InsP stereoisomers, and nonhydrolysable analogs focusing on pre‐ and postreaction states [215]. The second is a detailed structural study of the yeast ScDdp1 protein in complex with multiple natural substrates and nonhydrolysable substrate analogs for inositides, diadenosine polyphosphates, and polyP to determine the structures important for substrate recognition and catalysis [197].Both the ScDdp1 and HsDIPP1 enzymes have a broad, open V‐shaped cavity with a ridge made by residues having long flexible side chains able to accommodate inositides, nucleotides and polyP (top views in Figs 7B and 8A, and sideview in Fig. 7C) [215]. The yeast Ddp1 has an insertion of 26 amino acids, called the nose (see Fig. 8A) that is not found in the human DIPP enzymes [197]; the position of this insertion is referred to as the X‐loop [215]. Immediately N‐terminal to the Nudix motif is Lys63 in Ddp1 whereas it is Gly20 in DIPP1; although not conserved, both residues are critical for enzyme activity or thermal stability [197, 216]. Importantly, Glu80 and Glu84, which are critical for catalysis [162, 163], each interact with Mg2+ ions that position the β phosphate in the correct geometry for hydrolysis. These substrate‐ and Mg2+‐ contacts are conserved in the structures of the human DIPP1 enzyme when in complex with 1‐ and 5‐InsP7, with only some minor differences due to amino acid divergence.Fig. 7Structure of HsDIPP1 with 5‐InsP7.
