Figures are from [197], http://creativecommons.org/licenses/by/4.0/.Phosphates from all substrates make the same contacts with the yeast enzyme—Arg32, Ser52, Ser53, Lys63, Gly65, Arg102, Arg129, Arg152, and Arg171 (Fig. 8B)—and these positions anchor the substrates, following a defined path in the base of the cavity (Fig. 8A). The substrate polyP makes four additional contacts (His55, Lys57, Arg58, and Lys170) and Ap5A makes one unique contact (Asp100) [197]. The human DIPP1 protein makes the same contacts to the PP‐InsP substrates with some small differences: HsDIPP1 makes substrate contacts with Arg10, Arg20, and His90, which are not conserved amino acids. The key take‐away messages from these studies are that (1) these enzymes—which diverged about a billion years ago—retained the critical amino acids and overall core structure, and (2) these very different substrates are all recognized by the same conserved amino acids, and structural flexibility explains substrate ambiguity.Zong and colleagues examined the activity of Ddp1 with the canonical PP‐InsP substrates (1‐InsP7, 5‐InsP7, InsP8, and 5PP‐InsP4 [2‐OH]) as well as alternative PP‐InsP stereoisomers (2‐InsP7, 3‐InsP7, 4‐InsP7, and 6‐InsP7) [215]. The Vmax was highest for InsP8, and 1‐InsP7 had a ~ 2‐fold higher activity than 5‐InsP7, in agreement with previous studies [171]. The activity with 5PP‐InsP4 and 6‐InsP7 was the same as 5‐InsP7, and activity with the other stereoisomers also had significant but lower activity. These findings are important because some of these molecules have been detected in nature—4/6‐InsP7 in mouse, Dictyostelium discoideum, Arabidopsis thaliana, and Cryptococcus neoformans [7, 8, 10, 13, 217] and 2‐InsP7 from mouse colon, peripheral blood mononuclear cells, and heart [13]—and the proteins for their metabolism are under investigation.As opposed to the substrates Ap5P and polyP, the PP‐InsPs can bind the active site in different ways due to the symmetry of the phosphorylated myo‐inositol ring. The substrate 1‐InsP7 binds in only one orientation whereas both 5‐InsP7 and InsP8 bind in two ways—productive and nonproductive [197]. Kilari and colleagues [171] investigated the kinetic properties of the human and yeast DIPP enzymes, finding that 1‐InsP7 was hydrolyzed five‐ to sixfold faster than 5‐InsP7 or InsP8, even though the K
m values were about the same (see Table 3). Márguez‐Moñino and colleagues noted that their structural data showing nonproductive binding by 5‐InsP7 and InsP8 could explain the kinetic data. When InsP8 is the substrate, the preferred binding mode will position the C5 β phosphate in the productive mode; cleavage of this pyrophosphate group releases 1‐InsP7 that is rapidly hydrolyzed to InsP6. These data are in agreement with the order of cleavage as determined previously [171, 175].Mutations in the genes encoding the DIPP enzymes lead to phenotypes associated with the different substratesThis section will review what is known about mutant phenotypes associated with the mutations in the gene(s) for the DIPP enzymes from representative taxa and will consider each of the substrates when information is known.FungiFungi have one member of the DIPP subfamily in their genomes [166, 218]; DDP1 in S. cerevisiae and aps1
+ in S. pombe have been studied in detail. The ScDdp1 and SpAps1 enzymes have multiple substrates, but the best substrates for each are the PP‐InsPs. These enzymes hydrolyze the β phosphate of 5‐InsP7 to InsP6 and both β phosphates of InsP8 to InsP6 [172, 175]. SpAps1 has similar kinetics to ScDdp1 (Table 3), with a ~3‐fold faster k
cat and a twofold preference for 1‐InsP7 [43, 172]. There was a ~7‐fold increase in InsP7 abundance in the ddp1Δ strain, although the isoforms were not distinguished [35]. In S. pombe, there was a ~3‐fold increase in the InsP7 pools in the aps1Δ mutant [219], and the more recent study found that the aps1Δ strain accumulates about twice the level of 1‐InsP7 as the wild‐type strain [43]. The aps1Δ is synthetically lethal with nonfunctional alleles of asp1
+ (the bifunctional kinase‐histidine phosphate discussed above) due to an inability to metabolize the PP‐InsPs, leading to a toxic build‐up of 1‐InsP7 and InsP8 [43, 72, 77]. Together, these data indicate that ScDdp1 and SpAps1 play important cellular roles in determining the steady state levels of inositol pyrophosphates.The diadenosine polyphosphates have been shown to be in vitro substrates for the yeast enzymes. Ap6A is an better substrate than Ap5A for ScDdp1 and SpAps1 [173, 220]. The K
ms for substrate Ap5A with the SpAps1 and ScDdp1 were reported to be 22 μm and 70 μM, respectively [173, 220]; both groups used the same method to assay enzyme activity. However, in a follow‐up study from the Barnes group, they used a different method to quantify the product formed by SpAps1, and reported a ~7‐fold lower K
m to be 2.84 μm [218]; it is possible that the kinetic parameters for the ScDdp1 enzyme would be equally affected by the more sensitive assay. However, even with this change, the K
m for Ap5A remains at least 100‐fold higher than for the PP‐InsPs substrates. In S. pombe, the intracellular concentration of Ap5A is present at 0.06 pmol/mg of protein, approx. 4 nm, which is well below the K
m [219]. Although the deletion of Spaps1
+ decreased hydrolase activity, it did not affect the levels of Ap5A [219]. In S. cerevisiae during late sporulation, p4A levels increase [221]; as noted above, this molecule is the product of the reaction of Ap5‐6A, but a role for ScDdp1 in sporulation or germination has not been described. Although there is in vitro activity with Ap6A and Ap5A, these would be relevant substrates in vivo only if their intracellular levels increase substantially.Polyphosphates have been shown to be important intracellular substrates for the yeast DIPP enzymes. The purified recombinant ScDdp1 enzyme cleaved polyP as an endopolyphosphatase [175, 222]. Deletion of Scddp1 led to increased polyP accumulation whereas the overexpression of DDP1 in S. cerevisiae led to a decrease in acid‐soluble (short‐chain) and acid‐insoluble (long‐chain) polyP by 9% and 28%, respectively [65, 223]. The combined activities of ScDdp1 and ScPpx1, the cytosolic exopolyphosphatase, are both required to overcome the toxic effects of polyP accumulation in the cytosol when bacterial polyphosphate kinase is overexpressed in S. cerevisiae [224]. In S. pombe, the aps1Δ mutant has a constitutive phosphate starvation response [225, 226] and polyP accumulation was higher in the aps1Δ mutant [73]. These observations are consistent with the ScDdp1 and SpAps1 enzymes playing key roles in the turnover of polyP.Work on the other possible substrates has not been as thorough. There are no reports on PRPP as a substrate nor of the effect of mutations on flux through glycolysis. In vitro decapping activity was shown for ScDdp1 and mouse DIPP1 (see below; [213]), and the mutation of the mammalian DIPP1 impacted mRNA levels of specific messages. Overexpression of Spaps1
+ decreased cell growth and led to swollen, rounded, and multiseptated cells but paradoxically increased the levels of Ap5A [219]; this phenotype was attributed to effects on cell wall biosynthesis.Plant–fungal pathogens secrete a variety of molecules (effectors) that impact the host's immune responses to enhance their virulence [227, 228, 229]. Magnaporthe and Colletotrichum fungi are responsible for plant diseases causing blast on grain crops (e.g., rice, wheat, barley) and anthracnose on the leaves and shoots of plants of agricultural and horticultural importance (e.g., tea, peas, lentils, trees), respectively [230, 231]. An important effector from plant–fungal pathogens is a member of the DIPP family [232] and its mechanism of action was recently described [233]. Expression of the Colletotrichum CtNUDIX gene increased in the late biotrophic phase in planta [232], prior to the switch to the necrotic phase. Deletion of the NUDIX gene (or mutating the catalytic residues) decreased fungal pathogenesis, and its overexpression killed host cells during the biotrophic phase of infection but did not lead to pathogenesis [232, 233]. The Magnaporthe MoNUDIX protein has in vitro enzymatic activity with 5‐InsP7 but not with other substrates [233]. The effectors from Magnaporthe and Colletotrichum had robust in vitro hydrolytic activity toward 4‐InsP7, 5‐InsP7, and 6‐InsP7, and minor activity with 1‐InsP7 [234]. The MoNUDIX protein localized to the fungal biotrophic interfacial complex (BIC), the secretion structure for transport into plant cells, and mRFP‐tagged MoNUDIX was found in rice leaf cells [233]. The NUDIX effector was predicted to affect the phosphate starvation response by decreasing InsP8. When inorganic phosphate is abundant, InsP8 binds to the SPX regulatory protein to inhibit the transcriptional factor PHR1 to induce gene expression of target genes encoding high affinity Pi transporters, ubiquitin ligases, and hormone receptors (reviewed in [235]). The MoNUDIX effector stimulated the phosphate starvation response in the plants [233]. The plant's immune responses and signaling were also disrupted, although the full mechanism(s) for this are incompletely understood [231, 236].InvertebratesArthropod genomes carry one gene encoding the DIPP enzyme (319 arthropod species, NCBI search on 7/12/2025; [78]), whereas animal and plant genomes encode many more (see below), suggesting that functional specification evolved later. In Drosophila melanogaster, the Aps gene shows alternative transcriptional start and stop sites and differential splicing to generate three different mRNAs; however, each of these mRNAs produces the same protein [237]. The gene was cloned, recombinant protein was expressed and characterized [238]. The Aps enzyme prefers PP‐InsP5 with a K
m of 70 nm and a k
cat of 0.024·s−1 and it also hydrolyzed several different (di)nucleoside polyphosphates, with guanosine 5′‐tetraphosphate as the most efficient (K
m, 11 μm; kcat, 0.79·s−1). Aps expression was found in cells throughout the fly, and both the levels of PP‐InsP5 and gene expression were highest in embryos and declined throughout development. As a part of a larger study examining the mammalian Nudt3 protein and its effects on obesity (see below), expression from the Drosophila Aps gene was knocked down in the nervous system and specifically in a subset of neurosecretory cells that produce insulin [239]. The mutant flies had an inability to recruit lipids and carbohydrates during starvation, and exhibited a hyperinsulinemia‐like phenotype. These results indicate there is a role for the DmAps in the regulation of insulin signaling; however, the cellular mechanisms and critical substrate(s) for this regulation remain uncharacterized.MammalsHumans have four genes in the DIPP subfamily of Nudix hydrolases producing five proteins [170]. Gene NUDT3 (6p21) encodes the 172 amino acid protein DIPP1 [240]. The NUDT4 gene (12q22) generates two mRNAs through alternative splicing, encoding the 180 amino acid protein DIPP2α and the 181 amino acid protein DIPP2β with an insertion of Gln86 [241]. Genes NUDT10 and NUDT11 are adjacent, convergently transcribed genes that arose by gene duplication on the X chromosome (Xp11), and they encode proteins DIPP3α and DIPP3β, respectively, that differ by one amino acid [242, 243]. They have evolved to have different substrate preferences and expression patterns.DIPP1 is cytosolic and has a K
m for PP‐InsPs in the nanomolar range (Table 3), indicating that these molecules are the preferred substrates; the K
ms for Ap6A and Ap5A are ~6–8 μm, and they could be substrates if cytosolic concentrations increase. DIPP1 has been implicated in the decapping of mRNAs in vitro and in vivo [213, 244], indicating that mRNA caps are important substrates (more below). DIPP1 enzyme activity with particular substrates depends upon the cations bound: PP‐InsPs are the preferred substrates with Mg2+ [171], Ap5‐6A and mRNA caps require Mn2+ [172, 244, 245], and polyP is preferred with Zn2+ [246]. Mg2+ is at ~1 mm but other cations are sequestered at low levels. The in vivo implications are that the availability of the cations might vary or be regulated, and this could then influence substrate selection. In the case of Zn2+, oxidative stress induced with hydrogen peroxide or menadione leads to an increase in free Zn2+ and a decrease in cellular polyP [246]. Secondly, changes to substrate availability affect the competition for the enzyme in vivo, as was shown for a competition between PP‐InsPs and m7G caps [245]. In vitro assays showed that 5‐InsP7 inhibited decapping (IC50 of ~200 nm); in vivo, increasing the levels of 5‐InsP7 by 2–3 fold (through genetic manipulation by deleting the HsPPIP5K, see above) led to increases in levels of specific mRNAs and P‐body abundance [245]. Thus, changes to the steady state levels of cations, PP‐InsP substrates, and competing substrates will have broad effects.
NUDT3 has been linked to cancer, diabetes and obesity, and oxidative stress responses in humans and animals. Expression of the NUDT3 gene is found broadly in many tissues [170, 240]. There is higher expression in the mammalian brain, particularly in the hypothalamus, amygdala and striatum that are important for regulating food intake and behavior [239, 246]. The mouse Nudt3 gene is up‐regulated in the brain and liver during food deprivation [239]. Multiple genome‐wide association studies for obesity in humans have identified single‐nucleotide polymorphisms (SNPs) linked to NUDT3 [247, 248, 249, 250]. The porcine NUDT3 gene is also linked to lipid accumulation, adipocyte proliferation and pig fatness trait [251]. An siRNA knockdown of NUDT3 in cell lines decreased viability when oxidative stress or DNA damage were imposed [191, 246]. NUDT3 shows increased expression in tumors of the lungs, colon, uterus, and gall bladder and lower expression in a cancer of skeletal muscle [170, 252]. Silencing of NUDT3 in MCF‐7 cells led to the alterations in mRNA levels of 161 genes (144 are up, 17 down) of twofold or more, with increased levels of specific genes associated with cell motility, likely through decreased decapping [244]. Knocking out the two NUDT3 genes from zebrafish was lethal [246]. Determining what substrate(s) and mechanisms are important for these DIPP enzymes in human and animal health are important issues.Human NUDT4 is an ortholog of NUDT3. The ORFs from both genes are in five exons; the splice junctions are conserved, and they produce proteins of 173 and 167 amino acids (respectively) that are 74% identical—the majority of the protein differences are in the C‐terminal 31 or 25 amino acids. NUDT4 is highly expressed across a wide range of tissues; interestingly, it was downregulated in all cancers examined [170]. NUDT4 has two transcription start sites (TSS), alternative splicing, and multiple polyadenylation sites to generate at least thirteen transcripts. The second downstream TSS generates mRNAs with a different exon 1, and translation initiation occurs at an internal methionine within the Nudix motif (Fig. 4B, Met53 in the HsDIPP2‐5LTU structure). Assuming the proteins are stable, the predicted proteins of 128 and 129 amino acids would lack critical residues for substrate binding. Alternative splicing adds one amino acid in DIPP2β, Gln86, which is positioned at the end of β5 (Fig. 8B), and this insertion negatively impacts enzyme activity. PP‐InsPs are the preferred substrates: the kinetics of DIPP2α for 5‐InsP7 (K
m of 4.2 nm, k
cat of 0.15·s−1) is nearly the same as for DIPP1 (Table 3), but the DIPP2β is not as efficient (K
m of 4.8 nm, k
cat of 0.03·s−1) [241]. Neither 2α nor 2β is as active with InsP8 as DIPP1 by 3.5‐ to 10‐fold (the first‐order rate constant k

−1
 was determined as 320·s−1 for DIPP1, 90·s−1 for 2α, and 32·s−1 for 2β [241]). Considering polyP as a substrate, the GST‐DIPP2 fusion protein had ~25% of the activity as compared with GST‐DIPP1 [246]. The 2α and 2β enzymes have little activity with Ap6A (34 U and 18 U, respectively, vs. 310 U for DIPP1; 1 U = 1 pmol·min−1·μg−1) or Ap5A < 10 U and 26 U, respectively, ~1/10th the activity of DIPP1 (320 U) [241]. Neither protein cleaved the m7G‐cap [213]. Thus, the paradox is that although all of the critical contact positions for substrates and magnesium are conserved in DIPP1 and DIPP2α (Fig. 8B), the DIPP1 and 2α/2β enzymes only have PP‐InsPs as a common substrate. There are critical differences between the proteins that are yet to be uncovered. These observations are consistent with the idea that critical functions have been retained in NUDT3 following gene duplication, permitting silencing or subfunctionalization to occur on NUDT4 [253, 254].The human genes NUDT10 and NUDT11 produce DIPP3α (aka, hAps2) and DIPP3β (hAps1), respectively. They are found on the X chromosome, subjected to X‐inactivation, convergently transcribed, and likely the result of a gene duplication [242, 243]. Their genetic structure of NUDT10 and NUDT11 is different from NUDT3 and NUDT4 in that there are only two exons, with the first exon encoding the entire uninterrupted ORF; this observation suggests that one of these genes is a retrogene that was then duplicated. The mouse genes Nudt10 and Nudt11 encode identical proteins; however, the two human genes differ at one codon: 3α carries Pro89, a substitution of the critical Arg89 that is found in 3β (equivalent to Arg129 in Fig. 8B). The proteins from these genes are nearly identical to the DIPP2β isoform, including the distinctive Gln86 insertion [242, 243]. Given that these genes encode proteins nearly identical to 2β, their substrate preferences and kinetics are the similar: (1) 3β was ~2.3‐fold more active than 3α, likely due to Pro89; (2) hydrolysis of 5‐InsP7 is preferred over Ap5‐6A by 100‐200‐fold; and (3) 3β has similar activity as 2β (only 1.4‐fold lower activity with 5‐InsP7) [242].In mouse, Nudt10 expression is highest in liver, kidney, and testis and Nudt11 is expressed in the brain [209]. In humans, expression of NUDT10 and NUDT11 is highest in testis [242]. Several SNPs associated with prostate cancer are linked to Xp11.22 where these genes are found [255, 256]. Expression of NUDT10 and NUDT11 was significantly reduced in two testicular cancers (testicular germ cell tumors, mesothelioma), but interestingly, silencing of expression is lethal in three other cancer cell lines: (A549 lung, MCF7 breast, and SW480 colon cancer) [170]. Thus, although there is more limited substrate selection as compared with DIPP1, narrowing which cells express the gene may be a type of neofunctionalization.Bryophytes and vascular plantsPlants generate a broader set of PP‐InsPs as compared with yeast and mammals: in addition to the canonical 1‐InsP7 and 5‐InsP7, they produce 4/6‐InsP7 and possibly 3‐InsP7 [7, 10, 29], and in some reports 4/6‐InsP7 is the major isoform that is more abundant than the ‘canonical’ isoforms [7, 10]. As noted earlier, plants do not synthesize polyP and instead store inorganic phosphate in vacuoles [185, 188, 189], and they do not generate Ap5‐6A [257, 258]. Thus, in contrast to fungi and animals, these molecules would not be expected to be substrates in vivo.Two recent reports [29, 259] examined these enzymes in the liverwort M. polymorpha. Liverworts carry one DIPP gene in their genome (Mp5g06600), named DDP1 and NUDT1 by the two groups [29, 259]. Expression of the wild‐type MpDDP1 gene (but not a mutated Mpddp1) in yeast fully complemented the Scddp1Δ mutant for hydrogen peroxide sensitivity and reduced the elevated InsP7 levels to wild‐type [259]. In vitro assays using purified recombinant MpNUDT1 uncovered robust activity with 1‐InsP7 as the substrate (757 nmol min−1 mg−1), minor activity with 5‐InsP7 (12 nmol·min−1·mg−1), and no activity with InsP8 [29]. Both groups knocked out the MpDDP1/MpNUDT1 gene using CRISPR/Cas9 and found similar effects on PP‐InsP pools in vivo: the mutant plants accumulated 1‐InsP7 and InsP8, but there was no effect on the levels of 5‐InsP7 or 4/6‐InsP7 [29, 259]. The effects on the 1‐InsP7 pools are consistent with the in vitro assays; however, the increase in the InsP8 pools in vivo suggests that this molecule could also be a substrate in vitro. These data provide in vivo and in vitro evidence that the MpDDP1/MpNUDT1 gene encodes a bone fide DIPP hydrolase with specificity to a subset of PP‐InsPs.The Mpnudt1 mutant plants, with higher than wild‐type levels of InsP8, have significantly lower levels of intracellular inorganic phosphate, consistent with the regulation of phosphate homeostasis by InsP8 [29]. Interestingly, the MpNUDT1 gene itself is regulated by phosphate, repressed during phosphate limitation, and genes for the other hydrolases show constitutive expression [29]. One group reported that the Mpddp1/Mpnudt1 null plants had an enlarged thallus, early bifurcation of the thallus lobe, early emergence of the gemmae cup, and greater numbers of mature gemmae than the wild‐type plant [259]; however, the second group reported reduced thallus area and no change to the number of gemmae per plant [29]. The Mpnudt

ge
 plants accumulated more nitrate and less phosphate than control, indicating a disruption in the homeostasis mechanisms [29].The Arabidopsis genome carries seven genes predicted to be in the DIPP subfamily [168, 169]. The predicted DIPP subfamily proteins are AtNUDT4 (locus At1g18300), AtNUDT12 (At1g12880), AtNUDT13 (At3g26690), AtNUDT16 (At3g12600), AtNUDT17 (At2g01670), AtNUDT18 (At1g14860), and AtNUDT21 (At1g73540). AtNUDT16 produces two different transcripts that are translated differently at the N‐termini: AtNUDT16.1 and AtNUDT16.2 (Note: the NUDT numbering in plants is not the same as in mammals; that is, they are not the closest ortholog). Phylogenetic analysis places these genes in a clade with ScDDP1, HsNUDT4, and MpDDP1 [29, 234, 259], with evidence for two subclades (see below) with neofunctionalization [234]. AtNUDT4 is expressed in roots, stems and leaves [260], and the AtNUDT12/13/16/17/21 genes are widely expressed in rosette leaves, stems, cauline leaves, inflorescence and roots, and AtNUDT18 showing no expression in rosette leaves and strongest expression in inflorescence [261].These putative DIPP enzymes were examined for activity on PP‐InsPs and other substrates. The Gillaspy group focused on AtNUDT13 because it has the strongest sequence homology with ScDdp1 in BLAST analyses [262]. Using purified GST‐AtNUDT13, they found in vitro hydrolysis with InsP7, InsP8, polyP, and Ap5A as substrates; these results differed from a prior study that reported activity with Ap6A and Ap5A, and no activity with 5‐InsP7 or PRPP [263]. An alternative approach was used by the Hothorn and Schaaf groups to identify DIPP enzymes able to bind to 5PCP‐InsP5, a nonhydrolysable analog of 5‐InsP7 [264], in pull‐down assays, followed by mass spectrometry to identify the proteins [29, 234]. The Hothorn group identified AtNUDT17, AtNUDT18, and AtNUDT21 proteins from 2‐week‐old seedlings [29]. Recombinant AtNUDT17 was purified and in vitro assays were performed with PP‐InsPs; the highest activity occurred with 5‐InsP7 (42 nmol·min−1·mg−1), minimal activity with 1‐InsP7 (2 nmol·min−1·mg−1), and no activity with InsP8 [29]. The Schaaf group found AtNUDT4, AtNUDT17, and AtNUDT21 proteins in shoot extracts, and AtNUDT16 and AtNUDT18 in root extracts [234]. They examined all seven of the potential DIPP enzymes by complementing the S. cerevisiae DDP1 mutant and examining InsP7 levels (which are higher in the Scddp1Δ mutant); each of the AtNUDT genes, except AtNUDT4 and AtNUDT18, significantly reduced the levels of InsP7, and AtNUDT16.1 was the strongest, reducing InsP7 to the level of the wild‐type ScDDP1 [234]. Using in vitro assays and purified recombinant MBP‐tagged proteins, the ability to hydrolyze a suite of chemically synthesized enantiomers of InsP7 with the diphosphate at each carbon of the ring was assessed [234]. Subclade I (AtNUDT4, AtNUDT17, AtNUDT18, and AtNUDT21) hydrolyzed 4‐InsP7 and AtNUDT18 also cleaved 6‐InsP7; none of the enzymes in subclade I showed significant activity with 5‐InsP7. This result—the lack of 5‐InsP7 cleavage by AtNUDT17—differs from [29] in which they found strong cleavage. Subclade II (AtNUDT12, AtNUDT13, AtNUDT16.1, and AtNUDT16.2) enzymes had robust hydrolase activity with 3‐InsP7 and against the 3β phosphate in the less common InsP8 isoform 3,5‐InsP8 (aka, 3,5PP2‐InsP4). These in vitro results broaden the understanding of which isoforms of InsP7 are substrates for these enzymes and suggest that divergence in the DIPP family has occurred since the split between land plants from fungi and animals [265].To examine in vivo effects, transgenic Arabidopsis plants were generated that overexpressed (OE) either the AtNUDT13 or ScDDP1 genes [262]. These OE lines had decreases in the InsP7 and InsP8 pools relative to wild‐type controls, and conversely, in a plant line with a T‐DNA insertion into AtNUDT13 (mutant allele nudt13‐1), InsP8 and InsP7 accumulated at 1.28× and 2.07× wild‐type levels, respectively [262]. In the second study, Arabidopsis plants were generated lacking three DIPP genes in a triple Atnudt17/18/21 null mutant and overexpressing the AtNUDT17 genes under control of a strong promoter [29]. The triple null mutant had elevated levels of InsP6, 1‐InsP7 and InsP8., and the OE‐AtNUDT17 plants had nearly undetectable levels of InsP8 [29]. In the third study, mutant plant lines were generated lacking subclade I (the Atnudt4/17/18/21 quadruple mutant) or lacking subclade II (the Atnudt12/13/16 triple mutant) [234]. The two subclade II triple mutant lines accumulated 1/3‐InsP7 and 5‐InsP7 whereas none of the three subclade I quadruple mutants were different than control plants [234]. Transient high expression of each of these genes, except AtNUDT13, in tobacco leaves reduced 4/6‐InsP7 and 5‐InsP7 levels [234]. Although there are some differences, these reports showed that manipulating these genes in planta affected levels of PP‐InsPs: loss‐of‐function mutations increased levels whereas the OE lines led to a decrease in their levels.The mutations that depleted the InsP8 molecule activated the phosphate starvation response (PSR) and induced the plants to accumulate inorganic phosphate [29, 234, 262]. The subclade II triple mutant (Atnudt12/13/16) lines also accumulated less iron in shoots relative to wild‐type plants [234]. Interestingly, the AtNUDT13, AtNUDT16, AtNUDT17, AtNUDT18, and AtNUDT21 genes are up‐regulated in response to phosphate limitation in shoots [29, 234]. The overexpression of AtNUDT17 led to reduced rosette areas and other changes to plant growth [29, 262]. The subclade II triple mutant affected expression of genes outside of the PSR, including genes for defenses, responses to reactive oxygen and oxidative stress, wounding, et al., implicating 3‐InsP7 in these roles [234].Summary of the Nudix phosphatase family and unanswered questionsThe DIPP subfamily in the Nudix superfamily is not found in protists but is represented in fungi, plants, invertebrates, and animals. The key feature of this phosphatase family is that it recognizes multiple isoforms of PP‐InsPs (1‐InsP7, 5‐InsP7, 4/6‐InsP7, and InsP8) and other substrates; the different substrates make the same contacts with the enzyme as PP‐InsPs, demonstrating recognition of common substrate features. Therefore, competition between substrates would occur. Studies with the human DIPP1 enzyme showed altered substrate activity depending on what cation was bound (Mg2+, Mn2+, Zn2+) to the enzyme, suggesting another mechanism to alter substrate selection. In fungi, these enzymes affect PP‐InsP and polyphosphate metabolism. Plant–fungal pathogens secrete a member of this family into plant cells to affect phosphate starvation, signaling and immune responses. An interesting phenotype in metazoans (Drosophila, animals, humans) is a connection between the DIPP family and nutrient sensing, obesity, and cancer. In liverworts, there is a link between the single gene and a disruption in nitrate and phosphate homeostasis; however, gene amplification in land plants will make teasing apart effects more challenging. Given the different substrates and competition between them as well as possible cation implications on substrate selection, determination of direct vs. indirect phenotypes.

The Nudix superfamily

The structure and biochemical characterization of the Nudix suprafamily of enzymes have been reviewed in detail in several excellent articles [162, 163, 164, 165]; here I will summarize key points. The Nudix superfamily (PF00293) belongs to the protein clan CL0261 that includes 4 additional superfamilies of enzymes: Nudix‐like (PF09296), Nudix‐2 (PF14815), Nudix‐4 (PF13869), and DBC1 (PF14443) [165]. A recent paper focused on fungi extends this clan to include additional families (PF03559, PF11788, PF15916, PF16262, PF16705, and PF18290) [166]. The Nudix superfamily was named based on the substrate structure described as nucleoside diphosphate linked to another moiety X (NDP‐X), with the Escherichia coli MutT as the founding member. These enzymes are metal‐requiring hydrolases that act upon a wide range of substrates including nucleotide substrates (such as oxidized nucleotides, nucleotide sugars, diadenosine polyphosphates, dinucleotide coenzymes, and capped RNAs) and non‐nucleotide substrates (such as inositol pyrophosphates, 5‐phosphoribosyl 1‐pyrophosphate, and thiamine pyrophosphate). Members of this superfamily have a ~130 amino acid β‐grasp domain [167] and catalysis depends upon the ~23‐amino acid Nudix box with the general sequence Gx5Ex7REUxEExGU, in which U stands for an aliphatic amino acid (Ile, Leu, or Val), X for any amino acid and the key glutamic acid residues in bold [162, 163]. The Nudix motif forms a loop–helix–loop structure that interacts with substrates and with Mg2+, Mn2+ or other divalent cations to activate water to break the covalent bond. As discussed below, enzyme interaction with different cations affects substrate preference.

The number of Nudix genes per genome varies across eukaryotes but increases with genome size [163]. S. cerevisiae has 6 Nudix genes (Npy1, Pcd1, Dcp2, Ysa1, YJR142w, and Ddp1), humans have 24, and the plant Arabidopsis thaliana have 28 [168, 169]. A comprehensive analysis of all of the human Nudix proteins with respect to structure, substrate preference and effects on cell cycle was reported by Carreras‐Puigvert and colleagues [170].

The DIPP subfamily of Nudix enzymes cleaves multiple substratesThe Nudix superfamily includes the diphosphoinositol polyphosphate phosphohydrolase (DIPP) subfamily. The DIPP subfamily is found across eukaryotes including vertebrates, invertebrates, fungi, and plants but is not found in protists, indicating a later evolution after the divergence of the major domains of life [165, 166]. The DIPP enzymes exhibit substrate ambiguity, meaning they have activity toward substrates that are related to each other but catalysis occurs with differences in efficiency [164]. They have been characterized to hydrolyze several substrates in vitro and some of these have been verified by effects in vivo. Based on kinetic measurements in vitro (Table 3), cleavage of the β phosphate from PP‐InsPs (Fig. 1) is the most physiologically compelling of the investigated substrates. However, in vitro and in vivo data demonstrate strong cases for their activity as endopolyphosphatases on inorganic polyP, which is cleaved more rapidly than 1‐InsP7 [175], and on diadenosine polyphosphates (e.g., Ap6P, Ap5A). Weaker cases are currently made for the hydrolysis of 5‐phosphoribosyl‐1‐pyrophosphate (PRPP) and the 7‐methyguanosine (m7G) cap of mRNAs (Fig. 6; reviewed in [164, 176]). What links each of these substrates is that they have phosphoanhydride bonds, either as the β phosphate in a pyrophosphate group or part of a longer chain of phosphates. Inositol pyrophosphates have been discussed above, and these additional substrates will be briefly described before focusing on recent reports of enzyme structures and substrate recognition.Table 3Kinetic data for ScDdp1 and HsDIPP1.Substrate

These molecules are alternative substrates for the DIPP family of enzymes.PolyP is a linear molecule with three to hundreds of inorganic phosphates connected by energy‐rich phosphoanhydride linkages that is involved in many important cellular roles (reviewed in [177, 178, 179, 180, 181]). PolyP is found in many subcellular compartments including nuclei, plasma membrane, mitochondria, and the cytosol [182]. It is found in very high levels in the acidocalcisomes of protists and mammalian mast cells, the dense granules of platelets, and the vacuoles of fungi serving as a phosphate reservoir (reviewed in [49, 66, 183, 184, 185]). Mammalian cells have substantially lower levels of polyP (25–120 μm) than are found in yeast (estimates from whole cells are ~230 mm and a recent determination found the vacuolar concentration to be ~30 mm) [66, 182, 186, 187]. Plant cells do not synthesize polyP, but instead store inorganic phosphate in vacuoles [185, 188, 189]. PolyP interacts with proteins as a scaffold and chaperone, promotes condensate formation, and can modify proteins post‐translationally (reviewed in [177, 180]). In fungal cells, polyP plays roles in cell cycle progression, DNA repair and genome stability, energy homoeostasis, oxidative stress responses, and virulence [62, 190, 191, 192]. In mammalian cells, induction of polyP synthesis led to its accumulation in the nucleolus, increased liquid–liquid phase separation and altered rRNA processing [193, 194]. PolyP plays important roles in blood coagulation, the inflammatory response, bone formation, neurotransmission and regulation of ion channels [177, 180, 181].Diadenosine polyphosphates (Ap
n
A) are generated in response to environmental and genotoxic stresses (reviewed in [195, 196]). These molecules have the 5'C of adenosine connected by three to six phosphates to the 5'C of a second adenosine. Originally thought to be damaged molecules, these molecules have been proposed to act as intracellular alarmones [195]. Degradation is necessary to regulate the abundance of these signaling molecules, and hydrolysis depends on phosphatases of either the DIPP subfamily or the histidine‐triad (HIT) family of diadenosine polyphosphate phosphatases. The DIPP enzymes interact with Ap6A or Ap5A to generate p4A and ADP or p4A and AMP, respectively, as the major products of hydrolysis [170, 172, 173, 197]. Ap6A and Ap5A are allosteric activators of eukaryotic IMP dehydrogenases, which regulate guanine nucleotide levels [198, 199], and they are potent inhibitors of nucleotide and protein kinases as well as other enzymes [172, 195], affecting metabolic and signaling pathways. In mammalian systems, diadenosine polyphosphates are also found in secretory vesicles of neurons and secretory cells, activating P2Y purinergic receptors upon their release, and they play major roles in the vascular system, affecting platelet cells and endothelial cells and acting as vasoconstrictors [200, 201, 202].The phosphorylated sugar 5‐phosphoribosyl‐1‐pyrophosphate (PRPP) is a substrate for the synthesis of purine, pyrimidine and pyridine nucleotides, and in the biosynthesis of histidine and tryptophan in fungi and plants. In vitro, the DIPP enzymes cleave the β phosphate from PRPP to generate the product ribose‐1,5‐bisphosphate (Rib‐1,5‐P2; [174]). In animals, Rib‐1,5‐P2 is an activator of glycolysis by stimulating the enzymatic activity of phosphofructokinase in brain, muscle, liver, and immune cells [203, 204, 205, 206]. The levels of both PRPP and Rib‐1,5‐P2 rapidly and transiently increase ~7‐fold during hypoxia; activity from a PRPP pyrophosphatase was detected but the identity of the protein was not determined [203]. Whether or not PRPP is a bone fide substrate for the DIPP enzymes has been a point of discussion since the initial observation [164, 176, 207, 208]. Enzyme kinetics argue against it: including an alkaline pH optimum, a high K
m for the PRPP substrate, and PRPP as a poor competitor relative to 5‐InsP7 [172, 203, 209]; however, no other enzyme with pyrophosphatase activities outside of the DIPP enzymes have been described to act upon these molecules.Eukaryotic mRNAs are modified at their 5′‐ends by the 7‐methylguanosine (m7G) cap that is important for translation initiation and mRNA stability, and at their 3′‐ends by the poly‐A tail that is critical for stability, nuclear export and translation. Degradation of mRNAs involves either progressive truncation of the poly‐A tail by exonucleases or cleavage of the cap (decapping) at the 5′ end. Decapping by Dcp2, a protein conserved across eukaryotes, is a regulated process linking the 5′‐3′ degradation to multiple decay pathways (reviewed in [210, 211, 212]). The yeast Ddp1 and mammalian DIPP1 proteins were shown to have robust activity in vitro with capped RNAs [213], suggesting a possible in vivo role which has not been deeply investigated.

The DIPP subfamily of Nudix enzymes cleaves multiple substrates

The Nudix superfamily includes the diphosphoinositol polyphosphate phosphohydrolase (DIPP) subfamily. The DIPP subfamily is found across eukaryotes including vertebrates, invertebrates, fungi, and plants but is not found in protists, indicating a later evolution after the divergence of the major domains of life [165, 166]. The DIPP enzymes exhibit substrate ambiguity, meaning they have activity toward substrates that are related to each other but catalysis occurs with differences in efficiency [164]. They have been characterized to hydrolyze several substrates in vitro and some of these have been verified by effects in vivo. Based on kinetic measurements in vitro (Table 3), cleavage of the β phosphate from PP‐InsPs (Fig. 1) is the most physiologically compelling of the investigated substrates. However, in vitro and in vivo data demonstrate strong cases for their activity as endopolyphosphatases on inorganic polyP, which is cleaved more rapidly than 1‐InsP7 [175], and on diadenosine polyphosphates (e.g., Ap6P, Ap5A). Weaker cases are currently made for the hydrolysis of 5‐phosphoribosyl‐1‐pyrophosphate (PRPP) and the 7‐methyguanosine (m7G) cap of mRNAs (Fig. 6; reviewed in [164, 176]). What links each of these substrates is that they have phosphoanhydride bonds, either as the β phosphate in a pyrophosphate group or part of a longer chain of phosphates. Inositol pyrophosphates have been discussed above, and these additional substrates will be briefly described before focusing on recent reports of enzyme structures and substrate recognition.

Kinetic data for ScDdp1 and HsDIPP1.

Structures of the molecules hydrolyzed by DIPP enzymes. These molecules are alternative substrates for the DIPP family of enzymes.

PolyP is a linear molecule with three to hundreds of inorganic phosphates connected by energy‐rich phosphoanhydride linkages that is involved in many important cellular roles (reviewed in [177, 178, 179, 180, 181]). PolyP is found in many subcellular compartments including nuclei, plasma membrane, mitochondria, and the cytosol [182]. It is found in very high levels in the acidocalcisomes of protists and mammalian mast cells, the dense granules of platelets, and the vacuoles of fungi serving as a phosphate reservoir (reviewed in [49, 66, 183, 184, 185]). Mammalian cells have substantially lower levels of polyP (25–120 μm) than are found in yeast (estimates from whole cells are ~230 mm and a recent determination found the vacuolar concentration to be ~30 mm) [66, 182, 186, 187]. Plant cells do not synthesize polyP, but instead store inorganic phosphate in vacuoles [185, 188, 189]. PolyP interacts with proteins as a scaffold and chaperone, promotes condensate formation, and can modify proteins post‐translationally (reviewed in [177, 180]). In fungal cells, polyP plays roles in cell cycle progression, DNA repair and genome stability, energy homoeostasis, oxidative stress responses, and virulence [62, 190, 191, 192]. In mammalian cells, induction of polyP synthesis led to its accumulation in the nucleolus, increased liquid–liquid phase separation and altered rRNA processing [193, 194]. PolyP plays important roles in blood coagulation, the inflammatory response, bone formation, neurotransmission and regulation of ion channels [177, 180, 181].

Diadenosine polyphosphates (Ap
n
A) are generated in response to environmental and genotoxic stresses (reviewed in [195, 196]). These molecules have the 5'C of adenosine connected by three to six phosphates to the 5'C of a second adenosine. Originally thought to be damaged molecules, these molecules have been proposed to act as intracellular alarmones [195]. Degradation is necessary to regulate the abundance of these signaling molecules, and hydrolysis depends on phosphatases of either the DIPP subfamily or the histidine‐triad (HIT) family of diadenosine polyphosphate phosphatases. The DIPP enzymes interact with Ap6A or Ap5A to generate p4A and ADP or p4A and AMP, respectively, as the major products of hydrolysis [170, 172, 173, 197]. Ap6A and Ap5A are allosteric activators of eukaryotic IMP dehydrogenases, which regulate guanine nucleotide levels [198, 199], and they are potent inhibitors of nucleotide and protein kinases as well as other enzymes [172, 195], affecting metabolic and signaling pathways. In mammalian systems, diadenosine polyphosphates are also found in secretory vesicles of neurons and secretory cells, activating P2Y purinergic receptors upon their release, and they play major roles in the vascular system, affecting platelet cells and endothelial cells and acting as vasoconstrictors [200, 201, 202].

The phosphorylated sugar 5‐phosphoribosyl‐1‐pyrophosphate (PRPP) is a substrate for the synthesis of purine, pyrimidine and pyridine nucleotides, and in the biosynthesis of histidine and tryptophan in fungi and plants. In vitro, the DIPP enzymes cleave the β phosphate from PRPP to generate the product ribose‐1,5‐bisphosphate (Rib‐1,5‐P2; [174]). In animals, Rib‐1,5‐P2 is an activator of glycolysis by stimulating the enzymatic activity of phosphofructokinase in brain, muscle, liver, and immune cells [203, 204, 205, 206]. The levels of both PRPP and Rib‐1,5‐P2 rapidly and transiently increase ~7‐fold during hypoxia; activity from a PRPP pyrophosphatase was detected but the identity of the protein was not determined [203]. Whether or not PRPP is a bone fide substrate for the DIPP enzymes has been a point of discussion since the initial observation [164, 176, 207, 208]. Enzyme kinetics argue against it: including an alkaline pH optimum, a high K
m for the PRPP substrate, and PRPP as a poor competitor relative to 5‐InsP7 [172, 203, 209]; however, no other enzyme with pyrophosphatase activities outside of the DIPP enzymes have been described to act upon these molecules.

Eukaryotic mRNAs are modified at their 5′‐ends by the 7‐methylguanosine (m7G) cap that is important for translation initiation and mRNA stability, and at their 3′‐ends by the poly‐A tail that is critical for stability, nuclear export and translation. Degradation of mRNAs involves either progressive truncation of the poly‐A tail by exonucleases or cleavage of the cap (decapping) at the 5′ end. Decapping by Dcp2, a protein conserved across eukaryotes, is a regulated process linking the 5′‐3′ degradation to multiple decay pathways (reviewed in [210, 211, 212]). The yeast Ddp1 and mammalian DIPP1 proteins were shown to have robust activity in vitro with capped RNAs [213], suggesting a possible in vivo role which has not been deeply investigated.

The structural studies provide insights into substrate recognition and catalysisThe structures of several of the DIPP enzymes have been determined by crystallization and X‐ray diffraction: human DIPP1 [214], entries in the Protein Database for human DIPP2 and DIPP3α and mouse DIPP2α, and the human DIPP1 in complex with 1‐ and 5‐InsP7 [17]. In 2021, two reports expanded our structural understanding of these enzymes. One is of the human DIPP1 enzyme in complex with natural PP‐InsPs, alternative PP‐InsP stereoisomers, and nonhydrolysable analogs focusing on pre‐ and postreaction states [215]. The second is a detailed structural study of the yeast ScDdp1 protein in complex with multiple natural substrates and nonhydrolysable substrate analogs for inositides, diadenosine polyphosphates, and polyP to determine the structures important for substrate recognition and catalysis [197].Both the ScDdp1 and HsDIPP1 enzymes have a broad, open V‐shaped cavity with a ridge made by residues having long flexible side chains able to accommodate inositides, nucleotides and polyP (top views in Figs 7B and 8A, and sideview in Fig. 7C) [215]. The yeast Ddp1 has an insertion of 26 amino acids, called the nose (see Fig. 8A) that is not found in the human DIPP enzymes [197]; the position of this insertion is referred to as the X‐loop [215]. Immediately N‐terminal to the Nudix motif is Lys63 in Ddp1 whereas it is Gly20 in DIPP1; although not conserved, both residues are critical for enzyme activity or thermal stability [197, 216]. Importantly, Glu80 and Glu84, which are critical for catalysis [162, 163], each interact with Mg2+ ions that position the β phosphate in the correct geometry for hydrolysis. These substrate‐ and Mg2+‐ contacts are conserved in the structures of the human DIPP1 enzyme when in complex with 1‐ and 5‐InsP7, with only some minor differences due to amino acid divergence.Fig. 7Structure of HsDIPP1 with 5‐InsP7. Figures are from [197], http://creativecommons.org/licenses/by/4.0/.Phosphates from all substrates make the same contacts with the yeast enzyme—Arg32, Ser52, Ser53, Lys63, Gly65, Arg102, Arg129, Arg152, and Arg171 (Fig. 8B)—and these positions anchor the substrates, following a defined path in the base of the cavity (Fig. 8A). The substrate polyP makes four additional contacts (His55, Lys57, Arg58, and Lys170) and Ap5A makes one unique contact (Asp100) [197]. The human DIPP1 protein makes the same contacts to the PP‐InsP substrates with some small differences: HsDIPP1 makes substrate contacts with Arg10, Arg20, and His90, which are not conserved amino acids. The key take‐away messages from these studies are that (1) these enzymes—which diverged about a billion years ago—retained the critical amino acids and overall core structure, and (2) these very different substrates are all recognized by the same conserved amino acids, and structural flexibility explains substrate ambiguity.Zong and colleagues examined the activity of Ddp1 with the canonical PP‐InsP substrates (1‐InsP7, 5‐InsP7, InsP8, and 5PP‐InsP4 [2‐OH]) as well as alternative PP‐InsP stereoisomers (2‐InsP7, 3‐InsP7, 4‐InsP7, and 6‐InsP7) [215]. The Vmax was highest for InsP8, and 1‐InsP7 had a ~ 2‐fold higher activity than 5‐InsP7, in agreement with previous studies [171]. The activity with 5PP‐InsP4 and 6‐InsP7 was the same as 5‐InsP7, and activity with the other stereoisomers also had significant but lower activity. These findings are important because some of these molecules have been detected in nature—4/6‐InsP7 in mouse, Dictyostelium discoideum, Arabidopsis thaliana, and Cryptococcus neoformans [7, 8, 10, 13, 217] and 2‐InsP7 from mouse colon, peripheral blood mononuclear cells, and heart [13]—and the proteins for their metabolism are under investigation.As opposed to the substrates Ap5P and polyP, the PP‐InsPs can bind the active site in different ways due to the symmetry of the phosphorylated myo‐inositol ring. The substrate 1‐InsP7 binds in only one orientation whereas both 5‐InsP7 and InsP8 bind in two ways—productive and nonproductive [197]. Kilari and colleagues [171] investigated the kinetic properties of the human and yeast DIPP enzymes, finding that 1‐InsP7 was hydrolyzed five‐ to sixfold faster than 5‐InsP7 or InsP8, even though the K
m values were about the same (see Table 3). Márguez‐Moñino and colleagues noted that their structural data showing nonproductive binding by 5‐InsP7 and InsP8 could explain the kinetic data. When InsP8 is the substrate, the preferred binding mode will position the C5 β phosphate in the productive mode; cleavage of this pyrophosphate group releases 1‐InsP7 that is rapidly hydrolyzed to InsP6. These data are in agreement with the order of cleavage as determined previously [171, 175].

The structural studies provide insights into substrate recognition and catalysis

The structures of several of the DIPP enzymes have been determined by crystallization and X‐ray diffraction: human DIPP1 [214], entries in the Protein Database for human DIPP2 and DIPP3α and mouse DIPP2α, and the human DIPP1 in complex with 1‐ and 5‐InsP7 [17]. In 2021, two reports expanded our structural understanding of these enzymes. One is of the human DIPP1 enzyme in complex with natural PP‐InsPs, alternative PP‐InsP stereoisomers, and nonhydrolysable analogs focusing on pre‐ and postreaction states [215]. The second is a detailed structural study of the yeast ScDdp1 protein in complex with multiple natural substrates and nonhydrolysable substrate analogs for inositides, diadenosine polyphosphates, and polyP to determine the structures important for substrate recognition and catalysis [197].

Both the ScDdp1 and HsDIPP1 enzymes have a broad, open V‐shaped cavity with a ridge made by residues having long flexible side chains able to accommodate inositides, nucleotides and polyP (top views in Figs 7B and 8A, and sideview in Fig. 7C) [215]. The yeast Ddp1 has an insertion of 26 amino acids, called the nose (see Fig. 8A) that is not found in the human DIPP enzymes [197]; the position of this insertion is referred to as the X‐loop [215]. Immediately N‐terminal to the Nudix motif is Lys63 in Ddp1 whereas it is Gly20 in DIPP1; although not conserved, both residues are critical for enzyme activity or thermal stability [197, 216]. Importantly, Glu80 and Glu84, which are critical for catalysis [162, 163], each interact with Mg2+ ions that position the β phosphate in the correct geometry for hydrolysis. These substrate‐ and Mg2+‐ contacts are conserved in the structures of the human DIPP1 enzyme when in complex with 1‐ and 5‐InsP7, with only some minor differences due to amino acid divergence.

There is an insertion of 26 amino acids in the ScDdp1 protein (the nose) relative to the mammalian DIPP enzymes; the ‘nose’ insertion is shown with InsP7 and polyP, but it was disordered with AP5P. These images are the same or adaptations from those reported in Márquez‐Moñino et al., 2021 [197]. (B) An alignment of the amino acid sequences of the ScDdp1 and human DIPP1 enzymes. Interaction of ScDdp1 with substrates [197] and of HsDIPP1 with InsP7, from Dollins et al., 2020 [17], are depicted below the alignment with colored circles. Figures are from [197], http://creativecommons.org/licenses/by/4.0/.

Phosphates from all substrates make the same contacts with the yeast enzyme—Arg32, Ser52, Ser53, Lys63, Gly65, Arg102, Arg129, Arg152, and Arg171 (Fig. 8B)—and these positions anchor the substrates, following a defined path in the base of the cavity (Fig. 8A). The substrate polyP makes four additional contacts (His55, Lys57, Arg58, and Lys170) and Ap5A makes one unique contact (Asp100) [197]. The human DIPP1 protein makes the same contacts to the PP‐InsP substrates with some small differences: HsDIPP1 makes substrate contacts with Arg10, Arg20, and His90, which are not conserved amino acids. The key take‐away messages from these studies are that (1) these enzymes—which diverged about a billion years ago—retained the critical amino acids and overall core structure, and (2) these very different substrates are all recognized by the same conserved amino acids, and structural flexibility explains substrate ambiguity.

Zong and colleagues examined the activity of Ddp1 with the canonical PP‐InsP substrates (1‐InsP7, 5‐InsP7, InsP8, and 5PP‐InsP4 [2‐OH]) as well as alternative PP‐InsP stereoisomers (2‐InsP7, 3‐InsP7, 4‐InsP7, and 6‐InsP7) [215]. The Vmax was highest for InsP8, and 1‐InsP7 had a ~ 2‐fold higher activity than 5‐InsP7, in agreement with previous studies [171]. The activity with 5PP‐InsP4 and 6‐InsP7 was the same as 5‐InsP7, and activity with the other stereoisomers also had significant but lower activity. These findings are important because some of these molecules have been detected in nature—4/6‐InsP7 in mouse, Dictyostelium discoideum, Arabidopsis thaliana, and Cryptococcus neoformans [7, 8, 10, 13, 217] and 2‐InsP7 from mouse colon, peripheral blood mononuclear cells, and heart [13]—and the proteins for their metabolism are under investigation.

As opposed to the substrates Ap5P and polyP, the PP‐InsPs can bind the active site in different ways due to the symmetry of the phosphorylated myo‐inositol ring. The substrate 1‐InsP7 binds in only one orientation whereas both 5‐InsP7 and InsP8 bind in two ways—productive and nonproductive [197]. Kilari and colleagues [171] investigated the kinetic properties of the human and yeast DIPP enzymes, finding that 1‐InsP7 was hydrolyzed five‐ to sixfold faster than 5‐InsP7 or InsP8, even though the K
m values were about the same (see Table 3). Márguez‐Moñino and colleagues noted that their structural data showing nonproductive binding by 5‐InsP7 and InsP8 could explain the kinetic data. When InsP8 is the substrate, the preferred binding mode will position the C5 β phosphate in the productive mode; cleavage of this pyrophosphate group releases 1‐InsP7 that is rapidly hydrolyzed to InsP6. These data are in agreement with the order of cleavage as determined previously [171, 175].

Mutations in the genes encoding the DIPP enzymes lead to phenotypes associated with the different substratesThis section will review what is known about mutant phenotypes associated with the mutations in the gene(s) for the DIPP enzymes from representative taxa and will consider each of the substrates when information is known.FungiFungi have one member of the DIPP subfamily in their genomes [166, 218]; DDP1 in S. cerevisiae and aps1
+ in S. pombe have been studied in detail. The ScDdp1 and SpAps1 enzymes have multiple substrates, but the best substrates for each are the PP‐InsPs. These enzymes hydrolyze the β phosphate of 5‐InsP7 to InsP6 and both β phosphates of InsP8 to InsP6 [172, 175]. SpAps1 has similar kinetics to ScDdp1 (Table 3), with a ~3‐fold faster k
cat and a twofold preference for 1‐InsP7 [43, 172]. There was a ~7‐fold increase in InsP7 abundance in the ddp1Δ strain, although the isoforms were not distinguished [35]. In S. pombe, there was a ~3‐fold increase in the InsP7 pools in the aps1Δ mutant [219], and the more recent study found that the aps1Δ strain accumulates about twice the level of 1‐InsP7 as the wild‐type strain [43]. The aps1Δ is synthetically lethal with nonfunctional alleles of asp1
+ (the bifunctional kinase‐histidine phosphate discussed above) due to an inability to metabolize the PP‐InsPs, leading to a toxic build‐up of 1‐InsP7 and InsP8 [43, 72, 77]. Together, these data indicate that ScDdp1 and SpAps1 play important cellular roles in determining the steady state levels of inositol pyrophosphates.The diadenosine polyphosphates have been shown to be in vitro substrates for the yeast enzymes. Ap6A is an better substrate than Ap5A for ScDdp1 and SpAps1 [173, 220]. The K
ms for substrate Ap5A with the SpAps1 and ScDdp1 were reported to be 22 μm and 70 μM, respectively [173, 220]; both groups used the same method to assay enzyme activity. However, in a follow‐up study from the Barnes group, they used a different method to quantify the product formed by SpAps1, and reported a ~7‐fold lower K
m to be 2.84 μm [218]; it is possible that the kinetic parameters for the ScDdp1 enzyme would be equally affected by the more sensitive assay. However, even with this change, the K
m for Ap5A remains at least 100‐fold higher than for the PP‐InsPs substrates. In S. pombe, the intracellular concentration of Ap5A is present at 0.06 pmol/mg of protein, approx. 4 nm, which is well below the K
m [219]. Although the deletion of Spaps1
+ decreased hydrolase activity, it did not affect the levels of Ap5A [219]. In S. cerevisiae during late sporulation, p4A levels increase [221]; as noted above, this molecule is the product of the reaction of Ap5‐6A, but a role for ScDdp1 in sporulation or germination has not been described. Although there is in vitro activity with Ap6A and Ap5A, these would be relevant substrates in vivo only if their intracellular levels increase substantially.Polyphosphates have been shown to be important intracellular substrates for the yeast DIPP enzymes. The purified recombinant ScDdp1 enzyme cleaved polyP as an endopolyphosphatase [175, 222]. Deletion of Scddp1 led to increased polyP accumulation whereas the overexpression of DDP1 in S. cerevisiae led to a decrease in acid‐soluble (short‐chain) and acid‐insoluble (long‐chain) polyP by 9% and 28%, respectively [65, 223]. The combined activities of ScDdp1 and ScPpx1, the cytosolic exopolyphosphatase, are both required to overcome the toxic effects of polyP accumulation in the cytosol when bacterial polyphosphate kinase is overexpressed in S. cerevisiae [224]. In S. pombe, the aps1Δ mutant has a constitutive phosphate starvation response [225, 226] and polyP accumulation was higher in the aps1Δ mutant [73]. These observations are consistent with the ScDdp1 and SpAps1 enzymes playing key roles in the turnover of polyP.Work on the other possible substrates has not been as thorough. There are no reports on PRPP as a substrate nor of the effect of mutations on flux through glycolysis. In vitro decapping activity was shown for ScDdp1 and mouse DIPP1 (see below; [213]), and the mutation of the mammalian DIPP1 impacted mRNA levels of specific messages. Overexpression of Spaps1
+ decreased cell growth and led to swollen, rounded, and multiseptated cells but paradoxically increased the levels of Ap5A [219]; this phenotype was attributed to effects on cell wall biosynthesis.Plant–fungal pathogens secrete a variety of molecules (effectors) that impact the host's immune responses to enhance their virulence [227, 228, 229]. Magnaporthe and Colletotrichum fungi are responsible for plant diseases causing blast on grain crops (e.g., rice, wheat, barley) and anthracnose on the leaves and shoots of plants of agricultural and horticultural importance (e.g., tea, peas, lentils, trees), respectively [230, 231]. An important effector from plant–fungal pathogens is a member of the DIPP family [232] and its mechanism of action was recently described [233]. Expression of the Colletotrichum CtNUDIX gene increased in the late biotrophic phase in planta [232], prior to the switch to the necrotic phase. Deletion of the NUDIX gene (or mutating the catalytic residues) decreased fungal pathogenesis, and its overexpression killed host cells during the biotrophic phase of infection but did not lead to pathogenesis [232, 233]. The Magnaporthe MoNUDIX protein has in vitro enzymatic activity with 5‐InsP7 but not with other substrates [233]. The effectors from Magnaporthe and Colletotrichum had robust in vitro hydrolytic activity toward 4‐InsP7, 5‐InsP7, and 6‐InsP7, and minor activity with 1‐InsP7 [234]. The MoNUDIX protein localized to the fungal biotrophic interfacial complex (BIC), the secretion structure for transport into plant cells, and mRFP‐tagged MoNUDIX was found in rice leaf cells [233]. The NUDIX effector was predicted to affect the phosphate starvation response by decreasing InsP8. When inorganic phosphate is abundant, InsP8 binds to the SPX regulatory protein to inhibit the transcriptional factor PHR1 to induce gene expression of target genes encoding high affinity Pi transporters, ubiquitin ligases, and hormone receptors (reviewed in [235]). The MoNUDIX effector stimulated the phosphate starvation response in the plants [233]. The plant's immune responses and signaling were also disrupted, although the full mechanism(s) for this are incompletely understood [231, 236].InvertebratesArthropod genomes carry one gene encoding the DIPP enzyme (319 arthropod species, NCBI search on 7/12/2025; [78]), whereas animal and plant genomes encode many more (see below), suggesting that functional specification evolved later. In Drosophila melanogaster, the Aps gene shows alternative transcriptional start and stop sites and differential splicing to generate three different mRNAs; however, each of these mRNAs produces the same protein [237]. The gene was cloned, recombinant protein was expressed and characterized [238]. The Aps enzyme prefers PP‐InsP5 with a K
m of 70 nm and a k
cat of 0.024·s−1 and it also hydrolyzed several different (di)nucleoside polyphosphates, with guanosine 5′‐tetraphosphate as the most efficient (K
m, 11 μm; kcat, 0.79·s−1). Aps expression was found in cells throughout the fly, and both the levels of PP‐InsP5 and gene expression were highest in embryos and declined throughout development. As a part of a larger study examining the mammalian Nudt3 protein and its effects on obesity (see below), expression from the Drosophila Aps gene was knocked down in the nervous system and specifically in a subset of neurosecretory cells that produce insulin [239]. The mutant flies had an inability to recruit lipids and carbohydrates during starvation, and exhibited a hyperinsulinemia‐like phenotype. These results indicate there is a role for the DmAps in the regulation of insulin signaling; however, the cellular mechanisms and critical substrate(s) for this regulation remain uncharacterized.MammalsHumans have four genes in the DIPP subfamily of Nudix hydrolases producing five proteins [170]. Gene NUDT3 (6p21) encodes the 172 amino acid protein DIPP1 [240]. The NUDT4 gene (12q22) generates two mRNAs through alternative splicing, encoding the 180 amino acid protein DIPP2α and the 181 amino acid protein DIPP2β with an insertion of Gln86 [241]. Genes NUDT10 and NUDT11 are adjacent, convergently transcribed genes that arose by gene duplication on the X chromosome (Xp11), and they encode proteins DIPP3α and DIPP3β, respectively, that differ by one amino acid [242, 243]. They have evolved to have different substrate preferences and expression patterns.DIPP1 is cytosolic and has a K
m for PP‐InsPs in the nanomolar range (Table 3), indicating that these molecules are the preferred substrates; the K
ms for Ap6A and Ap5A are ~6–8 μm, and they could be substrates if cytosolic concentrations increase. DIPP1 has been implicated in the decapping of mRNAs in vitro and in vivo [213, 244], indicating that mRNA caps are important substrates (more below). DIPP1 enzyme activity with particular substrates depends upon the cations bound: PP‐InsPs are the preferred substrates with Mg2+ [171], Ap5‐6A and mRNA caps require Mn2+ [172, 244, 245], and polyP is preferred with Zn2+ [246]. Mg2+ is at ~1 mm but other cations are sequestered at low levels. The in vivo implications are that the availability of the cations might vary or be regulated, and this could then influence substrate selection. In the case of Zn2+, oxidative stress induced with hydrogen peroxide or menadione leads to an increase in free Zn2+ and a decrease in cellular polyP [246]. Secondly, changes to substrate availability affect the competition for the enzyme in vivo, as was shown for a competition between PP‐InsPs and m7G caps [245]. In vitro assays showed that 5‐InsP7 inhibited decapping (IC50 of ~200 nm); in vivo, increasing the levels of 5‐InsP7 by 2–3 fold (through genetic manipulation by deleting the HsPPIP5K, see above) led to increases in levels of specific mRNAs and P‐body abundance [245]. Thus, changes to the steady state levels of cations, PP‐InsP substrates, and competing substrates will have broad effects.
NUDT3 has been linked to cancer, diabetes and obesity, and oxidative stress responses in humans and animals. Expression of the NUDT3 gene is found broadly in many tissues [170, 240]. There is higher expression in the mammalian brain, particularly in the hypothalamus, amygdala and striatum that are important for regulating food intake and behavior [239, 246]. The mouse Nudt3 gene is up‐regulated in the brain and liver during food deprivation [239]. Multiple genome‐wide association studies for obesity in humans have identified single‐nucleotide polymorphisms (SNPs) linked to NUDT3 [247, 248, 249, 250]. The porcine NUDT3 gene is also linked to lipid accumulation, adipocyte proliferation and pig fatness trait [251]. An siRNA knockdown of NUDT3 in cell lines decreased viability when oxidative stress or DNA damage were imposed [191, 246]. NUDT3 shows increased expression in tumors of the lungs, colon, uterus, and gall bladder and lower expression in a cancer of skeletal muscle [170, 252]. Silencing of NUDT3 in MCF‐7 cells led to the alterations in mRNA levels of 161 genes (144 are up, 17 down) of twofold or more, with increased levels of specific genes associated with cell motility, likely through decreased decapping [244]. Knocking out the two NUDT3 genes from zebrafish was lethal [246]. Determining what substrate(s) and mechanisms are important for these DIPP enzymes in human and animal health are important issues.Human NUDT4 is an ortholog of NUDT3. The ORFs from both genes are in five exons; the splice junctions are conserved, and they produce proteins of 173 and 167 amino acids (respectively) that are 74% identical—the majority of the protein differences are in the C‐terminal 31 or 25 amino acids. NUDT4 is highly expressed across a wide range of tissues; interestingly, it was downregulated in all cancers examined [170]. NUDT4 has two transcription start sites (TSS), alternative splicing, and multiple polyadenylation sites to generate at least thirteen transcripts. The second downstream TSS generates mRNAs with a different exon 1, and translation initiation occurs at an internal methionine within the Nudix motif (Fig. 4B, Met53 in the HsDIPP2‐5LTU structure). Assuming the proteins are stable, the predicted proteins of 128 and 129 amino acids would lack critical residues for substrate binding. Alternative splicing adds one amino acid in DIPP2β, Gln86, which is positioned at the end of β5 (Fig. 8B), and this insertion negatively impacts enzyme activity. PP‐InsPs are the preferred substrates: the kinetics of DIPP2α for 5‐InsP7 (K
m of 4.2 nm, k
cat of 0.15·s−1) is nearly the same as for DIPP1 (Table 3), but the DIPP2β is not as efficient (K
m of 4.8 nm, k
cat of 0.03·s−1) [241]. Neither 2α nor 2β is as active with InsP8 as DIPP1 by 3.5‐ to 10‐fold (the first‐order rate constant k

−1
 was determined as 320·s−1 for DIPP1, 90·s−1 for 2α, and 32·s−1 for 2β [241]). Considering polyP as a substrate, the GST‐DIPP2 fusion protein had ~25% of the activity as compared with GST‐DIPP1 [246]. The 2α and 2β enzymes have little activity with Ap6A (34 U and 18 U, respectively, vs. 310 U for DIPP1; 1 U = 1 pmol·min−1·μg−1) or Ap5A < 10 U and 26 U, respectively, ~1/10th the activity of DIPP1 (320 U) [241]. Neither protein cleaved the m7G‐cap [213]. Thus, the paradox is that although all of the critical contact positions for substrates and magnesium are conserved in DIPP1 and DIPP2α (Fig. 8B), the DIPP1 and 2α/2β enzymes only have PP‐InsPs as a common substrate. There are critical differences between the proteins that are yet to be uncovered. These observations are consistent with the idea that critical functions have been retained in NUDT3 following gene duplication, permitting silencing or subfunctionalization to occur on NUDT4 [253, 254].The human genes NUDT10 and NUDT11 produce DIPP3α (aka, hAps2) and DIPP3β (hAps1), respectively. They are found on the X chromosome, subjected to X‐inactivation, convergently transcribed, and likely the result of a gene duplication [242, 243]. Their genetic structure of NUDT10 and NUDT11 is different from NUDT3 and NUDT4 in that there are only two exons, with the first exon encoding the entire uninterrupted ORF; this observation suggests that one of these genes is a retrogene that was then duplicated. The mouse genes Nudt10 and Nudt11 encode identical proteins; however, the two human genes differ at one codon: 3α carries Pro89, a substitution of the critical Arg89 that is found in 3β (equivalent to Arg129 in Fig. 8B). The proteins from these genes are nearly identical to the DIPP2β isoform, including the distinctive Gln86 insertion [242, 243]. Given that these genes encode proteins nearly identical to 2β, their substrate preferences and kinetics are the similar: (1) 3β was ~2.3‐fold more active than 3α, likely due to Pro89; (2) hydrolysis of 5‐InsP7 is preferred over Ap5‐6A by 100‐200‐fold; and (3) 3β has similar activity as 2β (only 1.4‐fold lower activity with 5‐InsP7) [242].In mouse, Nudt10 expression is highest in liver, kidney, and testis and Nudt11 is expressed in the brain [209]. In humans, expression of NUDT10 and NUDT11 is highest in testis [242]. Several SNPs associated with prostate cancer are linked to Xp11.22 where these genes are found [255, 256]. Expression of NUDT10 and NUDT11 was significantly reduced in two testicular cancers (testicular germ cell tumors, mesothelioma), but interestingly, silencing of expression is lethal in three other cancer cell lines: (A549 lung, MCF7 breast, and SW480 colon cancer) [170]. Thus, although there is more limited substrate selection as compared with DIPP1, narrowing which cells express the gene may be a type of neofunctionalization.Bryophytes and vascular plantsPlants generate a broader set of PP‐InsPs as compared with yeast and mammals: in addition to the canonical 1‐InsP7 and 5‐InsP7, they produce 4/6‐InsP7 and possibly 3‐InsP7 [7, 10, 29], and in some reports 4/6‐InsP7 is the major isoform that is more abundant than the ‘canonical’ isoforms [7, 10]. As noted earlier, plants do not synthesize polyP and instead store inorganic phosphate in vacuoles [185, 188, 189], and they do not generate Ap5‐6A [257, 258]. Thus, in contrast to fungi and animals, these molecules would not be expected to be substrates in vivo.Two recent reports [29, 259] examined these enzymes in the liverwort M. polymorpha. Liverworts carry one DIPP gene in their genome (Mp5g06600), named DDP1 and NUDT1 by the two groups [29, 259]. Expression of the wild‐type MpDDP1 gene (but not a mutated Mpddp1) in yeast fully complemented the Scddp1Δ mutant for hydrogen peroxide sensitivity and reduced the elevated InsP7 levels to wild‐type [259]. In vitro assays using purified recombinant MpNUDT1 uncovered robust activity with 1‐InsP7 as the substrate (757 nmol min−1 mg−1), minor activity with 5‐InsP7 (12 nmol·min−1·mg−1), and no activity with InsP8 [29]. Both groups knocked out the MpDDP1/MpNUDT1 gene using CRISPR/Cas9 and found similar effects on PP‐InsP pools in vivo: the mutant plants accumulated 1‐InsP7 and InsP8, but there was no effect on the levels of 5‐InsP7 or 4/6‐InsP7 [29, 259]. The effects on the 1‐InsP7 pools are consistent with the in vitro assays; however, the increase in the InsP8 pools in vivo suggests that this molecule could also be a substrate in vitro. These data provide in vivo and in vitro evidence that the MpDDP1/MpNUDT1 gene encodes a bone fide DIPP hydrolase with specificity to a subset of PP‐InsPs.The Mpnudt1 mutant plants, with higher than wild‐type levels of InsP8, have significantly lower levels of intracellular inorganic phosphate, consistent with the regulation of phosphate homeostasis by InsP8 [29]. Interestingly, the MpNUDT1 gene itself is regulated by phosphate, repressed during phosphate limitation, and genes for the other hydrolases show constitutive expression [29]. One group reported that the Mpddp1/Mpnudt1 null plants had an enlarged thallus, early bifurcation of the thallus lobe, early emergence of the gemmae cup, and greater numbers of mature gemmae than the wild‐type plant [259]; however, the second group reported reduced thallus area and no change to the number of gemmae per plant [29]. The Mpnudt

ge
 plants accumulated more nitrate and less phosphate than control, indicating a disruption in the homeostasis mechanisms [29].The Arabidopsis genome carries seven genes predicted to be in the DIPP subfamily [168, 169]. The predicted DIPP subfamily proteins are AtNUDT4 (locus At1g18300), AtNUDT12 (At1g12880), AtNUDT13 (At3g26690), AtNUDT16 (At3g12600), AtNUDT17 (At2g01670), AtNUDT18 (At1g14860), and AtNUDT21 (At1g73540). AtNUDT16 produces two different transcripts that are translated differently at the N‐termini: AtNUDT16.1 and AtNUDT16.2 (Note: the NUDT numbering in plants is not the same as in mammals; that is, they are not the closest ortholog). Phylogenetic analysis places these genes in a clade with ScDDP1, HsNUDT4, and MpDDP1 [29, 234, 259], with evidence for two subclades (see below) with neofunctionalization [234]. AtNUDT4 is expressed in roots, stems and leaves [260], and the AtNUDT12/13/16/17/21 genes are widely expressed in rosette leaves, stems, cauline leaves, inflorescence and roots, and AtNUDT18 showing no expression in rosette leaves and strongest expression in inflorescence [261].These putative DIPP enzymes were examined for activity on PP‐InsPs and other substrates. The Gillaspy group focused on AtNUDT13 because it has the strongest sequence homology with ScDdp1 in BLAST analyses [262]. Using purified GST‐AtNUDT13, they found in vitro hydrolysis with InsP7, InsP8, polyP, and Ap5A as substrates; these results differed from a prior study that reported activity with Ap6A and Ap5A, and no activity with 5‐InsP7 or PRPP [263]. An alternative approach was used by the Hothorn and Schaaf groups to identify DIPP enzymes able to bind to 5PCP‐InsP5, a nonhydrolysable analog of 5‐InsP7 [264], in pull‐down assays, followed by mass spectrometry to identify the proteins [29, 234]. The Hothorn group identified AtNUDT17, AtNUDT18, and AtNUDT21 proteins from 2‐week‐old seedlings [29]. Recombinant AtNUDT17 was purified and in vitro assays were performed with PP‐InsPs; the highest activity occurred with 5‐InsP7 (42 nmol·min−1·mg−1), minimal activity with 1‐InsP7 (2 nmol·min−1·mg−1), and no activity with InsP8 [29]. The Schaaf group found AtNUDT4, AtNUDT17, and AtNUDT21 proteins in shoot extracts, and AtNUDT16 and AtNUDT18 in root extracts [234]. They examined all seven of the potential DIPP enzymes by complementing the S. cerevisiae DDP1 mutant and examining InsP7 levels (which are higher in the Scddp1Δ mutant); each of the AtNUDT genes, except AtNUDT4 and AtNUDT18, significantly reduced the levels of InsP7, and AtNUDT16.1 was the strongest, reducing InsP7 to the level of the wild‐type ScDDP1 [234]. Using in vitro assays and purified recombinant MBP‐tagged proteins, the ability to hydrolyze a suite of chemically synthesized enantiomers of InsP7 with the diphosphate at each carbon of the ring was assessed [234]. Subclade I (AtNUDT4, AtNUDT17, AtNUDT18, and AtNUDT21) hydrolyzed 4‐InsP7 and AtNUDT18 also cleaved 6‐InsP7; none of the enzymes in subclade I showed significant activity with 5‐InsP7. This result—the lack of 5‐InsP7 cleavage by AtNUDT17—differs from [29] in which they found strong cleavage. Subclade II (AtNUDT12, AtNUDT13, AtNUDT16.1, and AtNUDT16.2) enzymes had robust hydrolase activity with 3‐InsP7 and against the 3β phosphate in the less common InsP8 isoform 3,5‐InsP8 (aka, 3,5PP2‐InsP4). These in vitro results broaden the understanding of which isoforms of InsP7 are substrates for these enzymes and suggest that divergence in the DIPP family has occurred since the split between land plants from fungi and animals [265].To examine in vivo effects, transgenic Arabidopsis plants were generated that overexpressed (OE) either the AtNUDT13 or ScDDP1 genes [262]. These OE lines had decreases in the InsP7 and InsP8 pools relative to wild‐type controls, and conversely, in a plant line with a T‐DNA insertion into AtNUDT13 (mutant allele nudt13‐1), InsP8 and InsP7 accumulated at 1.28× and 2.07× wild‐type levels, respectively [262]. In the second study, Arabidopsis plants were generated lacking three DIPP genes in a triple Atnudt17/18/21 null mutant and overexpressing the AtNUDT17 genes under control of a strong promoter [29]. The triple null mutant had elevated levels of InsP6, 1‐InsP7 and InsP8., and the OE‐AtNUDT17 plants had nearly undetectable levels of InsP8 [29]. In the third study, mutant plant lines were generated lacking subclade I (the Atnudt4/17/18/21 quadruple mutant) or lacking subclade II (the Atnudt12/13/16 triple mutant) [234]. The two subclade II triple mutant lines accumulated 1/3‐InsP7 and 5‐InsP7 whereas none of the three subclade I quadruple mutants were different than control plants [234]. Transient high expression of each of these genes, except AtNUDT13, in tobacco leaves reduced 4/6‐InsP7 and 5‐InsP7 levels [234]. Although there are some differences, these reports showed that manipulating these genes in planta affected levels of PP‐InsPs: loss‐of‐function mutations increased levels whereas the OE lines led to a decrease in their levels.The mutations that depleted the InsP8 molecule activated the phosphate starvation response (PSR) and induced the plants to accumulate inorganic phosphate [29, 234, 262]. The subclade II triple mutant (Atnudt12/13/16) lines also accumulated less iron in shoots relative to wild‐type plants [234]. Interestingly, the AtNUDT13, AtNUDT16, AtNUDT17, AtNUDT18, and AtNUDT21 genes are up‐regulated in response to phosphate limitation in shoots [29, 234]. The overexpression of AtNUDT17 led to reduced rosette areas and other changes to plant growth [29, 262]. The subclade II triple mutant affected expression of genes outside of the PSR, including genes for defenses, responses to reactive oxygen and oxidative stress, wounding, et al., implicating 3‐InsP7 in these roles [234].

Mutations in the genes encoding the DIPP enzymes lead to phenotypes associated with the different substrates

This section will review what is known about mutant phenotypes associated with the mutations in the gene(s) for the DIPP enzymes from representative taxa and will consider each of the substrates when information is known.

FungiFungi have one member of the DIPP subfamily in their genomes [166, 218]; DDP1 in S. cerevisiae and aps1
+ in S. pombe have been studied in detail. The ScDdp1 and SpAps1 enzymes have multiple substrates, but the best substrates for each are the PP‐InsPs. These enzymes hydrolyze the β phosphate of 5‐InsP7 to InsP6 and both β phosphates of InsP8 to InsP6 [172, 175]. SpAps1 has similar kinetics to ScDdp1 (Table 3), with a ~3‐fold faster k
cat and a twofold preference for 1‐InsP7 [43, 172]. There was a ~7‐fold increase in InsP7 abundance in the ddp1Δ strain, although the isoforms were not distinguished [35]. In S. pombe, there was a ~3‐fold increase in the InsP7 pools in the aps1Δ mutant [219], and the more recent study found that the aps1Δ strain accumulates about twice the level of 1‐InsP7 as the wild‐type strain [43]. The aps1Δ is synthetically lethal with nonfunctional alleles of asp1
+ (the bifunctional kinase‐histidine phosphate discussed above) due to an inability to metabolize the PP‐InsPs, leading to a toxic build‐up of 1‐InsP7 and InsP8 [43, 72, 77]. Together, these data indicate that ScDdp1 and SpAps1 play important cellular roles in determining the steady state levels of inositol pyrophosphates.The diadenosine polyphosphates have been shown to be in vitro substrates for the yeast enzymes. Ap6A is an better substrate than Ap5A for ScDdp1 and SpAps1 [173, 220]. The K
